# TensorQuill v3 — Product and Build Plan

Nov 13, 2026 · @Ak B

TensorQuill lets ML engineers and their coding agents see inside a PyTorch training run, so a failing run is caught and explained before it wastes days of GPU time. It was called TensorLens until Nov 14, 2026, when the name TensorQuill was chosen (see Decisions); the dated review reports in this folder still use the old name. This version replaces the larger v2 plan.

## Product

TensorQuill owns the capture SDK, detectors, the evidence they produce, and (decided Nov 14, 2026; postponed Nov 16, 2026, Decisions: "Rune viewer postponed") its own Rust viewer app built on Rune. Until that app covers the needed views, stock Rerun displays the evidence, and the team's coding agent uses it to investigate incidents.

- **Who it is for:** teams training mixture-of-experts and other large models on several GPUs, where the loss curve looks healthy while the run fails. They keep MLflow or W&B for scalars. (Brought in line with the focused plan on Nov 17, 2026. The Nov 13 text started with RL and robotics teams; those packs are frozen until a user brings a workload.)
- **The pitch:** add one line to your PyTorch script and see inside a failing run (activations, attention, MoE routing), with evidence an agent can read.
- **Against trackers:** MLflow and W&B show scalar curves, and their MCP servers let coding agents query those runs. This shows what happens inside the layers.
- **Against CoreWeave ARIA:** ARIA is an agent inside W&B that analyses experiments, proposes next runs and launches them with approval (public preview June 2026). It reasons over what W&B logged, mostly scalars, and is tied to W&B and CoreWeave. This captures layer internals and works with whichever tracker, agent and cloud the team already has.
- **Against Trainy Pluto:** Pluto is a Neptune replacement built for responsiveness with many runs, and its experimental MCP server lets an agent read per-layer panels in one call. It displays what the user logs; no capture SDK or failure detectors turned up. This captures the internals cheaply under `torch.compile` and FSDP and detects known failures.
- **Against debugging agents and per-layer debuggers:** free add-ons and research agents such as RunSleuth read logs, loss curves and gradient norms. Per-layer debuggers now exist: NeuralDBG, MoEWatch and LLM-Training-Debugger (checked Nov 16, 2026), and SCOUT names a faulty rank with no per-layer evidence. None of the three debuggers detects multi-GPU failures or publishes a measured cost under `torch.compile` (focused plan, "What sets it apart"). This gives the team's own agent the evidence and the numbers behind it.

**The gap.** A search on Nov 13, 2026 found no product that captures layer internals (activations, attention, MoE routing, cross-GPU drift) and hands an agent the evidence to explain a failure. That no longer holds as written. The check of Nov 16, 2026 found three per-layer debuggers, and the papers review of Nov 17, 2026 added SCOUT and SageMaker Debugger, so "first" is not the claim. OpGuard (OSDI '26, no code link found) instruments compiled code and keeps the execution mode, `torch.compile` included, so the compile claim is a measured overhead, never that no other tool works under compilation. What none of them states is these together: multi-GPU failures with layer-level evidence, a measured cost under `torch.compile`, and evidence that any agent can read. The first two become public claims only after results 1 and 3 of the focused plan. Public requests for such a tool were not found, so demand is unproven; the closest signal is that CoreWeave and Trainy are both building agent access to training runs.

**Why now.** OpenAI bought Neptune, whose product logged thousands of per-layer metrics to debug unstable runs, and the hosted service shut down in March 2026. Teams that relied on it said the hard requirement was a tool that stays responsive with thousands of runs and heavy logging.

## How it works

Capture signals inside the training script, write them to an append-only local file on each rank first, and optionally stream them from that file's byte offset over Arrow Flight to a self-hosted Rust ingest server that stores partitioned Parquet. Detectors run while the run is in progress: in Rust inside the server over each stored part (the live path, since Nov 15, 2026), and in Python on the local files or the server's Parquet files (the reference, and the offline path). They export JSON evidence for coding agents and an `.rrd` recording and blueprint for engineers to explore in Rerun.

Scalars keep going to the tracker the team already uses. Tensor summaries take their own path, so a slow tensor pipeline or network stall never blocks metric logging or drops training steps.

## Build and borrow

Start with stock Rerun and reuse the MoE recording and blueprint from the bake-off. Capture those signals from a real training run, stream them to the self-hosted ingest server for multi-node storage and live detection, and generate the detector evidence.

| Piece | Build or borrow | Implementation | Note |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Capture SDK | Build | Python (`sdk/src/tensorquill/`) | One line to add (`tensorquill.init(model)`). Tags the tensor stream with the MLflow or W&B run ID and writes `tensorquill-out/<run_id>.rank<rank>.jsonl` through a bounded queue (`WRITER_QUEUE = 8`) |
| Opt-in Flight sender | Build | Python (`sdk/src/tensorquill/flight.py`) | Activated by `endpoint=` or `TENSORQUILL_ENDPOINT`. Tails the local `.jsonl` file from `<run_id>.rank<rank>.offset` and streams `RecordBatch`es over Arrow Flight `DoPut` |
| Ingest server | Build | Rust (`server/`) | Single self-hosted binary (`tensorquill-server`). Receives Arrow Flight `DoPut`, deduplicates by `(run_id, rank, attempt, step)`, flushes partitioned Parquet to S3, GCS, or local disk via `object_store`, runs the detectors over each stored part and watches for ranks that stop |
| Detectors | Build | Python (`sdk/src/tensorquill/evidence.py`), the reference; Rust in the server (`server/src/detect.rs`, `watchdog.rs`), the live path | Thirty-two incident kinds across MoE routing, weights, gradients, activations, attention, step numbers, resumes, data progress, checkpoints, loss scaling, non-finite values, GPU memory and health, input starvation, stalled jobs and cross-GPU drift. Python runs on local `.jsonl` or server Parquet; twenty-eight kinds also run in the server, which `python -m tensorquill --watch` reads when its report is current. Ported on Nov 15, 2026, after the 64-rank benchmark put Python over the 250 ms budget |
| Evidence and Rerun export | Build | Viewer-neutral JSON (`schema_version: 1`), `.rrd`, Rerun blueprint | Exports the incident summary, status heatmap, expert-load table, probe tab, and training-step timeline (`python -m tensorquill`) |
| Viewer app | Build | Rust on Rune (wgpu, headless PNG and element-tree inspection) | TensorQuill's own desktop and web app (decided Nov 14, 2026). Reads `evidence.json` and the JSONL/Parquet step data directly, not `.rrd`. Must match Rerun's step timeline, layer × expert heatmap, histograms and attention maps before it replaces Rerun. Not started; postponed Nov 16, 2026 (Decisions: "Rune viewer postponed") |
| Viewer and screenshots, interim | Borrow, temporary | Rerun 0.43.2 (MIT / Apache 2.0) | Stopgap until the Rune app covers the views above: stock desktop viewer, built-in views, timeline, headless screenshots, and `rerun viewer-mcp`. Keep `export_rerun` working until then |

Postponed from v2 and v1: a built-in VLM agent loop, agent-written layout blueprints, live fixes to a running optimizer, a custom browser client, Tier 3 activation replay, and the MLflow scalar-only run importer (revisit the importer only if three or more engineers join the project).

## Capture SDK

Inside the compiled graph, hooks compute only cheap statistics of fixed size. Copies to the CPU, JSONL writing, Arrow Flight encoding, and probe passes happen outside the compiled step.

**Setup:** `python -m pip install -e 'sdk[rerun]'` and `tensorquill.init(model)`, with the team's MLflow or W&B configuration left unchanged. `init` tags the tensor stream with the tracker's run ID, and the SDK records its own global step with every batch.

- **Run ID.** Takes an explicit `run_id=` first, then `MLFLOW_RUN_ID`, `mlflow.active_run()`, or `wandb.run.id`. Without any of those, it uses the launcher's job ID (`TORCHELASTIC_RUN_ID` unless `"none"`, or `SLURM_JOB_ID` and `SLURM_STEP_ID`), and otherwise generates a unique `run-<UTC YYYYMMDD-HHMMSS>-<hex6>` ID, broadcast from rank 0 when `torch.distributed` is initialized, so separate runs never append to the same file and every rank shares one name (Nov 14, 2026; since Nov 19, 2026 through the process group's store instead of a broadcast, lane T1). Because `mlflow.active_run()` is thread-local and `None` when a run was created through `MlflowClient` (as PyTorch Lightning's `MLFlowLogger` does on rank 0 via `@rank_zero_experiment`), the Lightning callback (`tensorquill.lightning.TensorQuillCallback`, Nov 14, 2026) reads the logger's run ID on rank 0 and broadcasts it with `trainer.strategy.broadcast`.
- **Step.** An MLflow run has no current step; the step is passed with each `log_metric` call. By default (`auto_step=True`), TensorQuill counts training iterations through `register_optimizer_step_post_hook` (imported from `torch.optim.optimizer`), or takes `step=` from `session.step(step=...)` / `trainer.global_step` when `auto_step=False`. With multiple optimizers on one model (such as Muon plus AdamW), the first optimizer to step becomes the lead optimizer and commits the iteration's step; other optimizers in the same iteration are deduplicated by parameter gradient `(id(p.grad), p.grad._version)` signatures (`sdk/src/tensorquill/capture.py`). When `torch.amp.GradScaler` skips `optimizer.step()` on gradient overflow, neither optimizer hook fires; pass `scaler=` (built Nov 14, 2026), which ends the step at `scaler.update()` and records skipped overflow steps, or use `auto_step=False` and call `session.step(lr=...)` once per iteration.

Do not ship a tracking-store plugin with its own URI scheme (`tensorquill+https://…`). MLflow selects the plugin by scheme, but its proxied artifact store (`mlflow-artifacts:`) accepts only `http` and `https` tracking URIs, so `log_artifact` would fail for teams that use it. The model registry would also need its own plugin.

**Rules (matching `sdk/src/tensorquill/capture.py` and `probe.py` as of Nov 16, 2026)**

- **Static compiled hooks.** Register forward hooks **before** `torch.compile` and keep them attached after `session.close()`. With default PyTorch settings, hooks added to a module that had none when compiled are silently ignored (`skip_nnmodule_hook_guards`); on a module that already had hooks, Dynamo guards the hook dictionary, so adding or removing a hook triggers a recompile. `init()` rejects an outer `torch.compile(model)` wrapper (`_orig_mod`).
- **Fixed-shape on-device hook bodies.** Hook bodies compute mean, std, absolute max, and zero fraction per watched module (or router expert counts via compare-and-sum into an `int64` buffer) and write into preallocated on-device buffers marked with `torch._dynamo.mark_static_address`. No histograms, `.cpu()`, `bincount`, `nonzero`, numpy, or I/O run inside a compiled hook. Across microbatches (gradient accumulation), hooks keep the largest `abs_max`, sum router counts, and keep the last microbatch's `mean`, `std`, and `zero_frac`; recomputed forward passes during activation checkpointing backward are skipped.
- **Gradient norms without backward hooks.** Read `p.grad` norms in `register_optimizer_step_pre_hook` (before the lead optimizer's `step()` or `zero_grad()`, or after `step(closure)` when a closure is used) with `torch._foreach_norm(..., dtype=torch.float32)`, grouped by `(device, dtype)` after unwrapping `DTensor.to_local()`, falling back to chunked `torch.linalg.vector_norm` when `_foreach_norm` is unavailable. Because the pre-hook runs after user gradient clipping, TensorQuill also wraps `torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_` while the session is open (and keeps the wrapper callable after `close()`) to record the pre-clipping total norm (`clip_grad_norm`) at zero extra cost; callers can also call `session.record_grad_norm(norm)` or `session.record_grads()` before manual clipping.
- **Ring buffer and bounded writer queue.** On each step, outside the compiled graph, hook buffers copy into slot `_slot` of an on-device ring buffer of length `every` (`every=50` by default). Every `every` steps (and on `close()`), the float32 ring buffer, int64 router counts, clip-norm ring, replica-spread ring, and logged-scalar ring move to the CPU and are enqueued to a background writer thread with a bounded queue (`WRITER_QUEUE = 8` blocks of `every` steps). If the sink stalls, training blocks on `queue.put` with a one-time `RuntimeWarning` rather than growing host memory or dropping steps.
- **Tier 2 probe pass (`probe=`, `probe_every=1000`).** Requires PyTorch 2.6+ (`torch.compiler.set_stance("force_eager")`). Runs after the step's ring-buffer copy and clears the capture hook buffers afterwards so probe activations never pollute training statistics. Snapshots and restores each submodule's `training` flag, turns off deterministic algorithms for `histc` / `index_add_`, and restores CPU/CUDA RNG states. Temporarily switches Hugging Face `flash_attention_2` and `flash_attention_3` modules to `sdpa` and wraps `F.scaled_dot_product_attention` so the fused kernel still produces the forward output while the wrapper recomputes `softmax(QKᵀ)` in query blocks of at most $2^{26}$ scores (256 MB), repeating `key` heads to match `query` heads under grouped-query attention (`enable_gqa`). Each query row's entropy is normalized by the log of that row's visible key count (`entropy_share`), excluding all-masked and non-finite rows. If a probe raises an exception, `probe.error` is recorded with a `RuntimeWarning` and the step record is preserved.
- **Per-rank files.** Every rank writes its own file, `tensorquill-out/<run_id>.rank<rank>.jsonl`, reading the rank from `torch.distributed` or `RANK` / `LOCAL_RANK`.
- **Compiler gate.** Verified in-process via Dynamo counters: [test_compile.py](sdk/tests/test_compile.py) and [test_probe.py](sdk/tests/test_probe.py) run on CPU with `error_on_recompile=True` and check `counters["graph_break"]` is empty; [gpu_check.py](sdk/examples/gpu_check.py) also checks `counters["inductor"]["cudagraph_skips"]` on CUDA, with `TORCH_LOGS="graph_breaks,recompiles,cudagraphs"` enabled for diagnostic logs.

**Data tiers**

| Tier | Data | Computed how | When | Approx. size |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 0 | Logged scalars (`session.log`), learning rate, `clip_grad_norm`, per-parameter `grad_norms`, per-block activation `mean`, `std`, `abs_max`, `zero_frac`, and 50-step float64 `weight_norms` / `weights_unchanged` with `optimizer_ownership` and `weight_dtypes` | Forward hooks into static buffers; `_foreach_norm` in optimizer pre-hook | Computed every step (weights every 50), flushed to CPU every `every=50` steps | ~26 KB JSON per step on Granite full capture |
| 1 | MoE expert load counts (`moe`), router entropy, diversity `exp(H)/N`, dead-expert fraction (`moe_stats`), and `moe_top_k` | On-device compare-and-sum per router in forward hook; entropy and diversity computed on CPU flush | Accumulated every step, flushed every `every=50` steps | ~4 KB JSON per step for 24 routers × 32 experts |
| 1 | Cross-GPU spread (`replica_spread`): float64 L2 norm, sum and seeded projection per replicated parameter | Per-rank float64 norm, sum and projection after `optimizer.step()`, one `all_gather` on `replica_group` | Every `replica_every=50` steps where `(step + 1) % replica_every == 0` | 24 bytes per parameter tensor per rank on the wire |
| 2 | Activation histograms (64 bins) per repeated block; per-head attention `max_logit`, `entropy`, `entropy_share`, `masked_rows`, `invalid_rows`, and 64×64 pooled attention map | Eager probe pass (`force_eager`, `no_grad`) on a fixed batch | Every `probe_every=1000` steps | ~0.5–2 MB JSON per probe step on Granite (24 layers) |
| 3 | Full activations for flagged layers (replay) | Postponed from v1 | — | — |

Attention requires the Tier 2 probe pass because FlashAttention and fused SDPA never materialize the attention matrix during training. The probe uses a fixed batch so distributions and attention maps compare cleanly across steps.

## W&B support

Support W&B from inside the customer's own training script. Hold off on anything that uses a customer's W&B key until a lawyer has read W&B's terms.

| Route | What it does | State and risk |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Read the active run in the script | `tensorquill.init(model)` reads `wandb.run.id` once when a run is active (after `run_id=`, `MLFLOW_RUN_ID`, and `mlflow.active_run()`) and tags the tensor stream with it | **Built.** Low risk: uses the MIT-licensed client on the user's machine and touches no W&B server |
| Wrap `wandb.log` | Keeps TensorQuill steps aligned with the steps W&B records | **Not built.** Low legal risk, but leans on client internals (`commit=False`, explicit `step=`, `define_metric`) |
| Viewer reads W&B history with the customer's key, on their machine | Pulls scalars into the viewer next to the tensor views | **Not built.** Needs legal review; terms restrict accessing the service to build a competing product |
| Hosted service holds the customer's W&B key | Cloud service pulls their W&B data | **Not built.** Highest risk; terms also restrict sharing access credentials with a third party |

Do not read `wandb.run.step` per training step. It waits on a round trip to the W&B background process, with a 30-second timeout, and raises an error in `mode="shared"` runs. It is also W&B's own log counter, not the training step: PyTorch Lightning's `WandbLogger` records the step as a `trainer/global_step` metric. Use the SDK's own global step.

The W&B Python client is MIT-licensed. The W&B server is proprietary, and its free local licence is for personal projects only, so never bundle it. If TensorQuill ever ships a default metadata server, use MLflow, whose client and server are both Apache 2.0. The terms were read through a third-party summary, not the original text.

## Cross-GPU detector

This detector checks that weights meant to be identical copies on several GPUs stay identical, and fires on the first step they drift apart.

**Why it is in the plan.** In the BLOOM-176B run, gradient clipping was applied on only one worker in each tensor-parallel group. LayerNorm weights that should have matched drifted apart while the loss curve looked healthy. It was found 13 days into the run, by a test that compared the weights across ranks, and the bug predated the run (TrainCheck's write-ups say ten or fifteen days). A separate study of faulty hardware found the same pattern: parameters on an unhealthy node drift while the loss stays close to normal.

**How it works (`sdk/src/tensorquill/distributed.py`)**

1. Every `replica_every` steps (`50` by default), where `(step + 1) % replica_every == 0` after `optimizer.step()`, each rank computes three fingerprints per parameter tensor, all in float64: its L2 norm, its sum, and a dot product with fixed pseudo-random weights derived from the element index (no random number generator is used, so training's random streams are untouched; decided Nov 15, 2026). The projection catches changes that keep the norm and sum, such as two elements swapping places; it is a screen, not proof of equality. Scheduling from the shared step number rather than a rank-local call counter guarantees every rank enters the collective on the exact same step.
2. The ranks that hold copies of the same weights share their `[n_params, 3]` fingerprints with the same single `dist.all_gather` on `replica_group` (24 bytes per parameter tensor per rank). `replica_projection=False` sends two columns.
3. Every rank in `replica_group` computes each fingerprint's spread divided by `max(mean_norm, 1e-12)` per parameter and records it in its own step record as `replica_spread`; a NaN or Inf weight on any rank makes that parameter's spread non-finite.
4. The detector (`sdk/src/tensorquill/evidence.py`) fires when the largest of `norm_spread`, `sum_spread` and `projection_spread`, each normalized by the parameter's mean L2 norm across ranks, exceeds `drift_tolerance` (`build_evidence(..., drift_tolerance=1e-6)` by default, one relative tolerance for both spread components) or is not finite; non-finite parameters carry `"non_finite": true` in the `replica_drift` incident.

It runs outside the compiled step and needs no hooks. See the implementation in [distributed.py](sdk/src/tensorquill/distributed.py) and the two-process test script in [replica_check.py](sdk/examples/replica_check.py), which also handle empty parameter shards, Apple MPS fallback (where float64 is unavailable), missing `_foreach_norm`, and non-finite weights.

The sum is paired with the L2 norm because a norm cannot see a sign flip: `[1, -1]` and `[1, 1]` have the same norm and different sums. Both fingerprints are float64 because a norm or sum in bf16 or fp16 is too coarse to show small drift. On CUDA, `torch._foreach_norm(ts, 2, dtype=torch.float64)` reads bf16 and fp16 tensors directly in one fused kernel per `(device, dtype)` group; sums and non-fast-path norms split flat tensors into `1 << 24`-element chunks (at most 128 MB temporary float64 memory). A float64 norm pass over Granite's 2.6 GB of bf16 weights measured ~3.7 ms per check on the RTX PRO 6000 (~0.07 ms/step amortized at `replica_every=50`); the two-GPU `all_gather` cost over NCCL is not yet measured.

**Where it applies**

| Setup | What is checked | Note |
| --- | --- | --- |
| DDP (data parallel) | Every trainable parameter | Pass `replica_group=dist.group.WORLD`; each rank holds a full copy |
| Tensor parallelism | Only weights replicated across TP ranks, such as LayerNorm | Pass `replica_params=` (Nov 14, 2026): a `(name, param) -> bool` filter, or the parameters or their names. With Megatron, `lambda name, p: not getattr(p, "tensor_model_parallel", False)` selects the replicated weights. Without it, every trainable parameter is compared, which reports false drift under tensor parallelism |
| Hybrid FSDP (sharded inside a node, replicated across nodes) | Each rank's local shard, compared across the inter-node replication group | Pass the replication process group as `replica_group=`. `distributed.py` unwraps FSDP2 `DTensor`s via `.to_local()`; FSDP1 flat parameters are not supported |
| Fully sharded (FSDP full shard, ZeRO-3) | Nothing | Each rank holds a different shard, so there are no copies to compare |

**Limits**

- A swap of two values inside a tensor changes neither the norm nor the sum. The seeded projection (built Nov 15, 2026, step 1 above) catches it for one extra pass over the tensor; it is a screen, so equal fingerprints are still not proof of equal weights. Add a full hash at checkpoint time if that matters.
- Buffers such as BatchNorm running statistics differ per rank by design and are left out.
- It does not catch faults that hit every rank alike, slow nodes, or crashes.
- Verified on two CPU processes with Gloo ([replica_check.py](sdk/examples/replica_check.py)); the two-GPU NCCL run and the `1e-6` tolerance on healthy multi-GPU training are pending Gate 2.

## Ingest server and local-first streaming

Multi-node runs need a single store that collects every rank's stream and runs detectors before the job ends. The ingest pipeline combines three patterns from the reference codebases:

- **Local log first and offset-based flow control (from W&B):** W&B writes every record to its local `.wandb` transaction log first and keeps at most 32 records in memory (`InMemorySize: 32`), collapsing any network backlog into a 24-byte file-offset range (`FlowControlBufferSavedChunk` in `wandb/core/internal/stream/flowcontrolbuffer.go`) and seeking back to `lastReadOffset` when a trailing record is incomplete (`ResetLastRead` in `wandb/core/internal/runsync/runreader.go`). By contrast, MLflow's `AsyncLoggingQueue` buffers in an unbounded Python `Queue()` with no disk log (`mlflow/utils/async_logging/async_logging_queue.py`), and Rerun's `re_grpc_client` drops queued messages if its 5-second flush times out (`re_grpc_client/src/write.rs`).
- **Single-binary self-hosted Rust server with bounded byte quota (from Rerun):** Rerun ships an open-source Rust server (`re_server` on port `51234`, `re_grpc_server` on port `9876`) using `tonic`, `arrow`, `parquet`, `object_store`, `tcp_nodelay(true)`, `http2_adaptive_window(true)`, a Bearer token `Authenticator` interceptor (`re_auth`), and a byte-bounded input channel (`CHANNEL_SIZE_BYTES = 128 MiB` in `re_quota_channel`).
- **Composite-key deduplication on retry (from W&B and MLflow):** W&B deduplicates `file_stream` retries by virtual line offset (`HistoryLineNum` in `filestreamstate.go`), and MLflow deduplicates `LogBatch` retries by upserting on `(run_uuid, key, step, timestamp)` (`sqlalchemy_store_upsert_utils.py`).

### Specification

1. **Local append-only file (`sdk/src/tensorquill/sink.py`):**
   - The SDK always writes `tensorquill-out/<run_id>.rank<rank>.jsonl` first via the bounded 8-block `JsonlSink` queue (`WRITER_QUEUE = 8`). Each JSON line carries `"schema_version": 1`, `"run_id"`, `"rank"`, `"step"`, `"ts"` (Unix seconds when the step ended) and the captured metrics.
   - Local files use JSON Lines rather than Parquet because Parquet writes its footer only on close; a killed or preempted training process would leave an unreadable Parquet file.
2. **Opt-in Flight sender (`sdk/src/tensorquill/flight.py`):**
   - Enabled only when `endpoint=` or `TENSORQUILL_ENDPOINT` (`grpc://host:port` or `grpcs://host:port`) is set; default local runs open zero sockets.
   - A background daemon thread tails `tensorquill-out/<run_id>.rank<rank>.jsonl` from the byte offset saved in `tensorquill-out/<run_id>.rank<rank>.offset` (updated via atomic write-and-rename of a scratch file of its own per writer after each server `PutResult` ack; since Nov 19, 2026 the file also holds the server, run, rank and digests of the file's bytes, and a resume that does not match them starts from 0). It holds one `DoPut` stream open per rank; the server acknowledges an offset only once those rows are in a stored Parquet part (see Decisions).
   - **Torn-line recovery:** only complete lines ending in `\n` that parse as JSON and match `run_id` are batched. If EOF is reached mid-line (or a crashed prior process left a truncated line before the next newline), the sender does not advance past the incomplete tail until `\n` arrives. Since Nov 19, 2026 the next writer to open the file ends a torn last line with a newline before it appends, so the fragment stays a line of its own and is skipped.
   - **Batching and backoff:** sends a `pyarrow.RecordBatch` over Arrow Flight `DoPut` every **50 steps or 2 seconds** (whichever comes first, capped at **4 MiB** uncompressed). Holds at most one batch in RAM; on disconnect or `Status::resource_exhausted`, sleeps with full-jitter exponential backoff (`100 ms` base, `30 s` cap) and re-reads from the saved `.offset` on reconnect. On `session.close()`, flushes remaining complete lines with a 5-second timeout and prints the local `.jsonl` path if unacked bytes remain.
3. **Arrow Flight `DoPut` contract and schema (`server/src/ingest.rs`):**
   - Crate versions: `arrow-flight = "60.1.0"`, `arrow = "60.1.0"`, `parquet = "60.1.0"`, `object_store = "0.14.2"`, `tonic = "0.14"`, `tokio = "1"`.
   - `FlightDescriptor`: path `["runs", "<run_id>", "rank=<rank>"]`.
   - `FlightData.app_metadata`: UTF-8 JSON `{"schema_version": 1, "run_id": "<id>", "rank": 0, "min_step": 0, "max_step": 49, "end_byte_offset": 128450}`.
   - **Arrow schema (one row per step):**
     - Scalar/vector columns: `schema_version: Int32`, `run_id: Utf8`, `rank: Int32`, `attempt: Int64`, `step: Int64`, `ts: Float64`, `lr: List<Float64>`, `grad_norm: Float64` (nullable), `clip_grad_norm: Float64` (nullable), `weight_norm: Float64` (nullable), `weights_unchanged: List<Utf8>` (nullable), `step_skipped: Boolean` (nullable), `grad_scale: Float64` (nullable).
     - Map columns: `scalars: Map<Utf8, Float64>`, `grad_norms: Map<Utf8, Float64>`, `weight_norms: Map<Utf8, Float64>`, `moe_top_k: Map<Utf8, Int32>`, `moe_counts: Map<Utf8, List<Int64>>`, `replica_spread: Map<Utf8, List<Float64>>`.
     - Canonical JSON-string columns for nested objects whose keys vary by model: `layers_json: Utf8` (nullable), `moe_stats_json: Utf8` (nullable), `probe_json: Utf8` (nullable), and `extra_json: Utf8` (nullable) for any key or value the columns above cannot hold. Storing these three nested structures as UTF-8 JSON strings inside Parquet preserves 100% lossless round-tripping back to the dict format `evidence.py` expects (`IM2`).
4. **Deduplication, backpressure, auth, and Parquet layout (`server/src/storage.rs`):**
   - **Watermark deduplication:** the server tracks the last kept step per `(run_id, rank, attempt)`, initialized when an attempt's first batch arrives by reading the `max(step)` row-group statistics from the newest existing Parquet footer under `{store_root}/runs/{run_id}/rank={rank}/attempt={attempt}/`. `attempt` is a column of every row (the writing session's start time, Unix milliseconds); a batch that spans two attempts is split. On each `DoPut` batch, rows with `step <= last_acked_step[(run_id, rank, attempt)]` are filtered out before buffering, making client retries idempotent.
   - **Memory cap:** total buffered Arrow bytes across all open streams are bounded at **128 MiB**. When full, `DoPut` returns `tonic::Status::resource_exhausted("ingest buffer full")` so senders pause on their local `.jsonl` file.
   - **Parquet flushing:** because S3 and GCS do not support in-place append, each `(run_id, rank)` buffer flushes an immutable Zstd-compressed Parquet part file every **500 steps or 10 seconds** (and on stream EOF or `SIGTERM`) via `object_store::ObjectStore::put` to:
     `{store_root}/runs/{run_id}/rank={rank}/attempt={attempt}/part-{min_step:08}-{max_step:08}.parquet`
     and replies with `PutResult{app_metadata: {"end_byte_offset": N}}` after the flush succeeds.
   - **Authentication and TLS:** `tensorquill-server --listen 127.0.0.1:50051 --store file:///var/lib/tensorquill` binds to loopback by default. Binding to a non-loopback address requires either `TENSORQUILL_TOKEN` (checked by a `tonic` interceptor against `Authorization: Bearer <token>` in constant time) or `--allow-unauthenticated`, with optional TLS via `--tls-cert` and `--tls-key`.
5. **Live detection (as built; the benchmark below decided it on Nov 15, 2026):**
   - The server runs the detectors over each part once it is stored and rewrites `runs/{run_id}/rank={rank}/incidents.json`; stalls are in `runs/{run_id}/watchdog.json`. `python -m tensorquill --watch {store_root}/runs/{run_id}` polls every 2 seconds, takes the incidents from those files while the report is current, and writes `{run_id}.evidence.json` (`schema_version: 1`) atomically via rename. On a local `.jsonl` file, or when the report is missing or stale, it reconstructs step dicts losslessly and runs the Python detectors itself.
   - **The benchmark that decided it:** a 64-rank synthetic MoE stream (50-step flushes), measuring (a) Python `evidence.py` reading new Parquet parts via `pyarrow.parquet` against (b) an in-server Rust detector pass over incoming `RecordBatch`es, with the rule to keep detectors in Python unless the Python worker exceeded **250 ms** per 50-step flush across 64 ranks or **1 CPU core** steady-state. Python exceeded both at Granite's shape; the Rust pass takes about 60 ms (Decisions: "Live detection runs in Rust").

## Viewer and agent evidence

Use stock Rerun 0.43.2 as the interim viewer, until TensorQuill's own Rune app covers the views below (decided Nov 14, 2026: Rerun is temporary) (`rerun-sdk==0.43.2` pinned in [sdk/pyproject.toml](sdk/pyproject.toml)). The [MoE bake-off](experiments/moe-bakeoff/README.md) and shipped exporter (`export_rerun` in [evidence.py](sdk/src/tensorquill/evidence.py)) use a blueprint and built-in views for the detector summary, status heatmap, per-expert load table, probe tab, logged scalars tab, and diversity timeline. Keep the evidence schema independent of Rerun so engineers and agents read the exact same numbers in JSON.

- **Schemas and versioning (`sdk/src/tensorquill/evidence.py`, `capture.py`).** Every JSONL step record carries `"schema_version": 1`, `"ts"` and `"attempt"` (all Nov 14, 2026; `attempt` was added without changing the version, because it is optional to readers: a record without it reads as attempt 0); readers treat records without `schema_version` as version `0` (missing `moe_top_k` already defaults from `--top-k`). The exported `<input>.evidence.json` carries `"schema_version": 1`, `"run_id"`, `"layer_count"`, `"expert_count"`, `"router_names"`, `"overload_threshold"`, `"top_k"`, `"starve_window"`, `"starve_ratio"`, `"incidents"` (each with `kind` and `step`, kind-specific fields, and `scalars` when values were logged with `session.log`), and `"samples"` (one per router per step). Incident fields added Nov 16, 2026, all optional to readers and without a version change: on `grad_spike`, `trigger: "parameter"` and `sustained` when one parameter's own history raised it; on `stalled_weights`, `onsets` (each step at which parameters first stalled, with `parameters`, `stalled`, `trained_before` and `optimizer`), `stalled_total`, `onsets_omitted` and `weight_dtypes`. Evidence written by `--watch` on a server's store also carries an optional `"detection"` (`source`, `state`, `reason`, and the attempts and steps compared; Nov 15, 2026), and when its incidents come from the server's report `"samples"` is empty and the router keys are absent.
- **Headless screenshots and sample-step sidecar.** Use Rerun's headless Viewer for per-view PNGs and `rerun viewer-mcp` for live screenshots and the widget tree. Because a timeline view displays the most recent sample at or before the cursor, exporting a screenshot for `<run_id>` at requested `<req_step>` writes `<out_dir>/<run_id>.step<req_step>.<view>.png` alongside `<out_dir>/<run_id>.step<req_step>.<view>.meta.json` containing `{"run_id": "<id>", "requested_step": <req_step>, "sample_step": <actual_step>, "view": "<view>", "rrd_path": "<path>"}`.

**Own viewer app on Rune (decided Nov 14, 2026).** Rerun is temporary. TensorQuill will ship its own Rust app built on Rune instead of a custom `ViewClass` inside Rerun. Rerun 0.43.2's built-in dataframe view cannot sort, and `ViewerClient.send_table` opens a separate table page outside the recording blueprint; a custom Rerun view would also tie the product to Rerun's extension interfaces.

- **Data.** The app reads `evidence.json` and the per-rank JSONL (and the ingest server's Parquet parts later) directly. `.rrd` stays an export for the interim Rerun viewer only, so capture and detectors do not change.
- **Parity before replacing Rerun.** Step timeline with scrubbing, layer × expert status heatmap, sortable expert-load table that follows layer selection, time series, probe histograms and attention maps, and loading a full Granite run (650 steps, about 17 MB of JSON) smoothly.
- **Agent access.** Rune apps render headless to PNG and expose their element tree, which replaces Rerun's headless viewer and `rerun viewer-mcp` for Step 19; keep the `requested_step` / `sample_step` sidecar.
- **Headless on Linux.** Step 20's software-Vulkan check (`lavapipe`) applies to the Rune app; Rune renders through wgpu.

## Roadmap

Twelve weeks in five phases starting Nov 13, 2026. There are no customer interviews: the author is the first user, and TensorQuill is validated on the author's own training runs first, then through a free public release. Gate criteria are thresholds set before measuring and changed only at a gate review. If a gate fails after one extra week of work, follow the gate's cut order.

### Progress so far

Work ran ahead of the phase order: the SDK, the incident detectors (seventeen kinds at first, thirty-two since Nov 16, 2026), and the Rerun exporter were built before Gate 0 and Gate 1. Details and raw numbers are in the [SDK README](sdk/README.md); the [failure list](TensorQuill%20failure%20list.md) records what each detector covers. State as of Nov 16, 2026; the detector count and the packaging and release rows were brought up to date on Nov 17, 2026, and the other rows were not re-read then. To add TensorQuill to a job, follow the [run-on-your-job guide](sdk/RUN_ON_YOUR_JOB.md).

Status values are fixed to **Done**, **In progress**, **Not started**, or **Postponed**, with the remaining done criterion stated for each unfinished row.

**What the table above does not yet say (added Nov 19, 2026; the rows carry their earlier wording and their gate statuses still hold).** Two outside code reviews and five waves of fix lanes (R, T, U, V and W, then a sixth, X, and a seventh, Y) came after it; each is in the decision log below, and `OPEN_BUGS.md` is the list of what is still open: 13 of the 72 rows it began with after wave Y, 10 of them fixed in part, two of them P1 (a process group that fails after `init`, decided on Nov 20, 2026 and not built; every stop notice lost on a streaming run).
- *Row 11:* the writer queue is no longer `WRITER_QUEUE = 8` blocks that make training wait. It holds about 400 steps, training never waits, and a sink that fails or falls behind stops capture with one warning (wave U).
- *Row 12:* router counts follow the routing map a Megatron-style router returns, a negative index is no assignment and an index past the experts refuses the router (waves U and W). The drift check is agreed among the ranks at `init` (wave W). Still unmet: `replica_check.py` on two GPUs over NCCL.
- *Row 14:* which attempt owns a rank is decided by when it was first seen and kept in `attempts.json`; a rank whose capture stopped has a state of its own; the protocol number is 3 (wave W).
- *Row 15:* the stalled-weights rule breaks a streak on any change, and both engines read attempts in first-seen order and steps in step order (wave W). The `activation_drift` false alarms on healthy small trainings are still open.
- *Row 13:* CI now runs Python 3.10 with PyTorch 2.6 and PyTorch 2.9 as well, and the `package` job blocks. On the push of Nov 19, 2026 those runs found two faults on older PyTorch that macOS with PyTorch 2.15 had not; their fix is committed and waits for a push.
- *No gate has moved:* every unmet criterion in the table needs a GPU run, a real job, the agent trial or the release.

Findings along the way:

- **Fixed per-step thresholds failed on real data.** Healthy Granite has experts picked by 85–100% of tokens, and with two documents per batch single experts jump to over half the tokens for one step. Top-k overload therefore requires the router's diversity to drop below 0.6 of its recent median (healthy minimum 0.82; the seeded collapse 0.29). Experts nearly unused for hundreds of steps needed a 50-step windowed check, since one empty step is noise.
- **float32 norms cannot show whether bf16 weights changed.** A first stalled-weight check reported 147 of 218 parameters falsely; float64 norms fixed it but cost ~3.7 ms per check on the RTX PRO 6000. Reading the weights alone costs ~1.5 ms, so the check runs every 50 steps (`weights=50`), as `wandb.watch` (1,000 batches) and TrainCheck (`sampling_interval`) do.
- **Pure-bf16 training freezes norm weights.** In the Granite benchmark's setup (bf16 weights, AdamW, lr 1e-5, no float32 master weights) all 49 RMSNorm weights never change: their bf16 step size near 1.0 is ~0.008. TensorQuill reports this.
- **Block summaries miss growth in a few ordinary channels (A100, Nov 15, 2026).** SmolLM2-360M's block 16 output has `abs_max` about 20,000 and `std` 31 to 37, so two channels scaled 100× moved `std` 1.07 to 1.14× and `abs_max` 0.95 to 1.07× although the loss went from 2.3 to 6.9. Growth of the massive channels or of the whole block is seen at the seeded step. Per-channel statistics would see the rest and are not captured (decision: "What the dense checks seed").
- **A fault in one block shows in every later block.** Seeded in block 16 of 32, the activation spike covered blocks 16 to 31 and listed block 31 first, because modules are listed by ratio. A reader would name the wrong layer. Fixed Nov 15, 2026 in the Python and Rust detectors: the incident leads with the first block in the model, then the rest by ratio (so the largest is second and a list cut at 20 keeps both; checked on an RTX PRO 6000 with the case the old list would have broken: a fault in block 8 reached 24 blocks, the list holds 20, and block 8 still leads with block 31 second), and the summary reads "first in model.layers.16 … the largest, abs_max 723×, is in model.layers.31" on the A100 traces. The first version of the fix would have named the wrong block over the server: the Flight sender wrote `layers_json` with sorted keys, so `model.layers.10` came before `model.layers.2`; `layers_json` now keeps the model's order, and a test sends 12 unpadded blocks through the real server and Parquet.
- **The `Trainer` callback passes on a GPU.** `trainer_check.py` on an A100 (Inductor, fp16, clipping 1, SmolLM2-360M): all ten checks with `--fault massive` and with `--fault block`; 0 graph breaks and no recompiles; loss and clipping norm equal to `Trainer`'s logged values.
- **The probe on a dense GPU run.** SmolLM2-360M on an A100: full capture +2.94%, a probe step +170 ms (0.17 ms per step at every 1,000), the seeded block's largest value 100× between probes, and the next block's attention gone uniform (entropy share 0.34 to 1.00).
- **A100 router-only overhead, profiled.** Granite on an A100: +0.41%, +0.80%, +0.95% over three paired 100-step rounds, against +1.06% to +1.33% at an earlier commit; the added time is GPU work (+1.81 ms busy, 26 more launches, syncs unchanged), not host waits.

Open gaps:

- **A gradient spike in one small parameter: closed on CPU Nov 16, 2026.** `detect_grad_spikes` used to raise an incident only when the total gradient norm reached 5× its median; a parameter holding a share `s` of the total, scaled by `f`, moves the total to `sqrt(1 + (f²−1)s²)`, so ×100 on a `k_proj` holding 0.18% moved it ×1.02 and nothing was reported. Now a parameter at 5× its own median for 3 recorded steps in a row is reported on its own (Decisions: "Per-parameter gradient and stalled-weight rules"). *What is left:* the `--factor 100` run on a GPU (next action 2); and a jump in one small parameter that lasts one or two steps, which is not reported because healthy captures are full of them (277 to 473 single steps at 5× in each 650-step Granite capture, up to 36×).
- **`stalled_weights` named only the parameters stalled at the first onset: closed on CPU Nov 16, 2026.** Each parameter is judged on its own history and the incident's `onsets` name every later stall at its own step, in Python and in the server; the former strict xfail `test_with_bfloat16_weights_the_incident_names_the_seeded_parameter` passes. *What is left:* the gate command on a GPU in bf16 (next action 2). Seen on the recorded SmolLM2 capture and not explained by a run of its own: four bf16 norm weights that had changed at an early check stalled at step 399, so in low precision "changed before, then stopped" is not by itself a planted freeze.
- **Full-capture overhead.** Block-level full capture (`Stage 3` in [sdk/README.md](sdk/README.md#rtx-pro-6000-run-with-block-level-capture-stage-3)) measured +4.15% on the RTX PRO 6000 (~0.36 ms routers, ~0.85 ms block activations, ~1.5 ms per-step gradient norms). *Blocks:* None (`capture="routers"` is the MoE default; `capture="full"` is opt-in for debugging). *Closed when:* optional `grads=N` step sampling is benchmarked against single-step spike recall.
- **Headless rendering on Linux.** Headless screenshots have only been run on macOS. Rerun's native viewer on headless Linux requires Vulkan (`mesa-vulkan-drivers` `lavapipe` + `libvulkan1`). *Blocks:* Step 20 and Gate 3. *Closed when:* two consecutive headless exports in a Linux container produce byte-identical PNGs.
- **Many ranks on a shared filesystem.** `WRITER_QUEUE = 8` (`88a20e7`) bounds memory by pausing training with a `RuntimeWarning` when disk writes stall. *Blocks:* Gate 2 multi-GPU run. *Closed when:* tested with ≥2 ranks writing local `.jsonl` and streaming to `tensorquill-server`: done on CPU with two ranks on Nov 15, 2026; what is left is the two-GPU NCCL run and a shared filesystem.
- **`--watch` on a long run.** Python recomputation still reruns the detectors over the whole run (`--recompute`, fallback when reports are missing/stale, or one-shot `build_evidence`). Measured Nov 15, 2026 on a synthetic store of Granite's shape: one rank's update takes 0.4 s at 500 steps, 1.6 s at 2,000 and 8.1 s at 10,000, against 250 ms. On the live path (next action 4, decided Nov 15, 2026), steady-state `--watch` now reads the server's `incidents.json` (2 ms at 10,800 steps) instead of rerunning Python. *Blocks:* only fallback/offline updates (`build_evidence`, `--recompute`) and whether a 10,000-step server restart rebuild (6–7 s) should stop holding back the rank's next part. *Closed when:* either incremental checkpoints/state are kept across `--recompute`/`build_evidence` runs, or 250 ms holds at 2,000 steps for offline recompute as well.
- **Untuned thresholds.** The spike factor (5×), diversity drop (0.6), starved ratio (10% of an even share), and stall count (3 checks) rest on Granite and synthetic fixtures. *Blocks:* Gate 3. *Closed when:* evaluated on the author's own dogfood job without false alarms on healthy segments.

**Without a GPU**

5. **Per-parameter checks (decided and built Nov 16, 2026; Gate 2).** Built without recording ‖Δw‖/‖w‖: both open detector gaps closed from fields the capture already writes, at no capture cost (Decisions: "Per-parameter gradient and stalled-weight rules").
   - Done: a parameter whose gradient norm stays at least 5× its own 50-step median for 3 recorded steps in a row is a spike even when the total is not; every parameter's stall is named at its own onset, with whether it had changed before; `weight_dtypes` on each weight check; Python and Rust, with parity tests and 19 deliberate changes to the Rust rules each failing them.

   - Left: the three `seeded_failures.py` commands on a GPU (action 2), and the same no-false-alarm check on the author's own job (Gate 0).
   - Not built, on purpose: the update-to-weight ratio itself. A zero Δw in bf16 does not separate an update lost to precision from a zero learning rate any better than the unchanged norm does, and a magnitude ("updates too large or too small") has no threshold to judge it by yet. If a real job asks for it, measure this design first, since it needs no full copy of the weights: on a weight check, read a fixed strided sample of each parameter (a view, then one `cat` per dtype) before and after `optimizer.step()`, and record the RMS of the difference over the RMS of the sample. Its GPU cost is not measured.

Each step, the norm of Adam's update to each parameter, read from the optimizer's own state with no copy of the weights; an incident when the largest update jumps from one step to the next, by the paper's rule (the prototype below says why not a ratio to the parameter's own median). Opt-in, and it does not fire the trigger by default | Altenbernd et al., arXiv 2604.00726, pp. 6-9; review proposal list, second candidate | `capture.py`, `flight.py`, `ingest.rs`, `detect.rs`, `evidence.py`, golden files | Nothing. A CPU prototype ran on Nov 17, 2026 (below the table) | On a CPU toy a seeded gradient fault fires at its step and names the parameter; clean toy runs over several seeds stay silent; an optimizer without Adam's moments is `not_checked` with its reason; Python and the server agree on the golden cases. **Built and merged Nov 18, 2026** (Decisions: "Four lanes merged"). Open: the evidence lacks the update over the learning rate, and a fault in Adam's own state is not seen (row Q2) |
| K2 | **Cross-rank activation compare** (an anomaly). At the same step, compare one per-layer activation statistic across data-parallel ranks and name the rank far from the others' median. This is the check for corruption that `replica_drift` cannot see | TrainSDC, arXiv 2608.30769, pp. 4-5, 10-11; review proposal 9 | The all-ranks view in `evidence.py`, the cross-rank part of the server, tests | K1 merged (both add a kind to the same tables). At least three ranks. **A design choice first** (Decisions: "Second batch of papers"): ranks in data parallelism see different batches, so their statistics differ by more than rounding and a tolerance costs precision. Two designs avoid that: two ranks run the same inputs and are compared (Ma et al. propose this as a shadow replica that compares gradients; with TensorQuill's probe batch it would compare block statistics), or a forward output is compared with its recomputation under activation checkpointing. A mismatch is reported as "bug or hardware", never as hardware (Decisions: "Review corrections after the full read"). Recommended Nov 18, 2026, not yet the author's decision: the same-input design on the probe batch (Decisions: "An outside codebase review, checked, and six fix lanes"). What the one published version needed (TrainSDC, pp. 6, 10-11): 100 clean steps to calibrate, a threshold floor per layer and path, a 64-step history, and an alarm only when the worst rank scores 1.5 times the next; at its chosen setting 1 clean step in 1,628 was flagged, and recall was 41% to 43% for faults in 10 elements and 78% to 86% for 100,000 | A three-process CPU run with one rank's activations corrupted names that rank, and a clean one stays silent. A real multi-GPU run is the author's |

Numbers only, as `weight_trends` is. The mean is signed because the drift has gone both ways: "very negative" in Wortsman et al. (ICLR 2024, p. 4) and "strongly positive" in Laguna M.1 (arXiv 2605.27605, p. 9). | K6 | **Slow ranks, as an observation.** Per-collective timing from the flight recorder, with tokens per rank per step beside it | SCOUT p. 3; Lin et al. pp. 5, 9-12; MegaScale pp. 8, 12 | `flight.py`, the all-ranks view | A multi-rank run to see real timings. Only compute-slow is reachable: the rank that spends the least time in a collective is the slow one, and Flight Recorder's durations can show it. Communication-slow needs counters inside the communication library. How much that covers depends on the cluster: 81.8% of slow cases were compute-slow on one, while on a shared network 42 of 107 four-node jobs were slowed by congestion. The answer may also be "no single rank" (Decisions: "Review corrections after the full read"). Added Nov 18, 2026 from the review's sections 9 to 11: a slowdown passes (mean 10, 24 and 72 minutes in FALCON's three settings, arXiv 2410.12588, pp. 1, 4-5), so the observation needs a start and an end; some slow ranks are the data's doing (one rank with 3.54 times the mean attention work under document packing, Motif 3, p. 13) and the slow rank can change from step to step; forward-only slowness points at a collector pause, forward and backward together at uneven sequence lengths (Lin et al., pp. 11-12) | A three-process CPU run with one slowed rank names it |
| K7 | **Hidden expert collapse.** Last. Corrected twice on Nov 18, 2026. First to "without a source", because the one case report cited for it (Sha) describes routed experts that are exact frozen copies of one network. Then, after the review's sections 10 to 12, to this: **it has a source for the design and for the statistic, and still no reported case.** Motif 3 names "hidden collapse" (an expert gets tokens but its output projection contributes less and less) and "functional collapse" (experts learn similar representations) and gives a monitor with working values for each; Omi, Sen and Farhadi measure the mean pairwise cosine similarity of expert outputs per layer through training. No paper in the folder shows either failure happening in a run, and none gives a threshold, a lead time or a false-alarm rate. One known source of false alarms: with the standard balancing loss, one layer far above the others in expert similarity is common (Omi et al., p. 8). It needs expert outputs, which nothing captures today, and the published statistic runs every expert on the sampled tokens (3.6 to 4.9 times a forward pass), so it is an offline check at checkpoints, not a hook on a training step (Decisions: "Review sections 9 to 12") | Motif 3, arXiv 2608.09119, pp. 16-18 and Table 3 (design and working values, one run); Omi, Sen, Farhadi, arXiv 2506.14038v2, pp. 5-8 (the statistic). Sha, arXiv 2605.19378, pp. 5-6 and 12, does not support it | Not designed | A user or a real job that shows it. Its numbers would have to come from TensorQuill's own seeded runs | Not set |

*K1's prototype (Nov 17, 2026; a throwaway script outside the repository, nothing in the SDK).* A 4-block, 96-wide character model trained from scratch on CPU for 800 steps with AdamW, 100 warm-up steps and cosine decay: 4 seeds with clipping at 1.0 and 4 without. Each step, every parameter's update norm was read from the optimizer's state.

- *A ratio to the parameter's own history does not separate.* Against its own 50-step median (both divided by the learning rate), a healthy parameter's update reached 2.3 to 2.7 times with clipping and 2.5 to 3.4 times without, after step 100, and never 5 times. One parameter's gradient multiplied by 10 to 10,000 at step 400 took that parameter to 2.2 to 4.2 times: inside the healthy range or just above it. Adam's normalisation bounds how far one bad gradient can move a parameter, so the jump is small.
- *The paper's rule does, on this toy.* The change between consecutive steps of the largest RMS update, against its mean over the 100 warm-up steps, scaled by the rise in the pre-clipping gradient norm, with α = 0.05. It flagged no step in the 8 clean runs. It flagged the fault step in 10 of 12 one-parameter cases and 10 of 12 whole-gradient cases; both misses in each set were the ×10 fault with clipping on.
- *What it does not show.* That K1 finds silent data corruption: on production traces loss and gradient-norm monitoring covered 25.0% of defective GPUs, and under injection the gradient norm caught 17% (Zheng et al., OSDI '26, Tables 5 and 6), so K1 is described as a check on the optimizer's update and not as a corruption detector. It also does not cover a fault in the optimizer's own state, which can leave the loss near normal and end in a worse model (row Q2). And on a run that trains its matrices with Muon and only embeddings and vectors with AdamW, as Motif 3 does (p. 10), most parameters would be `update_not_read`. Nor any real run, any model beyond a toy, a GPU, or the cost: reading the state took about 5 ms a step here, on CPU, which says nothing about a GPU. The paper's own figures (no false alarm on 21 clean runs, detection under half at this α in its Fig. 4, about 1% cost) are the paper's, on its models.

*Not new kinds: small code that can start now.*

*Gaps in built kinds, found by the review's sections 10 and 11 (added Nov 18, 2026; none has a fix chosen or a number set). Q is this table's own label; the review's labels are in the "From" column.*

| # | Gap | From | How it was checked | What a fix needs |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Q1 | `attention_logit_growth` misses a slow rise. The baseline is the median of the last five clean probes, which on a steady rise is the value three probes back, so the rule needs growth of about 26% a probe (1.26 cubed is 2). Probing more often makes it blinder. `attention_collapse` uses the same moving baseline | Review H1 (section 10) | **Run here on `main` at `d2f67b5`** with the review's script and the detector unchanged: a logit rising from 10 to 8,078 at 25% a probe gives no incident; 27% a probe fires | A second comparison, against a baseline frozen from the first probes, or an absolute bound. The papers give no number: their reference values use three different statistics |
| Q2 | A fault in Adam's own state is not seen. `update_spike` watches the largest update across parameters and needs the gradient norm to rise; `stalled_weights` needs a norm that is exactly unchanged (the `weights_unchanged` field). One tensor's second moment wrong by 1e12 drops that tensor's update to about 3e-7 of normal for hundreds of steps and meets neither | Review H3 (section 10) and M5 (section 11); He et al., ISCA '23; LLMFT, SC '25 | The review ran it on a CPU toy against `main`; not repeated here. Read here: `detect_update_spikes` takes the largest of the per-parameter values | A rule for one parameter's update collapsing, judged from the `update_rms` already stored. It must tell a fault from the harmless case where the gradient nears Adam's epsilon (Wortsman et al., Fig. G.19). Whether it stays quiet on healthy runs is not known |
| Q3 | Weight norms are recorded every 50 steps and judged by nothing: `weight_norm_trends` gives numbers "with no threshold and no incident". A weight corrupted in memory is seen only through its effect on the gradient | Review M1 (section 11); LLMFT, pp. 4-7 | The review's CPU toy: one weight times 1e8 took its tensor's norm from 5.3 to 21 million and neither the gradient rule nor the update rule fired behind a tanh; `detect_activation_anomalies` was not exercised. Not repeated here. Read here: the docstring | A rule on a jump in one tensor's norm between two checks. The one healthy figure (a largest change of 1.0007 between checks, in five recorded GPU runs) is an observation from short runs, not a threshold |
| Q4 | `replica_drift` keeps only the largest fingerprint minus the smallest and never says which ranks agree, though every rank's fingerprint reaches every rank | Review H2 (section 10); Elsen et al., p. 10 | Read here: `spread_from_fingerprints` in `distributed.py` | Goes with D1: record how many distinct fingerprints there were and which ranks held each. One rank apart points at that rank's update; a run of neighbouring ranks apart points at the network during the gather |

Q1 to Q3 each change or add a rule in the same two files and would move the golden files, so they join that lane, one at a time; Q4 goes with D1, after the release. S7 and S8 edit only the README and the agent instructions and can run beside anything.

### Week 1: set up the dogfood runs (Gate 0)

1. Pick one or two real training jobs run by the author, ideally an MoE model on multiple GPUs with `torch.compile`. These are the dogfood runs for every later gate.
2. Fill in the author's own failures table in [TensorQuill failure list.md](TensorQuill%20failure%20list.md#your-own-failures): what went wrong, how long it took to notice, and what signal would have caught it sooner.
3. Record the dogfood job's tracker (MLflow or W&B) and multi-GPU parallelism setup (DDP, FSDP2, TP, or Megatron).
4. Re-check the competitors named under Product (CoreWeave ARIA, Trainy Pluto, W&B MCP, MLflow MCP, RunSleuth, and TrainCheck) for per-layer capture or detectors launched since Nov 13, 2026.
5. **Gate 0 checklist (pass all four to proceed):**
   - [ ] At least one real training script and command are recorded in the plan or `sdk/RUN_ON_YOUR_JOB.md` and run for ≥100 steps with `tensorquill.init(model)` producing a valid `.jsonl` file.
   - [ ] The author's own failures table in `TensorQuill failure list.md` contains ≥1 failure that scalar loss/grad-norm curves alone did not explain.
   - [ ] The tracker and multi-GPU parallelism mode for the dogfood job are recorded under Decisions below.
   - [x] Competitor check table updated with Nov 2026 status for ARIA, Pluto, tracker MCPs, and TrainCheck (Nov 16, 2026, below; the ARIA blog and Pluto's MCP docs and changelog were read, the other rows are from search results; none is from using the product).
   - *Cut order if blocked:* if no multi-GPU MoE job is available immediately, run Gate 2 on the 2-GPU Granite benchmark plus the author's single-GPU dense/MoE job.

**Competitor check, Nov 16, 2026.** The gap under Product still holds: nothing found captures layer internals or ships failure detectors. What moved since Nov 13 is agent access to what the user already logs.

| Product | Status found | Per-layer capture or failure detectors | Source |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| CoreWeave ARIA | Public preview on Jun 29, 2026; generally available as part of CoreWeave Forge (the blog post's metadata is dated Oct 2, 2026). Automations fire when an experiment completes or a metric crosses a threshold. It reads W&B runs, builds visualizations, compares the code of two branches and drafts each change on a branch for review | None found; it works on what W&B logged | [CoreWeave blog](https://coreweave.com/blog/coreweave-aria-the-coding-agent-that-drives-continuous-improvement-across-the-ai-loop) |
| Trainy Pluto | The MCP server is still marked experimental; its docs list 26 tools (16 in June). Dashboard tools arrived in v0.0.66 (Sep 1, 2026) and `read_dashboard` in v0.0.68 (Sep 23), which reads a set of panels in one call (their example: 50 per-layer gradient-norm panels) with per-curve summaries of spikes, plateaus and non-finite values; the newest release read is v0.0.70 (Sep 28); an experimental Chat runs the same tools through Claude Code or Codex on the user's machine | It displays what the user logs, and summarizes each curve for the agent; no capture SDK, alerts or detectors found | [MCP docs](https://docs.trainy.ai/pluto/mcp), [changelog](https://docs.trainy.ai/pluto/changelog) |
| W&B MCP server | Hosted and local; its stated scope is to query, compare and diagnose W&B Models runs | No gradient or per-layer tool mentioned; only what the run logged | [W&B docs](https://docs.wandb.ai/platform/mcp-server) |
| MLflow MCP server | Ships with MLflow (`mlflow[mcp]`, 3.5.1 or later): traces, feedback, experiments and runs | No | [MLflow docs](https://mlflow.org/docs/latest/genai/mcp/index.html) |
| TrainCheck | No 2026 release with distributed support was found; the authors describe multi-model work (federated, ensembles) as ongoing | Invariant checks that need a reference run; TensorQuill ingests its results (`--traincheck`) | [OSDI '25 page](https://www.usenix.org/conference/osdi25/presentation/jiang) |
| Mechanism-Aligned Observability for Training Instability of Large Language Models ([arXiv 2606.28116](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2606.28116); called "Mechanism-driven monitors" here until Nov 17, 2026) | A paper, not a product: the spectrum of attention weight updates and per-token routing entropy as early warnings. It argues against router weight similarity, which this row used to list (corrected Nov 17, 2026 from the full paper, p. 3); no released code was confirmed | The closest published work to the rules deferred until after Gate 1 | arXiv |
| SCOUT, as `lm-resiliency` (added Nov 17, 2026) | Open source, BSD 3-Clause, cloned beside this repo. Localises hangs, stragglers and silent data corruption to a rank by strict majority among equivalent replicas, with an out-of-band observer and replay of sampled layers. Its README says "less than 1% amortized overhead"; the paper says it "does not measure end-to-end throughput and resource overhead" (p. 13) | No per-layer statistics, routing, attention or gradient checks. The closest tool on multi-GPU failures, and it covers corruption in a gradient, which `replica_drift` cannot see | [arXiv 2608.11034](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.11034); `lm-resiliency/README.md` |

What it means for the plan: every tracker now hands an agent its data through an MCP server, while TensorQuill hands over a file. That is enough for a coding agent on the same machine, and it needs no service. But the file has to be one an agent can read (Decisions: "Plan and implementation reviewed", the agent interface).

### Weeks 2–3: what must the Rune app do first? (Gate 1)

6. Generate 6 unseen synthetic MoE and dense fixtures using `experiments/moe-bakeoff/generate.py` wired through `tensorquill.build_evidence` and `export_rerun`, varying incident steps, layers, and experts. *Done Nov 15, 2026.*
7. Pre-register 6 timed diagnosis questions (each requiring identifying the first failing step, layer, and failure kind/expert IDs) that neither default screen answers without interaction.
8. Run three arms across all 6 fixtures using fresh coding-agent sessions (same model and system prompt, 3 trials per fixture per arm): (A) stock Rerun 0.43.2 with the bake-off blueprint + `evidence.json`, (B) the Rune app prototype + `evidence.json`, and (C) `evidence.json` alone. Also time the author across counterbalanced fixtures on (A) vs. (B).
9. **Gate 1 checklist:**
   - [ ] Accuracy across the 6 fixtures is within ±5 percentage points between arms (or higher in Arm B).
   - [ ] Arm B cuts median agent tool calls or median human time-to-correct-answer by **≥2.0×** relative to Arm A.
   - *Decision rule (changed Nov 14, 2026):* the Rune app is built either way (Step 22). The trial decides its order of work: the interactions that cut time or tool calls most come first, and the release ships with Rerun as the viewer until the Rune app reaches parity.
10. Steps 10a and 10b are done.

### Weeks 4–7: capture, stream, and detect on a real run (Gate 2)

11. Complete Tier 0 capture and the opt-in Arrow Flight sender (`sdk/src/tensorquill/flight.py`).
12. Complete Tier 1 capture: run `sdk/examples/replica_check.py` on two CUDA GPUs over NCCL.
13. Wire the compiler gate (`gpu_check.py`, `test_compile.py`, `test_probe.py`) into `.github/workflows/ci.yml`. Note: in `moe_dogfood.py`, compiling two separate benchmark models with different hook sets in one process causes one expected Dynamo recompile when switching models; a single model with TensorQuill attached must have **zero** recompiles after warmup.
14. Build the Rust ingest server (`server/`, binary `tensorquill-server`) per the [Ingest server specification](#ingest-server-and-local-first-streaming) above, and run the 64-rank Python-vs-Rust detector benchmark.
15. Verify every incident detector kind (seventeen when this step was written, thirty-two since Nov 16, 2026) in `sdk/src/tensorquill/evidence.py` on both `.jsonl` and server-written `part-*.parquet` inputs.
16. Run the Tier 2 probe pass on a CUDA GPU via `sdk/examples/dense_outlier_check.py` and `sdk/examples/moe_dogfood.py --probe-every 1000`, recording per-probe ms and JSON/Parquet byte sizes.
17. **Gate 2 checklist (pass all four to proceed; the heading said five until Nov 16, 2026, with the same four boxes):**
    - [ ] **Overhead:** `capture="routers"` adds **≤1.0%** median ms/step against capture off across 3 paired 200-step rounds (50 warmup steps) on the dogfood GPU (verified on RTX PRO 6000 at +0.02% to +0.78%; A100 profiled and either ≤1.0% or documented host-sync bound).
    - [ ] **Compiler gate:** `gpu_check.py` and `test_compile.py` report `0` graph breaks, `0` recompiles after warmup, and `0` CUDA-graph skips under `mode="reduce-overhead"`.
    - [ ] **Seeded failures:** 100% of seeded cases caught within **±1 flush/check interval** on the seeded layer/parameter: Granite layer-12 routing collapse (`moe_dogfood.py --collapse-layer 12`), SmolLM2 block-8 100× outlier (`dense_outlier_check.py`), stalled weights and gradient spike (`seeded_failures.py`, built Nov 15, 2026: passes on the CPU toy, the bf16 and ×100 cases included since the per-parameter rules of Nov 16, 2026; not yet run on a GPU), and 2-GPU NCCL weight drift + sign flip (`replica_check.py`).
    - [ ] **Ingest reliability:** stopping `tensorquill-server` mid-run for 200 steps and restarting loses `0` steps, produces `0` duplicate steps in Parquet, and keeps sender memory growth `<8 MiB`.
    - *Cut order if a criterion fails after one extra week:* if `capture="full"` or `probe=` exceeds budget, keep both opt-in; the detector port to Rust was decided by the 64-rank benchmark on Nov 15, 2026 and is done.

### Weeks 8–10: evidence for engineers and agents (Gate 3)

18. Verify `python -m tensorquill` exporting both local `.jsonl` and server `part-*.parquet` runs to `.rrd` + blueprint.
19. Build `sdk/src/tensorquill/screenshots.py` writing headless PNGs and `.meta.json` (`requested_step`, `sample_step`).
20. Verify headless Linux rendering in an Ubuntu container with `mesa-vulkan-drivers` (`lavapipe`) and `libvulkan1`: two repeat renders of the same `.rrd` and step must produce byte-identical PNGs.
21. *Postponed unless ≥3 engineers join:* MLflow scalar-only importer (`sdk/src/tensorquill/mlflow_import.py`).
22. Build TensorQuill's own Rune app in `viewer/`, in the order Gate 1 set; Rerun stays the viewer until it reaches parity. *Postponed Nov 16, 2026 (Decisions: "Rune viewer postponed"); not part of the first release.*
23. **Gate 3 checklist (pass both to proceed):**
    - [ ] Across ≥8 seeded failure runs (covering MoE collapse, starved experts, frozen bf16 norms, gradient spike, activation outlier, and 2-GPU replica drift), a coding agent given TensorQuill `evidence.json` + Rerun screenshots identifies the exact failing step, layer/parameter, and failure kind in **≥85%** of trials, beating the same agent given scalar loss + total grad-norm curves alone by **≥30 percentage points**.
    - [ ] TensorQuill has run on ≥1 of the author's real training jobs and either surfaced a real anomaly or completed ≥1,000 steps with zero false-positive incidents.
    - *Cut order if blocked:* if headless PNGs do not improve agent accuracy over `evidence.json` alone, make screenshot generation optional for agents and keep Rerun for human inspection.

### Weeks 11–12: public release (Gate 4)

24. Publish `tensorquill` and `tensorquill-server` on PyPI with a first real release, the server as wheels (crates.io was dropped on Nov 17, 2026; Decisions: "Packaging and the release plan"; the code was renamed Nov 14, 2026), add `LICENSE-APACHE` and `LICENSE-MIT` (`Apache-2.0 OR MIT`), and publish the SDK, detectors, exporter, and `tensorquill-server` binary with the MoE bake-off as a self-contained demo (no external Rune checkout required). The open-source SDK and server include **zero outbound telemetry**.
25. Track adoption through public signals that require no SDK phone-home: GitHub stars, issues, PRs, PyPI download stats, and direct conversations with users who open issues or discussions.
26. Measure on the dogfood runs: (a) Parquet storage bytes per 1,000 steps per rank, (b) `tensorquill-server` CPU seconds and peak RSS per rank, and (c) headless screenshot render seconds per incident. (Diagnosis runs on the user's own coding agent, so TensorQuill incurs zero LLM token cost.)
27. **Gate 4 checklist:**
    - [ ] Package and `tensorquill-server` install cleanly in a fresh environment and pass the smoke test suite.
    - [ ] ≥3 outside engineers or teams run the SDK on their own jobs and report results via GitHub issues/discussions, with ≥2 expressing willingness to pay for managed multi-node ingest and alerting at a stated monthly price before any hosted control plane is built.

## Free and paid

Everything that runs on the user's own machine or cluster is free and open source. The paid tier, built only if Gate 4 passes, is managed multi-tenant hosting and alerting.

- **Free (`Apache-2.0 OR MIT`):** the capture SDK, opt-in Flight sender, self-hosted `tensorquill-server`, detectors, and Rerun evidence exporter, with zero telemetry.
- **Paid, \$250–500 per seat per month (unvalidated, post-Gate 4):** managed `tensorquill-server` cloud ingest, object-store retention, Slack/PagerDuty incident alerts, and automated PR drafts.
- **Self-hosted enterprise:** managed deployment, SSO/RBAC, and SLA inside the customer's own cloud account.

Cost per monitored run (Parquet storage bytes, `tensorquill-server` CPU/RAM, and headless screenshot rendering; zero LLM calls since the customer's coding agent diagnoses incidents) is measured in Step 26 before setting price.

## Risks

The biggest risk is commercial, not technical: teams may value the tool and still not pay for hosted monitoring.

| Risk | Effect | Mitigation |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Nobody pays for hosted monitoring | No revenue | Release the SDK and self-hosted `tensorquill-server` free first; build a multi-tenant hosted cloud only if Gate 4 shows ≥2 outside teams willing to pay |
| ARIA, Pluto, or TrainCheck adds compiled per-layer capture and MoE/attention detectors | The gap closes | Re-check all competitors at Gate 0 and before Gate 4; stay tracker- and cloud-neutral; lead with MoE, attention, cross-GPU drift, and zero-break `torch.compile` support |
| Trackers add per-layer views | The wedge narrows | Stay tracker-neutral; lead with MoE, attention, and `torch.compile` capture that scalar trackers lack |
| Rerun's extension interfaces change | The interim export breaks | Pin `rerun-sdk==0.43.2`; only the `.rrd` exporter depends on Rerun |
| The viewer depends on Rune, itself under heavy development | Rune's schedule delays the viewer, or a Rune change breaks it | Keep the Rerun export as a working fallback until parity; pin a Rune commit in `viewer/`; keep the app's input to `evidence.json` and JSONL so it can move to another renderer |
| Hooks stall training or break `torch.compile` | Users switch the SDK off | Overhead gate ≤1% for default `capture="routers"`, CI gate with `error_on_recompile` and `graph_break == 0`, and bounded writer queue (`WRITER_QUEUE = 8`) |
| Agents misread the pictures | Wrong diagnoses, lost trust | Every number travels in `evidence.json` (`schema_version: 1`); screenshots include `.meta.json` with `sample_step`; score on Gate 3 |
| Coding-agent clients handle tool images differently | The picture does not reach the model or the user | Test saved PNGs with target agent clients and always supply `evidence.json` alongside |
| Tensor data leaves the cluster | Enterprise deals fall through | Local `.jsonl` by default (zero network calls); opt-in Flight sender targets self-hosted `tensorquill-server` on the user's own cluster |
| W&B's terms restrict competing products and credential sharing | A W&B importer or hosted W&B sync breaches the customer's contract | Read only `wandb.run.id` in-process; require legal review before anything touches a customer's W&B API key |

## Unproven and open decisions

State as of Nov 16, 2026.
- **Recording.** The synthetic MoE bake-off verified recording read-back and headless rendering.
- **Capture on GPUs.** Capture runs on a real MoE model (Granite) and a dense one (SmolLM2-360M) on single-GPU RTX PRO 6000 and A100 instances.
  - Router-only overhead under the gate protocol is +0.70% on the RTX PRO 6000. The A100 was profiled at +0.41% to +0.95%, but in 100-step rounds rather than the gate protocol.
  - Full capture measured +4.11% on Granite and +3.72% on SmolLM2 on the RTX PRO 6000.
- **Detection.** The seeded Granite layer-12 collapse and the SmolLM2 block-8 outlier were found at their steps.
- **Detectors.** Thirty-two incident kinds are implemented in `evidence.py` (fifteen added Nov 16, 2026, CPU-tested), and twenty-eight also run in the Rust server (counted again Nov 18, 2026, after `update_spike`: thirty-three kinds, twenty-nine of them in the server: 26 per-rank kinds and 3 from its status). The server was timed under 64 live streams and across a restart. Since Nov 16, 2026 gradient spikes and stalled weights are also judged per parameter, checked against the recorded GPU captures.
- **Unmeasured:**
  - multi-GPU NCCL capture;
  - the `seeded_failures.py` cases on a GPU, and with them the per-parameter rules on a fresh GPU run;
  - Linux `lavapipe` rendering;
  - a real object store (S3 ran only against an emulator, and GCS has never run);
  - real failures on the author's own jobs;
  - coding-agent diagnosis accuracy.

**Unproven**

- Which Rune-app interactions (sorting, layer selection, linked table) cut diagnosis time or agent tool calls most in Gate 1.
- Whether `capture="routers"` on the A100 can be brought under 1% once profiled with `profile_overhead.py`, and whether `capture="full"` (+4.15%) needs optional gradient-norm step sampling.
- Whether the detector thresholds (5× spike, 3 steps in a row for one parameter, 0.6 diversity drop, 10% starved share, 3 unchanged weight checks) hold on models other than Granite and SmolLM2-360M without false alarms.
- Whether the hooks keep CUDA graphs on real models that support CUDA graphs (verified on the toy MoE; Granite's `grouped_mm` fails CUDA-graph capture even without TensorQuill).
- Whether the cross-GPU drift tolerance of `1e-6` holds on healthy multi-GPU NCCL runs without false positives from floating-point nondeterminism.
- Whether a coding agent with TensorQuill `evidence.json` and Rerun screenshots beats scalar-only tracker data by ≥30 percentage points in Gate 3.
- Whether outside teams want and will pay for hosted monitoring (Gate 4).

**Decisions**

- [x] **Product and package name (decided Nov 14, 2026): TensorQuill, `tensorquill`.** Checked Nov 14, 2026: free on PyPI and TestPyPI (including the `tensor-quill` spelling PyPI treats as the same), on crates.io (`tensorquill` and `tensorquill-server`), on npm, and as a GitHub handle; no GitHub repository uses the name. Formerly TensorLens, a placeholder: `tensorlens` is taken on PyPI and GitHub by unrelated projects. Rejected on the way: `gradpulse`, because PyPI already has a `gradpulse` (Pure State Labs, a PyTorch quantum pulse optimizer, since Jun 2026); and `gradsight`, chosen briefly the same day, which is free on PyPI and crates.io but whose GitHub handle belongs to an empty organisation created Jan 29, 2026. Done Nov 14, 2026: the GitHub organisation `tensorquill` (created by the author), and the rename in code and docs: package `tensorquill`, command `tensorquill`, server crate and binary `tensorquill-server`, output folder `tensorquill-out/`, environment variables `TENSORQUILL_*`, with no compatibility aliases for the old names, since nobody outside this repository used them. No record field or store path carried the name, so stored data needs no migration and `schema_version` stays 1; only `.rrd` exports change application ID. Not done: the first PyPI and crates.io uploads, which only the author's accounts can make, and moving this repository into the organisation.
- [ ] **Author's dogfood job, tracker, and multi-GPU setup** (Gate 0).
- [x] **Keeping a resumed run's repeated steps (decided and built Nov 14, 2026).** Each `tensorquill.init` stamps `attempt`, its start time in Unix milliseconds, on every record it writes. The server deduplicates on `(run_id, rank, attempt, step)` and writes under `rank=<rank>/attempt=<attempt>/`. The attempt lives on the record, not on the sender or the stream: a later process that resends old lines from the same file (a lost `.offset`, `send_file`) presents their original attempt, so they are still recognised as stored. Readers (`resolve_attempts`, applied by `load_records` and `build_evidence`) treat a later attempt that starts at step S as a rollback: earlier attempts' records at step S or later are left out, including steps the new attempt has not reached yet. `repeated_steps` still fires for a later attempt that starts the numbering over, which is the forgotten `start_step=` case, and for a step written twice within one attempt.
