Records and senders without `attempt` are attempt 0. This replaced the `(run_id, rank, step)` rule; no store written under the old layout is migrated, since none exists outside tests.
- [x] **Live detection runs in Rust inside the server; Python stays the reference (decided Nov 15, 2026; built the same day, in two slices).** The benchmark below put the Python detectors at 13 times the budget even with state kept, and reading 64 parts back costs about 830 ms before any detector runs, so faster Python alone could not meet it. The server already holds each batch in Arrow.
  - *Built, first slice.* `server/src/detect.rs` keeps per-rank state for `moe_imbalance`, `starved_experts`, `grad_spike`, `activation_spike`, `activation_drift` and `dead_activations`, including the logged-scalar context Python attaches to an incident.
  - *Built, second slice: fourteen of seventeen kinds in the server, three left to Python on purpose.* Added to `detect.rs`: `stalled_weights`, `loss_scale_trouble`, `non_finite`, `replica_drift`, `repeated_steps` and `resume_lr_change`. Added to `watchdog.rs`: `job_stalled` and `rank_silent`, as an `incidents` list in `watchdog.json`. They are not in `incidents.json` because that file is rewritten only when a part is stored, and a stall is when none is. `collective_hang`, `collective_desync` and `traincheck_violation` are not ported: PyTorch's analyzer reads Flight Recorder pickles on the training nodes and TrainCheck writes its results where it ran, so the server has neither (checked in the sibling PyTorch checkout: `torch/distributed/flight_recorder/components/loader.py` loads each rank's dump with `pickle.load` from a local directory, and `collectives.py` calls that analyzer's `build_db`).
  - *Attempts: every new attempt is a replay, and the replay reads rolled-back rows.* `repeated_steps` and `resume_lr_change` compare a new attempt with the rows it replaced and need each step's learning rate. Keeping that history for the life of the server would grow with the run, so it is held only during a replay, and a new attempt always triggers one (before, only an attempt that went back did). Between replays the one thing that can change is a restarted numbering writing more steps an earlier attempt had; the steps still ahead of it are kept for that. An incident of these two kinds names a step whose row may be written later by a newer attempt; its logged scalars are attached when that row is read.
  - *`not_checked` has Python's shape.* The list in `incidents.json` was kind names; it is now the same list of `{check, reason, ...}` entries as in `evidence.json`, with the two sources the server lacks and each resume that could not be compared. Nothing read the old shape.
  - *Three differences from Python by design.* NaN and Inf are `null`; a value that does not fit its column and travels in `extra_json` reads as absent (only `optimizer_ownership`, which has no column, is read from there, and only on the row where a stall is first reported); a step stored twice within one attempt is not looked for, because the server's watermark never stores one.
  - *Live evidence shape.* `runs/<run_id>/rank=<rank>/incidents.json`, rewritten after each stored part: the incidents as `build_evidence` shapes them, `last_step`, the kinds checked and not checked, the thresholds, `moe_flagged` (samples flagged and the last step, since an incident only names the first) and `rebuilds`. No per-step samples: at 10,000 steps Python's evidence file was 86 MB and took 878 ms to write, over budget by itself; the samples are already in Parquet.
  - *Only stored rows are judged, after the acknowledgement.* A crash can then never leave the incidents ahead of the store. A detector error or panic is logged and never fails a flush.
  - *One mechanism for restart and resume: a full replay of the rank's stored parts* with `resolve_attempts`' rules, on the first part after a start and whenever a part is not a plain continuation. Replaying only the last window, as first proposed, would be wrong: a held top-k baseline can be arbitrarily old, and first incidents and counts reach back to the start.
  - *Parity rule: the state after any prefix equals `build_evidence` on that prefix.* Python looks ahead over a finished list in three places; the stream matches it by restarting the gradient state at the first record with `clip_grad_norm`, and differs only for hand-made records whose `moe_top_k` changes in mid-run. `sdk/tests/test_server_detectors.py` (9 tests after the second slice, in the CI server job; 22 deliberate changes to the Rust rules each fail them or the watchdog end-to-end tests; one first survived, a learning-rate history that was not rolled back, and one branch was first unreached, a repeated step counted twice, and each got its own case) checks it through the real binary after 16 stretches of a 300-step run with ten kinds, after each of 12 stretches of a nine-attempt run, on seven short runs for single rules, across a restart inside a 140-step collapse, across two resumes that roll an incident back, on a top-k collapse before a baseline exists and dead experts with none overloaded, and on the seven Gate 1 fixtures (eight changes to the first slice's rules were tried the same way before). A NaN or Inf is `null` in the server's file.
  - *Measured* (M1 Pro, one thread, the benchmark's synthetic healthy store, 64 ranks × 2,000 steps, 50-step flushes, median of 40): detectors 54 ms per flush of 64 ranks (17 µs per step, against Python's 693 µs for all kinds), of which about 33 ms is decoding `layers_json` and `moe_stats_json`; writing the Parquet parts 138 ms; the incidents files 13 ms; about 205 ms together against 250 ms on this healthy run, and about 287 ms if no shortcut applied (136 ms of detectors), which is over. The pass line was set at 80 ms before measuring. The first version took 189 ms (on a busier machine, so roughly); exact shortcuts (no median when a window's lowest or highest value rules a hit out) brought it to 54 ms, and 136 ms is the cost when no shortcut applies. A rank's replay from 2,000 steps takes 73 ms, all 64 at once 0.94 s.
  - *Measured, second slice* (same machine and store): 56 ms per flush (worst 68) for twelve kinds, so the six added cost about 2 ms, not the 26 ms guessed from Python's proportions; replay 75 ms for one rank and 0.98 s for all 64. The store checks weights and replicas once in 50 steps and has no loss scaler, so a run that checks weights on every step was not timed.
  - *The watcher reads the server's report, and only when it is current (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).* `watch` on a rank's folder in a server's store takes its incidents from `incidents.json` and from the status's `incidents`, reads no part, and still adds Flight Recorder and TrainCheck findings itself. `build_evidence` and the one-shot command are unchanged and stay pure Python: they are the reference the parity tests compare the server with, and they need the parts anyway for per-step samples. The report has no clock, so it is dated against the store: current means its `attempt` and `last_step` equal the newest stored part's, read from the part's path (`read_server_report`); the newest attempt only, because after a resume from an earlier step an older attempt's parts reach further. An empty list counts as "no incidents" only then. Missing, unusable (another run or rank, fewer kinds, ahead of the store), or other thresholds: the detectors run here at once. Behind or unreadable: the watcher waits 30 s (`--report-grace`) counted from the last look that found the report current, not from the newest part, so a report that never catches up cannot hide behind parts that keep arriving; then it is stale and the detectors run here. A watcher that has never seen a current report does not wait. The status is dated by its own `written_at_ms`. The server wrote a status only when its verdict changed, so a settled stall would have looked exactly like a dead server after a minute (caught in review before it shipped, and the stall test now fails on the old server); it now rewrites an unfinished run's status at least every third look (`status_refresh`, 30 s by default, carried in the file as `refresh_seconds`). Older than twice that while claiming a running server, the summary says nothing is watching for stalls (a killed server leaves "active" behind; a restarted one takes the run up from that status and rewrites it at once, see the Decisions entry on S3 and the watchdog across a restart); a status without `refresh_seconds` is from an older server and its age is not judged; `server_stopped` is said in words; a finished run is left alone. The evidence says what happened under a new optional `detection` key (still `schema_version: 1`), and evidence from a report has no `samples`, `weight_trends` or router sizes. `--recompute` ignores the report. MLflow and W&B were read first: both keep a server-side summary next to the full history and serve it without recomputing (`SqlLatestMetric` / `_update_latest_metrics_if_necessary` in MLflow's `sqlalchemy_store.py`; `summaryMetrics` beside `history()` in `wandb/apis/public/runs.py`); that is the same split. Dating the summary against the store is added here, since this reader has only files and no server to ask. `sdk/tests/test_server_reports.py` (12 tests) produces each state with the real binary: a healthy run, `--no-detect`, a restart without detection inside and after the grace, a kill, a kill between a part and its report (simulated by putting the earlier report back), a clean stop, a stall with no part arriving, a resume from an earlier step, and reports that cannot be used. Twenty-two deliberate changes to the reader's rules each fail it; three first survived (a stall dropped from the status, a write on every look, a finished run called unwatched) and got cases. Found on the way: a NaN logged value, `null` in the server's file, printed as "None"; it now prints "not finite". Limits: a late part of an older attempt that was not judged goes unnoticed; stall incidents from the status carry no logged scalars; one rank per command unless `--rank all` is used (see the Decisions entry on it).
  - *Measured under 64 live streams (Nov 15, 2026).* `server/examples/live_streams.rs`, a Rust client (64 Python senders would have measured Python), one `DoPut` stream per rank against the release server on the same 8-core machine, replaying the benchmark store with all ranks' flushes coinciding. Flat out: 32,000 to 37,800 steps/s with detectors (18 runs), 44,700 to 48,000 without, against the 985 steps/s that 64 ranks at Granite's 65 ms per step produce. At Granite's pace: sent to acknowledged 54 ms median, 100 ms p99, 132 ms worst; 15.2 s of CPU in 130 s, 12% of a core. The report followed the acknowledgement by 0.1 ms at the median and 50 ms at worst (77 ms at 10,000 steps). Against the 250 ms budget: met as wall time (all 64 simultaneous flushes within 132 ms), not as CPU on one core: 358 ms per flush of 64 ranks, 253 ms of it receiving, decoding and storing (`--no-detect`) and about 105 ms the detectors and incidents files (69 ms when timed alone). The earlier single-thread figure of 210 ms left out receiving and decoding Flight. Detector memory: about 100 MB for 64 ranks, 1.5 to 2 MB a rank (peak 360 to 415 MB against 260 to 300 MB), not the 1 MB estimated. A restart with 10,000 steps stored: each rank's first part acknowledged in 100 to 170 ms (median), then all 64 ranks rebuilt at once in 6.1 to 7.0 s, during which each rank's next part waits for its acknowledgement; a new attempt on all 64 ranks costs the same. After five restarts and three new attempts the reports of the two ranks compared equal `build_evidence` on the store (10,800 steps, four attempts), and a watcher's look took 2.0 ms where `build_evidence` took 12.0 s. Once, in the first of 19 flat-out runs and on a machine still busy from a build, one stream ended with a broken pipe and no server log line; it did not recur and is unexplained (not the quota: that refusal arrives as `ResourceExhausted`). 64 coinciding 50-step parts of this store hold 85 MiB of the 128 MiB quota, so about 90 such ranks flushing together would fill it; not tried.
  - *Not measured or not done.* The replay's own memory (every step number and learning rate of the rank, under 100 bytes a step by its layout); more than 64 ranks; a network between sender and server; TLS under load; a run with incidents under load; real values; S3 or GCS; a part arriving for an older attempt after a newer one (handled by the replay rule, not tested through the sender); rebuilds beyond 10,800 steps (about a minute for 64 ranks of 100,000 by the slope, past the watcher's default wait). By default a part is written every 500 steps or 10 seconds, so an incident appears about 10 seconds after its step arrives at the latest. Megatron-LM's `router_diagnostics.py`, arrow-rs's `MapArray` and DataFusion's median were read first; none has a sliding median or a collapse rule to borrow.
- [x] **Detector benchmark on 64 ranks, Python half (measured Nov 15, 2026; Step 14).** `sdk/examples/benchmark_detectors.py` (one command, about 6 minutes and 3 GB of temporary disk; `--quick` about 30 s) builds a synthetic healthy 64-rank store shaped like the Granite benchmark (24 blocks, 32 experts, top-8, 219 parameters; record shapes from a real `tensorquill.init` capture; 50-step Zstd parts in the server's layout) and times `evidence.py` on it. Apple M1 Pro, 8 cores, 16 GB, macOS, no GPU, other work at load 3–6; repeated full runs agree within about 5%.
  - *Measured.* A `--watch` update for one rank (poll, rebuild, write) takes 395 ms at 500 steps, 1.63 s at 2,000 and 8.07 s at 10,000, against 250 ms, because every update reruns the detectors over the whole run: about 0.72 ms of detector time per step and 0.08 ms of JSON write. At the plan's 1,000-step protocol, about 0.8 s per rank. All 64 ranks in turn at 2,000 steps took 105 s. Peak memory: 1.15 GB for one rank's 10,000 steps.
  - *Projected from the measured slope, not run.* A worker that kept the detectors' state between flushes would still pay one 50-step part read (13.6 ms) and 50 steps of detectors (35.9 ms) per rank: about 50 ms, so 3.2 s for 64 ranks, 12.7 times the budget and 105% of one core at 65 ms/step.
  - *Shape matters.* A 2-block, 8-expert, top-1 model (21 parameters) costs about 17 times less per step: an update at 2,000 steps is 100 ms and the stateful floor for 64 ranks is 343 ms (1.4 times the budget, 17% of a core).
  - *Not measured.* The Rust arm, vectorised Python, real instead of synthetic values, a GPU job, a server writing the parts live.
  - *By the plan's rule* (over 250 ms per 50-step flush across 64 ranks, or over one core) the Python worker is over budget at Granite's shape in every reading, and keeping state between flushes alone does not bring it under. The next step has to cut the per-step cost by at least 13 times (the plan's in-server Rust pass over incoming `RecordBatch`es, or a vectorised rewrite) or accept the limit for small models and few ranks. There is no hot spot to tune: at 2,000 steps the activation checks are 27% of a build, gradient spikes 15%, the starved-expert window 15%, the non-finite scan 13% and the top-k overload rule 12%, all per-step loops with rolling medians. This is open; nothing is started.
  - *Fixed on the way.* Reading one rank from a directory of 64 opened every rank's parts (a poll took 1.0–1.5 s and 495 MB for 64 ranks × 100 steps, about 50 GB extrapolated to 10,000 steps); it now takes 13–21 ms and 32 MB, because `load_records(dir, rank=)` and the `--watch` tail skip the `rank=<other>` folders. The output is identical for every store the server writes: the server refuses a row whose rank differs from its stream's (`ingest.rs`), so a `rank=<n>` folder holds only rank `n`. It differs only for a hand-built store that breaks this, which is documented and tested. The activation-drift baselines are also computed once per module (a 10,000-step build went from 9.2 s to 7.2 s, identical output). 29 golden evidence files (healthy, 12 faulted synthetic runs covering 12 incident kinds, the real toy-MoE capture, Parquet loads and tails) are byte-identical before and after.
- [x] **Two ranks streaming end to end (tested on CPU Nov 15, 2026).** `sdk/tests/test_two_ranks_e2e.py` (7 tests, about 45 s, in the CI server job) runs two real Python processes with `RANK` and `WORLD_SIZE=2`, one `run_id` and a seeded toy MoE, streaming to one real `tensorquill-server`. Checked: both ranks' Parquet equals their local files; `world_size` reaches `watchdog.json` from either rank's batches; both finish → `finished`; one rank SIGKILLed → `lost` after the floor, `degraded`, the survivor `active`, `rank_silent` with `lost_connection`, and the killed rank's Parquet a prefix of its file with no gap or repeat; a rank that never starts is named from `world_size` (`ranks_never_connected`) and the run ends `degraded`; a server SIGKILL and restart under two streaming ranks loses and repeats nothing; the CLI's `--rank 0` and `--rank 1` build each rank's own evidence. It passed three times in a row and once under full CPU load. It found no bug in the server or the sender, and two elsewhere, both fixed the same day: (1) `build_evidence` on a run directory merged every rank's records (215 samples for 120 + 95 steps, repeated step numbers, and incidents that belong to neither rank), so it takes `rank=` and raises `ValueError` when several ranks are present and none is given; (2) ranks that called `init` without `run_id=` before `init_process_group` each generated their own ID, also under `TORCHELASTIC_RUN_ID=none`, which plain `torchrun` sets, and wrote one run per rank, each missing the other, with no warning. `init` now warns. The warning does not make the ranks agree: there is no value for them to share before the process group exists, so they pass `run_id=` or call `init` afterwards. Noted, not asserted: when one rank is killed and the survivor finishes, the summary is `stalled` and says "after step 209", the finished rank's last step, because the test's ranks share no collectives; a real survivor would hang at its next collective. Not tested: more than two ranks streaming at once, a remote server, a GPU job.
- [x] **Gate 1 fixtures come from the SDK's own pipeline (decided and built Nov 15, 2026; Step 6).** `experiments/moe-bakeoff/generate.py` makes every fixture through `JsonlSink` → `load_records` → `build_evidence` → `export_rerun`, with no detector rule, color or table of its own. It writes the visible `seeded` fixture (unchanged: the same counts, diversity, overloaded and dead lists as the old generator, compared sample by sample) and six unseen ones, `unseen-1` to `unseen-6`: four MoE (top-1 and top-4 routers, 32–128 experts) and two dense (a gradient spike and an activation outlier). The folder held one fixture, not six; the other six are new. Each planted failure is written out in the script, not read from the detectors, and goes to `artifacts/answer-key.json`, outside the fixture folders that participants and agents get; fixture names give nothing away. `experiments/moe-bakeoff/test_fixtures.py` (37 tests, in the CI extras job; in the experiment folder because the step was confined to it): each fixture has exactly one incident, of the planted kind, at the planted step with the planted layer and experts or parameter; records before the plant give none; `python -m tensorquill <name>.jsonl --rerun` gives a byte-identical `evidence.json` and the same recording contents (the `.rrd` bytes differ between runs). The trial (Steps 7–9) is pending, the Docker build is unverified, and the Rune inspector still bakes in the seeded stats and has no dense view. The fixtures exposed two detector bugs. The top-k baseline used to absorb flagged samples, so layer 11’s persistent collapse in `unseen-3` lost its overload labels by step 520. It now keeps experts 9–14 flagged through the final sample at step 600. The dead-expert fallback also used to lower the overload bar for top-1 and top-2. Those routers now always use the calibrated share threshold, and `unseen-4` tests a 34% expert beside 40 dead experts. For top-3 and higher, relative share is used only for independently flagged dead-expert events during baseline warm-up. Read from the code, not observed: `export_rerun` fixes the routing-share color range at 0–0.36 and uses one application ID for every recording.
- [x] **Phase-aware watchdog: heartbeats, severity and measured pauses (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** An absence of steps is not a hang: evaluation and checkpoints stop stepping too. Adopted defaults, all uncalibrated starting values and none verified safe: silence floor 300 s and 5 times the rank's own time between batches (`--silent-seconds`, `--silent-factor`), `warning` from that threshold and `critical` after 900 s (`--critical-seconds`). A thread of its own in the training process (`Heartbeat`, `init(heartbeat_seconds=15)`, only with `endpoint=`) calls a new `heartbeat` Flight action every 15 s, and at once on a phase change, with the rank's phase. `session.set_phase(name)` and `with session.phase("eval"):` set it; the Lightning callback marks validation, and the Hugging Face callback marks evaluation and checkpoints from `control.should_evaluate` and `should_save` (it relies on the flow callback running first, as it does by default). Rank states added: `paused` (no step, a phase other than `train` declared, heartbeats continuing: no alert) and `unresponsive` (no step and the heartbeats stopped too: the whole process is frozen or gone with its connection open; a SIGSTOPped child was tested). A quiet rank in `train` with live heartbeats, or with no heartbeats at all (the phase is unknown), is `silent`, and the incident's `assessment` is `suspected_hang`; it becomes `confirmed_hang` only when PyTorch's Flight Recorder dump shows a stuck collective (`corroborated_by`). A connection that dropped and did not return is `lost_connection`. The server measures how long each non-training phase lasted and writes `pauses` and `recommended_silent_seconds` (twice the longest pause, never below the floor) into `watchdog.json`; the summary text says what threshold would have tolerated them. Nothing in TensorQuill blocks or terminates training: this only reports. Refinements after review: a rank that has connected but sent no batch yet is `starting`, not stopped (the first batch carries `every` steps, so a slow job sends it late); the time to the first batch seeds the rank's cadence, so a slow job is not called silent before its second batch; `critical` comes after `--critical-seconds` or three of the rank's own silence thresholds, whichever is longer, so a slow rank still gets a `warning` first. A pause is bounded: the heartbeat carries a work counter (steps committed plus `session.tick()` calls, which the Lightning callback makes once per validation batch and the Hugging Face callback once per prediction step), a pause whose counter keeps moving is not limited, one with no sign of work is a suspected hang after `--pause-seconds` (default 1800), and one whose counter moved and then stopped is stuck. A committed training step always ends a pause, which also clears a phase a callback left behind. `confirmed_hang` needs a Flight Recorder dump that is complete and newer than the last recorded step's `ts`; an old or partial dump leaves it `suspected_hang`. `watch` rebuilds when severity, phase or the measured pauses change. An incident with only never-connected ranks is `ranks_never_connected`. The quiet time in the text is "at least", since the file is written only on change. A real Hugging Face `Trainer` with `eval_steps` and `save_steps` produced the phase sequence eval, train, eval, checkpoint, train, eval, checkpoint, train. Limits: the heartbeat thread is in the training process, so it is independent of the step hooks but not of the process (a separate sidecar process is not built): a main thread hung inside NCCL keeps the heartbeats going, which is `silent` in `train`, the same `suspected_hang` as an unknown phase. Lightning checkpoints are not marked. The Hugging Face hook cannot see a pause its flow callback did not announce.
- [x] **TrainCheck: ingest, do not embed (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** TrainCheck (sibling checkout at `7a62cbb`) wraps a script with `traincheck-collect`, infers invariants from a clean reference run, and checks a target run; `traincheck-check` writes `violations_summary.json` (`first_violation_step`, `distinct_invariants_violated`, and per violation `display_name`, `relation_type`, `first_step`, `last_step`, `occurrences`). Its roadmap still lists better distributed support as near-term, and it needs a reference run TensorQuill does not. `sdk/src/tensorquill/invariants.py` reads that file (or a results directory holding one per trace) into one `traincheck_violation` incident, at TrainCheck's first violation step, which is its own iteration count and matches TensorQuill's only without gradient accumulation. It is reached through `build_evidence(..., traincheck=path)` and `--traincheck`; an unreadable or unrecognised file is listed under `not_checked`. Not tried: running TrainCheck and TensorQuill in one process (each patches PyTorch's API), so the job runs TensorQuill and TrainCheck is run as it documents. The format was read from TrainCheck's code and its own tests, not from a real run of it.
- [x] **Flight Recorder: read the dumps, do not rebuild the tracing (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** PyTorch already records every collective per rank, dumps the buffers when a collective times out, and ships an analyzer (`torch.distributed.flight_recorder`, read in the sibling PyTorch checkout at `7c84e46`). `sdk/src/tensorquill/collectives.py` runs that analyzer over a dump directory (`build_db`, with `--allow-incomplete-ranks`) and turns its first problem into `collective_hang` (a rank never entered the collective, which names the ranks to look at, or every rank entered and it did not complete, which points at the network, NCCL or a GPU) or `collective_desync` (ranks issued different collectives, with the analyzer's culprit message). Reached through `build_evidence(..., flight_recorder=dir)` and `python -m tensorquill --flight-recorder dir`. The incident's `step` is the last step TensorQuill recorded, since the dumps carry no training step. The analyzer exists from PyTorch 2.10; an older one, an unreadable directory or a changed database format is listed under `not_checked` with the reason, never an error, because PyTorch says the format may change. TensorQuill does not start PyTorch's debug server (experimental, trusted networks only, needs aiohttp and Jinja2). Each rank's buffer is a ring (2000 entries by default) and ranks dump at different moments, so a collective older than the newest first entry of any rank in its group cannot be judged and is left out (`ignored_before_buffer_start`), or the oldest entries would read as hangs. The wording says "at dump time", since a dump is a snapshot. The dumps are pickles: read only dumps your own job wrote. Verified only on synthetic dumps in the analyzer's own test format; a real NCCL hang has not been run.
- [x] **Silent-rank watchdog in the server (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** A hung job's in-process hooks stop producing records, so the check must not live there. `tensorquill-server` tracks, per `(run_id, rank)`, the last new step it kept and when (a resend of rows it already has is not progress), and how each stream ended (the sender's `close()` is a clean end; an error or a cut connection is not). Rank states: `active`, `silent` (stream open, no progress for longer than max(`--silent-seconds`, `--silent-factor` times the rank's median recent time between batches); a hang keeps its connection open, so this is what a hang looks like), `reconnecting`, `lost` (dropped and not back after the floor), `finished`. The run summary is `stalled` (no rank active, some silent or lost), `degraded` (some silent, lost or never connected while another is active) or `finished`. The SDK sends `world_size` in each batch's metadata (optional key; older servers ignore it) so ranks that never connected are named. The server rewrites `runs/<run_id>/watchdog.json` every `--watchdog-seconds` (default 10) with its own clock, and `build_evidence` turns it into `job_stalled` or `rank_silent`; `--watch` rebuilds when the verdict, a rank's state or the missing ranks change, since a hung job writes no records. Thresholds are uncalibrated (default floor 300 s) and the cause is never claimed: a hang, a long checkpoint or evaluation pause, a killed job and a dead sender look alike. A rank is called missing only once the run has been seen for longer than the floor, since ranks start a moment apart. The status is rewritten only when the summary, a rank's state, stream or last step, or the missing ranks change, and not again once the run is finished, so `written_at_ms` is the time of the last change and an S3 store sees no steady stream of writes. At a clean shutdown every run that is not finished is rewritten with `summary: server_stopped` (a finished run keeps its verdict); a server killed with SIGKILL leaves its last status, and the next server takes that status up and rewrites it at once, with the quiet times it had. Verified against the real server and sender by killing the training process (SIGKILL) and by exiting without `close()`: both read `dropped`, then `lost`, never `finished`. Limits: it sees the ranks that connected since the server started and, after a restart, those in the last status of each run that was neither finished nor abandoned (see the Decisions entry on S3 and the watchdog across a restart); it does not find a slow GPU (`ts` is the host clock and synchronous ranks wait for each other); and capturing on rank 0 only under DDP makes every other rank "never connected" after the floor, so capture on every rank, as the adapters do.
- [x] **S3 and GCS stores, and the watchdog across a restart (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** *Stores.* `--store s3://bucket/prefix` and `gs://bucket/prefix` are opened by `Storage::open`, which gives the builder the provider's own variables only (`AWS_*` for S3, `GOOGLE_*` for GCS): before, every environment variable was passed and the builders also take bare `token`, `region`, `endpoint` and `bucket`, so a stray `TOKEN` or `ENDPOINT` could configure the store. Credentials come from the variables, then what `object_store` implements (EC2 IMDSv2, ECS and EKS web-identity, GCE metadata, `GOOGLE_APPLICATION_CREDENTIALS`); shared credential files and `AWS_PROFILE` are not read. They are handed to the store and never printed (errors name variables, not values; tested, and the end-to-end test checks the server's output for a dummy secret). An S3-compatible service is `AWS_ENDPOINT_URL`, with `AWS_ALLOW_HTTP=true` for `http://` (the server says so when the flag is missing). Cloud stores are cargo features `s3` and `gcs`, **on by default**: the binary's job is S3, GCS or local disk, and tonic's TLS already brings rustls; switching them off saves 33 crates and 3.9 MB of 22.3 MB (22,262,720 to 18,367,216 bytes, 283 to 250 crates); `--no-default-features` is a local-only build that says so when given an `s3://` store. The server needs only `GetObject`, `PutObject` and `ListBucket` (`list` requests); it never deletes. *Object-store semantics checked.* A PUT replaces `incidents.json` and `watchdog.json` atomically on S3, GCS and local disk (`object_store` writes a temporary file and renames on disk); nothing emulates rename. S3 and GCS list and read strongly consistently, so the watermark read after a restart (one listing of the attempt and one 64 KiB suffix read of the newest part per rank, counted in a test) sees every part that was acknowledged; an S3-compatible service without read-after-write consistency would break that and is not supported. `list` has the trailing delimiter, so `rank=1` does not list `rank=10` (tested). The 128 MiB quota and the rule "acknowledge only after the PUT succeeded" are unchanged and tested with a failing store: no acknowledgement, the rows stay buffered and the quota stays held. A PUT that reports failure may have landed: the buffer is then **sealed**, it takes no rows until a write of it succeeds, so the retry is the same part under the same name and no overlapping part with other bounds can be left (the alternative, writing the larger part and deleting the stale one, needs `DeleteObject`, which append-only IAM policies withhold, and can leave duplicates if the delete fails). The sender keeps the refused rows and resends. Requests are retried for 30 s (`--store-retry-seconds`); a request refused outright (403, 404) is not retried; a bad bucket or credentials stop the server at startup, with the store's own message, because restoring the watchdog is the first read of the store. A timed flush and SIGTERM write ranks 16 at a time, so SIGTERM against a failing store takes one retry period, not one per rank. *Watchdog across a restart.* Every `watchdog.json` also carries `restart`, per rank the clocks its entry in `ranks` leaves out (recent gaps between batches, which give a slow rank its threshold; the work counter; when the stream last changed; the pauses measured), and `abandoned`. All times in the file are seconds before `written_at_ms`, so the file says how long something had lasted, not when; a restart on another host or after a long outage needs no wall clock, and an age longer than the host has been up cannot panic (`Instant` cannot go back that far on every platform: it is cut to what the clock can show, never to zero). At startup, before any stream is served, the server lists `runs/` once (a delimiter listing), reads each run's status (32 at a time) and takes up every run that is neither finished nor abandoned. Rules: (1) *time the server was not running is not held against a rank*: it could not have delivered anything, and a rank that was active at the stop must not look stalled for the seconds its sender takes to reconnect; each clock carries on from what the file said (after a SIGKILL that is up to `status_refresh`, 3 x `--watchdog-seconds`, before the death, which is forgotten; after SIGTERM it is the moment of the stop). (2) Every restored rank that was not finished has no stream: it is `dropped`, `reconnecting` for the usual time while its sender comes back, then `lost`. (3) A rank that was already stopped keeps its quiet time, so a stall is neither forgotten nor started again: a silent or unresponsive rank keeps that verdict for `--silent-seconds` (a hung process whose connection the restart cut is still a suspected hang, not a lost connection) and is `lost` after it; a lost rank is lost at once. The first file the new server writes already carries the incident (`job_stalled` or `rank_silent`) with the old quiet time, from the file of a killed server and from the `server_stopped` file of a clean stop alike. If its sender reconnects, the rank reads silent again (open stream, the old last progress) and a new batch makes it active. (4) Phase-aware pauses carry over: the phase, how long it had lasted, the work counter and when it last moved, the heartbeat age, and the pauses measured (so `recommended_silent_seconds` survives); a pause is measured without the outage. (5) A run is *abandoned* when no stream is open and every rank has been disconnected or finished for `--abandon-hours` (72, 0 never): it is not restored, and its status stops being refreshed (otherwise every dead run is a PUT every 30 s for ever, and every restart would revive it); a reader then reads the old status as "nothing is watching this run", which is true. A run with a stream still open, however hung, is never abandoned. Finished runs are not restored and their files are not rewritten. A status with no `restart` (an older server's) is not restored. A status that cannot be read is logged and skipped; the rest of the runs are taken up. The new server rewrites a restored run's status at its first look, which also ends the reader's "nothing is watching" message. Cost: one listing and one small read per run at startup, before the server listens (simulated: 1,000 runs at 50 ms a request take 1.8 s); `watchdog.json` grows by about 230 bytes a rank (a 64-rank file is 34.7 KB, 14.9 KB of it `restart`) and is still one atomic PUT. Limits: a rank whose sender is dead and whose run is not abandoned shows as `lost` after every restart; the server cannot tell a dead run from a quiet one, as before. A rank that connected before the restore finished is left alone (the restore runs before serving, so this does not happen). Not covered: GCS (compile-checked only), real AWS, TLS to S3, IAM, real latency. *(Changed Nov 17, 2026: a restored rank is now judged from the restart, with a fresh window; see Decisions: "The trigger and the watchdog: one firing for one stall ...".)*
- [x] **`--rank all`: the watcher's view of every rank, from the reports only (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** `watch(run_folder, rank="all")` and `python -m tensorquill <run> --rank all [--watch]` keep one evidence file for a run with several `rank=N` folders, in a shape of its own (`"view": "all_ranks"`, version 1; no `schema_version`, `incidents` or `samples`, so nothing that reads a rank's evidence misreads it; per-rank evidence and `build_evidence` are unchanged). One look reads each rank's `incidents.json` and its newest part name (`read_server_report`) and the run's `watchdog.json`: no part, no detector, in any state. A rank is judged only while its report is `current`; every other state is unknown, with its reason, and is never counted among ranks with no incidents (the question after a restart, where a stale or missing report beside a current one with zero incidents is the case that misleads). Each rank has its own `--report-grace` wait and memory, as the single-rank watcher has for its one rank. Ranks come from the `rank=N` folders, the status's `ranks` and `missing_ranks`, and `range(world_size)`; a rank with nothing stored is `nothing_stored`. After a restart the new server's status knows only the ranks that reconnected, so a rank with parts and a current report is judged from the store, not called never-reporting. Incidents are grouped across judged ranks when the kind, step and what they name (layer, router, experts, parameters, modules, as the kind has it) are equal; measured numbers and logged scalars are not part of identity, since they differ per rank for the same fault; a group carries the first rank's incident and a flag for differing numbers, and incidents that differ are listed with their own ranks. Stalls and silent ranks, Flight Recorder and TrainCheck are run-level, once. The file is written by rename only when a rank's (state, attempt, last step, steps, rebuilds), the set of ranks, or the status changed; a rank inside its wait is masked so a part-then-report blip is no news, and the comparison is with what was written, not with the last look, so a rank written as behind and then current again is rewritten (a mutation that compares the masked key fails its test). `--recompute`, `--top-k` and `--rerun` are refused with `--rank all` (a recompute would read every rank's parts and run the detectors a rank at a time on every change, about 8 s a rank at 10,000 steps), pointing to `--rank N`. Measured (8-core M1 Pro, shared with other builds): one look of 64 ranks of 10,000 steps (200 parts a rank, ten incidents a rank) 34 ms, 29 ms when nothing changed, 102 ms at 50,000 steps; a real server with 64 ranks of 1,000 steps 14 ms. Against 250 ms. The first version listed part names with `pathlib` and took 100 ms at 10,000 steps and 418 ms at 50,000 (94% of a look was building a `Path` per part); `_newest_part` now walks names with `os.scandir`, equal on odd trees by test, which also speeds the single-rank watcher. Read first (a short check): PyTorch's Flight Recorder analyzer works in sets of expected, found and missing ranks and names the missing ones, Megatron's straggler report names the slowest and fastest rank, and W&B run groups and MLflow parent runs keep each run's record under an aggregate view; none groups incidents across ranks or separates an unknown rank from a clean one, which is designed here. Not covered: more than 64 ranks, a network file system, S3/GCS, a Flight Recorder dump finding a stuck collective in this mode (the one place a part is opened, to date the dump), ranks at very different steps (all current; the spread shows in `last_step` and the status's `stream` entries), and a run with a rank whose server report is rebuilt for longer than the grace. `sdk/tests/test_watch_all_ranks.py` (33 tests, real server; forty deliberate changes to the code: thirty-eight killed, two equivalent survivors; the first pass of that check was fooled by a harness that ignored SIGINT and by tests that did not vary what an incident names, a rank's move between two unknown states, `world_size` alone, or a new attempt ending at the same step: each got a test).
- [x] **Seeded gradient-spike and stalled-weights cases (built Nov 15, 2026; two decisions).** `sdk/examples/seeded_failures.py` (`--case grad_spike|stalled_weights|both`, `--toy` for CPU, `--dtype`, `--factor`) plants one failure in one named parameter (the middle block's `self_attn.k_proj.weight`) and exits 1 unless the incident starts within one interval and lists that parameter: a tensor hook multiplies its gradient from step 250, or its own optimizer group's learning rate goes to 0 from step 100 (the gradient stays finite and the optimizer still holds it), and 3 weight checks first show a freeze at step 249. `sdk/tests/test_seeded_failures.py` has 31 tests and one strict xfail; eight deliberate changes to the script each fail it; a toy run is in the CI `extras` job. On the CPU toy both cases pass (`grad_spike` at step 250, `stalled_weights` at step 249). *Decision 1: the factor.* The brief said ×100; the script instead measures the parameter's share of the total gradient norm and uses ×100 or the factor that takes the total to twice the threshold, whichever is larger (×7,200 on the toy), because ×100 on one `k_proj` moves the total ×1.02 (it holds 0.18% of the norm) and `detect_grad_spikes` fires only on the total (5× its median); `--factor 100` keeps the as-specified case and fails. The case tests a failure the detector is meant to catch, and the gap is recorded as a finding (Open gaps) rather than hidden in a red case. *Decision 2: the bf16 stall.* With bfloat16 weights the RMSNorm weights stall from the third check (step 199), before a freeze at step 100 can show (step 249); the one `stalled_weights` incident starts at the first onset and lists at most 20 parameters in model order, so it omits the seeded one although the capture recorded it (reproduced on the toy in bf16; `--dtype float32` is the control and passes). The strict `xfail` test `test_with_bfloat16_weights_the_incident_names_the_seeded_parameter` stays as the standing record and turns into a failure the day the detector is fixed. *Not run:* either case on a GPU (the script's CUDA path, SmolLM2 and fused AdamW with a zero-lr group have never run), `torch.compile`, torch 2.6 and 2.10, the Rust detectors on these captures, gradient clipping in the loop.
- [x] **Release packaging (prepared Nov 15, 2026, commit `488d0d4`; nothing published; superseded in part Nov 17, 2026: the server ships as wheels on PyPI and not as a crate on crates.io, Decisions: "Packaging and the release plan"):** Both packages are `Apache-2.0 OR MIT`. `LICENSE-APACHE` (the unmodified apache.org text) and `LICENSE-MIT` (`Copyright (c) 2026 TensorQuill contributors`) sit at the repo root, with identical copies in `sdk/` and `server/`, so the wheel (`dist-info/licenses/`), the sdist and the `tensorquill-server` crate each carry both. `sdk/pyproject.toml` uses the SPDX expression with `license-files` (this needs `setuptools>=77`), and `sdk/MANIFEST.in` keeps the sdist to source: setuptools would otherwise ship `tests/test*.py` without their `conftest.py`. Checked on macOS, Python 3.12: `python -m build` gives a pure `py3-none-any` wheel holding only `tensorquill/` and `dist-info`; `twine check --strict` passes on both archives; the wheel installed alone with PyTorch into a clean venv imports, runs `python -m tensorquill --help` and `toy_moe.py` to completion, with zero outbound connections (a Python audit hook that raises on any non-loopback connect, send or name lookup, shown to fire on a deliberate connect to a TEST-NET address, plus a macOS `deny network-outbound` sandbox). `cargo publish --dry-run` passes for `tensorquill-server` (16 files). `sdk/tests/test_packaging.py` pins the license metadata. The CI `package` and `crate` jobs are added (`continue-on-error` until they have run once on Linux). Not done: claiming the names and uploading to PyPI and crates.io (the author's accounts), choosing `urls` and authors, turning the README's relative links into absolute URLs, running the clean-venv smoke test on Linux, checking whether `rerun-sdk` (third party; its viewer sends anonymous usage statistics unless disabled) is covered by the zero-telemetry claim, and running `tensorquill-server` under a network audit (its zero-telemetry claim is so far a source and `Cargo.lock` reading only; it does make outbound calls by design to a configured S3 or GCS store).
- [x] **What the dense checks seed (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** `trainer_check.py` and `dense_outlier_check.py` took `--fault`: `massive` (default; the channels with the largest outputs on a calibration batch, as many as `--channels` names, scaled by `--factor`), `block` (every channel) or `channels` (the old seed). *Why:* the first A100 run of `trainer_check.py` scaled two ordinary channels of SmolLM2-360M's block 16 by 100; the loss went from 2.3 to 6.9 and the `GradScaler` skipped four steps, yet the block summary moved 1.07 to 1.14 times (`std`) and 0.95 to 1.07 times (`abs_max`) against a threshold of 5, because the output already carried massive activations (`abs_max` about 20,000, `std` 31 to 37). The detector read the statistics correctly; the seed was invisible to them. *Not chosen:* per-channel statistics now. They would see ordinary-channel growth, but cost capture time on a GPU where router-only capture already misses 1% (A100), and no real failure of that kind has been seen; if one is, measure them then. The limit is in the README and the failure list (entry 8). `sdk/tests/test_seeded_faults.py` builds the situation in miniature and runs the real detector: it sees `massive` and `block`, and does not see `channels`. *Measured on an A100 the same day:* `massive` and `block` both pass all ten `trainer_check.py` checks (0 and 3 steps skipped, loss 8.46 and 13.27) and `massive` passes `dense_outlier_check.py`; `channels` was not rerun on a GPU. *Found, then fixed:* a fault in one block shows in every later block, and the spike incident listed modules by ratio, so it led with block 31, not the seeded 16; it now leads with the first block in the model (the check still only requires the seeded block to be listed).
- [x] **Seeded projection in the replica fingerprint (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** `replica_spread` carries a third float64 value per parameter, a dot product with fixed weights computed from the element index by integer arithmetic (no random number generator, so training's random streams and every device and rank agree), in the same `all_gather` (24 bytes per tensor). It catches changes that keep the norm and sum, such as two swapped elements; it is a screen, not proof of equality, and it is compared only across parameters that really are replicated (`replica_params=`). It is on by default (`replica_projection=False` turns it off) at the existing synchronized `replica_every=50` interval. On CPU it took about seven times the norm and sum (3.7 s against 0.5 s for 24 bf16 4096×4096 tensors) with about 36 MB extra peak memory; its GPU cost is not measured, so the pending two-GPU run prints both timings (`sdk/examples/replica_check.py`). Records with two spread values still read; no schema change.
- [x] **Detector research reviewed (Nov 15, 2026).** Two outside documents proposed about 20 more detectors. Built now, with the tightenings the review asked for: the seeded replica projection (above), `resume_lr_change`, optimizer ownership as evidence for `stalled_weights`, and `weight_trends` as numbers only. Deferred until Gate 0 and the GPU runs give real traces to set thresholds on: attention-entropy and QK-norm rules, kurtosis, spectral entropy and rank metrics, routing-bias span, router collinearity, token-drop counting, RL and robotics packs, batch hashes, GPU-health ingestion, confidence scores and incident correlation. Not adopted as written: straggler localisation from `ts` (it is the host clock at `session.step()`, and synchronous ranks wait for each other, so step times even out and the slow rank is the one that waits least; it needs per-rank compute timing or NCCL wait counters), a compiled-versus-eager probe comparison (it recompiles unless it has its own compiled copy), probe parity across ranks under ZeRO-3 (ranks do not run the same probe batch today and no gather exists), and "one incident per layer" flooding (activation incidents already list their modules in one incident). The Rune viewer decision stands.
- [x] **Per-parameter gradient and stalled-weight rules (decided and built Nov 16, 2026; this is how next action 5 was built).** The decision of earlier the same day was to record ‖Δw‖/‖w‖ per parameter and judge each parameter on its own history. The second half is built; the first turned out not to be needed for either gap, so nothing was added to the capture's cost.
  - *Read first.* TrainCheck hashes each parameter and infers that it changes (`proxy_wrapper/hash.py`, `var_periodic_change_relation.py`); `wandb.watch` logs histograms every 1,000 batches; Lightning, Transformers, Accelerate and Megatron record no update-to-weight ratio. None judges a parameter's gradient against its own history.
  - *Measured before choosing the rule,* on captures recorded on Nov 15, 2026 and kept in `~/Downloads` (Granite 3.0 1B, 218 parameters, 650 steps, two captures; SmolLM2-360M, 290 parameters, three captures with a fault seeded at step 250 or 253; the files do not name their GPU, and one Granite file predates `schema_version`). A parameter at 5× its own 50-step median for one step is ordinary: 277 and 370 times in the two healthy Granite captures, the largest 23× and 36×, on 20 and 21 of each capture's 650 steps (a hard batch lifts 80 parameters at once). Two steps in a row: 2 and 12 times. Three in a row: never on healthy steps. *Checked afterwards on four more Granite captures the rule was not chosen on* (650 steps each): 298 to 473 single steps, 2 to 15 pairs, no three in a row, and no parameter-raised incident. Every seeded fault held it from its seeded step to the end of the capture (150 steps). So the plan's own closing criterion, "at least 5× its median, with no false alarms on the healthy captures", cannot be met by a single-step rule, and the rule asks for 3 recorded steps in a row (`SPIKE_SUSTAIN`).
  - *Gradient rule.* A parameter whose norm stays at least 5× its own median for 3 recorded steps in a row raises `grad_spike` with `"trigger": "parameter"`, dated at the first of those steps and known two recorded steps later; its excursion's norms stay out of its median. The incident is the first one either rule establishes and is never replaced or dated again, so a watcher's incident does not change under it (the first version re-dated a total spike when a parameter's excursion that began earlier was confirmed later; the server's existing fixture caught it). On the toy, ×100 on a `k_proj` holding 0.2% of the total is reported at step 250. On the SmolLM2 block-8 capture, where the total reached 3.9× and the gradient detector said nothing, it reports step 250 with `model.norm.weight` (62×) and `model.layers.8.input_layernorm.weight` (9×). The healthy Granite capture has no new incident.
  - *Stalled-weight rule.* One incident, still dated at the first step anything stalled, with `onsets`: every step at which parameters first reached 3 unchanged checks, their names (those that had changed at an earlier check first, so a later freeze is named even beside many others), `trained_before`, and the optimizer evidence for them; at most 20 onsets, the rest counted. A parameter is listed once. On the toy in bf16 the incident begins at step 199 with the RMSNorm weights (none ever changed) and names the seeded `k_proj` at step 249 as one that had.
  - *"Not updating in this precision" is evidence, not a verdict.* Timing cannot tell a bf16 weight whose updates round away from a float32 weight left out of the optimizer: both never change from the first check. The incident gives what can be known: whether the parameter ever changed, whether an optimizer holds it, and `weight_dtypes` (how many trainable parameters have each dtype, recorded at each weight check in `extra_json`; an optional key, still `schema_version: 1`). The summary says "the weights are bfloat16" only when all of them are.
  - *Server.* Both rules are in `detect.rs` with the same answers after every prefix. `incidents.json` reports `spike_sustain` among its thresholds, and the watcher treats a report without it as an older server's and runs the detectors itself.
  - *Cost.* Python: `detect_grad_spikes` went from 45 to 82 µs per step on the Granite capture (`build_evidence` 435 to 463 ms for 650 steps). Rust: 60.5 ms per flush of 64 ranks with the rules and 60.5 ms without, same machine and synthetic store (M1 Pro, 64 ranks × 2,000 steps, 219 parameters on every step), because a norm below 5× the lowest value in its window needs no median.
  - *Tests.* `sdk/tests/test_seeded_failures.py` (41 tests; the strict xfail is now a passing test), `sdk/tests/test_server_detectors.py` (16 tests, 6 new), a report from an older server in `test_server_reports.py`. Nineteen deliberate changes to the Rust rules each fail them; one first survived (excursion norms entering the median) until the fixture's norms were made distinct. Found by reading, not by a test: the server attached the logged scalars of the step that made a parameter's incident, two steps after the step it is dated at; they are now read when the excursion begins, and the fixtures log distinct values so the wrong step fails.
  - *Not done or not known.* No GPU run (the three `seeded_failures.py` commands). The "healthy" evidence is two models and about 2,000 healthy steps: the rule is uncalibrated beyond them. Warmup, a schedule change or a data-mixture change that lifts one parameter's gradient 5× within 25 steps and keeps it there would be reported, once. A one- or two-step jump in one small parameter is not reported. The total rule is unchanged, including its 2 spikes on one healthy Granite capture.
- [x] **A silent rank says where it is stuck (Nov 16, 2026).** Asked whether NCCL and PyTorch's Flight Recorder are used well. Findings: nothing has run over NCCL yet; the Flight Recorder path has only read synthetic dumps; and Flight Recorder helps only if its variables were set before the job started. Meta's Monarch (sibling checkout, Nov 14, 2026) was read for comparison: it uses neither PyTorch's Flight Recorder nor any `TORCH_NCCL_*` variable (its "flight recorder" is its own per-actor event ring, and its tensor engine calls NCCL directly), and it diagnoses hangs with py-spy stack dumps taken by its host agents and stored as Arrow tables.
  - Built: the heartbeat thread sends the Python threads' stacks once a rank's work has stood still for 60 s (`init(stack_seconds=)`); the server keeps the newest in `watchdog.json`; `job_stalled` and `rank_silent` carry `stuck_at` and the summary names the frame, the job's own calling line, and how long the stack has not changed. A call reached through TensorQuill's own code (the replica check's `all_gather`) is marked.
  - Why in-process and not py-spy: TensorQuill is a library inside someone else's job and has no agent on the host; py-spy is a separate binary that must be installed on every node (Monarch does not bundle it either).
  - Limits: Python frames only; no stack when the main thread hangs holding the interpreter lock (the rank is `unresponsive`); the assessment stays `suspected_hang`. `faulthandler.dump_traceback_later` would cover the lock case without the lock, but it is one process-wide timer that pytest and users also set, so it is left out.
  - The server builds `stuck_at` too (`watchdog.rs`, Python is the reference) so its own `incidents` match; the whole stacks stay in the status's `ranks` and out of incidents and `evidence.json`. A stack is at most 68 frames, under 10 KB a rank, so about 0.6 MB of status for 64 stalled ranks at the worst; a heartbeat over 64 KB loses its stack. The status is rewritten when a stack appears or goes, not when its seconds change.
  - Verified: 377 SDK tests, 71 server tests; one test runs a real process blocked in a wait through the real server, reads the frame from the evidence and checks the server's incidents equal Python's. Not verified: a real stuck collective on GPUs.
  - Still open from the same review: the two-GPU NCCL run of `replica_check.py`; one real forced hang read through `--flight-recorder`; a warning at `init` when several ranks run with no Flight Recorder buffer set; reading `duration_ms` (`TORCH_NCCL_ENABLE_TIMING`) for a slow rank; Flight Recorder dumps stay on the training nodes.
- [x] **Plan and implementation reviewed (Nov 16, 2026).** The code was run against this plan: 361 SDK tests (with the Hugging Face extra, `moto` and `boto3` installed; without them the seeded-failure runs and the S3 tests are skipped, 14 skips in the venv as it was), 68 server tests, 37 fixture tests, `cargo fmt` and `clippy` on both feature sets.
  - *Found stale and corrected here:* "How it works", the Build-and-borrow rows and specification item 5 still described detection as Python only and the port as conditional; the dedup key lacked `attempt`; the cross-GPU limits said the projection was not built; `scaler=` was "planned"; Gate 2 said "all five" over four boxes; the release item was nested under the CUDA runner. In `sdk/README.md`: "Skeleton", "S3 and GCS have never run", and the probe and logged scalars "not run on a GPU". In the failure list: entries 4 to 6.
  - *First-run walkthrough.* A script written only from `RUN_ON_YOUR_JOB.md` ("Plain PyTorch", a 4-block random Llama on CPU, clipping, `session.log`, `probe=`, 160 steps) ran, wrote 661 KB of records, and `python -m tensorquill` wrote the evidence and the recording with no incident. Three things a first user meets: `session.close()` says nothing, so the file's path and the next command have to be known from the guide; a healthy run prints only "No incidents detected."; and neither that line nor `evidence.json` says what was checked.
  - *Recommended, not built, in this order.* (1) **Say what was checked.** "No incidents" means little without it: a router-only capture cannot show stalled weights or activations, and a run without `scaler=` or `replica_group=` has no loss-scale or drift check. The all-ranks view already refuses to count an unknown rank as clean; one rank's evidence should do the same for kinds, from counts of the records that carried each kind's inputs, in Python and in the server's report. Before Gate 3, since an agent reads "no incidents" as "healthy". (2) **An evidence file an agent can read.** For an MoE run the per-step `samples` are almost the whole file (86 MB at 10,000 steps); an agent needs the incidents and a window around each. Offer the file without samples, or with samples only near incidents, and measure both arms in Gate 3. (3) **After Gate 1, a small read-only MCP server** over the same files (incidents, one incident, a series for one parameter or layer, the all-ranks status), because that is where every tracker now meets agents (competitor check); not before, since a coding agent on the same machine reads files. (4) One line at `close()` through `logging`, naming the file and the command.
- [x] **Platform roadmap reviewed (Nov 16, 2026).** Two more outside documents proposed widening TensorQuill into a general training-reliability platform: an always-on core, opt-in model-family packs (transformer, MoE, diffusion, multimodal, RL, robotics) and triggered forensics, on a 90-day schedule. They were checked against this repository and the sibling `pytorch` (`7c84e46`, Nov 13, 2026) and `Megatron-LM` (`4b11b81`) checkouts.
  - *Adopted now, inside Gate 2:* per-parameter checks judged against each parameter's own history, in place of separate fixes for the two open detector gaps (next action 5). Proposed as update-to-weight ratios; built the same day without the ratio, from fields the capture already writes (see the entry above). The ratio itself waits for a real job that needs it.
  - *After Gate 0, picked by what the author's job needs:*
    - data progress invariants: the sampler epoch and a forgotten `set_epoch`, hashed batch IDs, repeated or skipped shards;
    - a checkpoint manifest;
    - host time in `next(loader)`;
    - slow-rank timing;
    - passive NVML/DCGM readings and CUDA memory scalars.
  - *After Gate 1:*
    - attention-entropy and QK rules (the probe already records entropy and the largest logit);
    - MoE token dropping and routing-bias span;
    - a Transformer Engine FP8 adapter;
    - labelling each kind as invariant, anomaly or observation (built the same day: "Incident classes");
    - one run state (healthy, degraded, unknown, unhealthy), which `--rank all` already half provides (current, missing, unknown).
  - *Not in this plan:*
    - diffusion, multimodal, RL and robotics packs;
    - a tiered collector for 10,000+ ranks;
    - active DCGM diagnostics or an always-on profiler during training, which would stop or slow training and break the ≤1% gate.
  - *Corrected:*
    - PyTorch's wait counters do not time collectives. At `7c84e46` they cover TCPStore calls, compiles, the Flight Recorder dump and `ProcessGroupNCCL__checkTimeout`. So neither they nor `ts` find a slow rank. The source is the Flight Recorder's per-collective `duration_ms`, present when `TORCH_NCCL_ENABLE_TIMING` is set. This replaces "NCCL wait counters" in the entry above.
    - A host timer around `.to(device, non_blocking=True)` times only the enqueue, and making it accurate needs a synchronize.
  - *Confirmed in Megatron:* `count_zeros_fp32`, `check_weight_hash_across_dp_replicas_interval` and `gpu_sniff_test.py`. *Not checked* (no sibling checkout): the TRL, Transformer Engine and DCGM claims.
  - *Not adopted:* the documents' timetable, which moved update-to-weight checks after release. Every new kind costs a Python and a Rust detector with parity tests, so kinds are added only when a gate or a real job asks for them.
  - *Changed later the same day by the author: build ahead of Gates 0 and 1.* Three groups are built in parallel, each in its own worktree branch and merged into `main` afterwards:
    - **A:** data progress (sampler epoch and a forgotten `set_epoch`, hashed batch indices, duplicates across ranks, repeats or skips after a resume, no progress) and a checkpoint manifest (what a save held, and what a resume restored or reset).
    - **B:** an attention-entropy and logit-growth detector over the probe's existing records, and one run state (healthy, degraded, unknown, unhealthy).
    - **C:** GPU memory and NVML readings, and dataloader wait time.

    Each is held to the gates' rules: nothing blocks or slows training past the ≤1% router-only budget, any new capture is off by default unless it measures as free, and every kind is in Python and Rust with parity tests. Slow-rank timing waits for the two-GPU run; token dropping, the labels and the other packs stay deferred.
- [x] **Built ahead of Gates 0 and 1 (built Nov 16, 2026, three parallel branches merged the same day).** Fifteen kinds, CPU-tested; none has run on a new GPU job.
  - *A, data progress and checkpoint manifests.*
    - Opt-in capture. `session.watch_loader(loader)` (or `session.data(...)`) records, per step, the batches drawn, a 64-bit BLAKE2b of their sample indices, the pass and the sampler's own epoch. `session.checkpoint(state, event=)` records what a saved or loaded state dict holds. A resumed session records once whether its optimizer came back with its state. The Lightning, `Trainer` and Accelerate adapters record checkpoints; none turns the loader hook on.
    - Kinds: `data_stalled`, `epoch_order_repeated`, `duplicate_batches` (cross-rank, in `watchdog.json`), and `resume_data_mismatch` and `resume_incomplete`. The last two are observations judged at a resume's first step, as `resume_lr_change` is.
    - Not adopted: hooking `torch.save` (fragile), and comparing hashes on the training side (a blocking all-gather).
    - Cost on CPU: 4 µs per batch of 32 indices, 48 µs per 1,024. All 36 deliberate rule changes fail a test.
  - *B, attention rules and one run state.*
    - `attention_collapse` (a block's mean entropy share at or below 0.5× its baseline, Zhai et al. 2023), `attention_uniform` (at least 0.98 and 0.2 above its baseline) and `attention_logit_growth` (the largest logit at 2.0×), read from the probe's existing records.
    - A block's baseline is the median of its last 5 probes that met no rule, from its 2nd probe, and a rule must hold on 2 probes in a row.
    - Calibrated on the recorded SmolLM2 captures: uniform on the downstream blocks of both seeded runs, collapse on blocks 23–31 of the block run, nothing on healthy probes. Also calibrated on three tiny Llamas trained from scratch on CPU: nothing at lr 1e-3 and 3e-4; collapse, then logit growth past 100, at lr 3e-3.
    - The logit rule's margin is thin: healthy runs moved the largest logit 1.72×, and the one seeded rise was 1.69×. No MoE probe was calibrated. `probe_json` now keeps the model's module order.
    - `run_state` (`healthy`, `degraded`, `unknown`, `unhealthy`) is computed from the incidents, report freshness, ranks without a current report, `world_size`, the status's age and summary, and `not_checked`. It is in `evidence.json`, the all-ranks file, the CLI and `--watch`, and the server's `watchdog.json`. The worst reason wins, and any unrecognised state is `unknown`, so missing evidence never reads healthy.
    - Incidents are never closed, and observations (`resume_lr_change`, `memory_imbalance`) made a run `unhealthy` until kinds were labelled (fixed the same day: "Incident classes"). All 50 deliberate rule changes fail a test.
  - *C, GPU memory, NVML and data wait.*
    - `init(system=True)` reads, once per flush, the CUDA caching allocator's counters (allocated, reserved, peak, retries, OOMs since the session started). They are host-side and add no synchronize. On the writer thread it reads NVML's temperature, SM clock, power, volatile ECC counts, throttle reasons and Xid events (`tensorquill[nvml]`). It is off by default until the Colab cell measures its GPU cost.
    - `data_wait` times `next()` on PyTorch DataLoaders, or any iterable through `session.time_data`, on the training thread in the train phase, never the device move. It is on in the adapters, at +0.5 µs per batch on CPU.
    - Kinds: `memory_growth`, `memory_pressure`, `allocator_retries`, `hardware_errors` (evidence), `gpu_throttled` (an observation; the power cap alone is never reported) and `input_starvation`. `memory_imbalance` is cross-rank, in Python's all-ranks view only, from a `memory` summary the server now writes per rank.
    - The default capture's CPU cost is unchanged (−0.42%, within noise). All 36 deliberate rule changes fail a test.
    - The branch also fixed an existing hang. `FlightSender.last_error` kept its traceback, which tied the sender and the gRPC stream into a reference cycle. A forked DataLoader worker could collect that cycle and wait forever on gRPC threads the fork did not copy. The error is now kept without its traceback.
  - *Merge.* Mechanical conflicts in `capture.py`, `evidence.py`, `flight.py`, the adapters, `detect.rs`, `ingest.rs` and `test_server_detectors.py`. One test pinned the system kinds' position in `PORTED` by index and was changed to find it by name.
  - *Open:*
    - the group C Colab cell;
    - a real from-scratch run for the logit margin;
    - MoE probes;
    - wrapping loaders handed to Lightning or the `Trainer`;
    - per-epoch schedulers tripping `resume_incomplete`;
    - a hung NVML call, which blocks the writer thread and, once the 8-block queue fills, training (fixed by lane T3, merged Nov 19, 2026: every NVML call on a thread of its own, waited for a bounded time);
    - the server's detector timings, repeated the same day ("Re-timed with twenty-five kinds").
- [x] **Incident classes (decided and built Nov 16, 2026).** Every kind is labelled: `invariant` (12, should never happen, by a fixed rule), `anomaly` (11, judged against the run's own baseline) or `observation` (9, context that claims no failure). The table is defined once per language, `INCIDENT_CLASSES` in `evidence.py` and in `server/src/detect.rs`, and a test holds the two identical.
  - Every incident carries `class`, in `evidence.json`, the all-ranks file, `incidents.json` and `watchdog.json`. The key is optional and `schema_version` stays 1. The CLI adds a `By class:` line.
  - The run state is `unhealthy` only for an invariant, an anomaly, or a kind the reader does not know; a reader never trusts an incident's own `class`. Observations go under a new `observations` reason that never changes the state, so a run with only observations reads `healthy` with them named.
  - `job_stalled` and `rank_silent` are observations, because the stream cannot tell a hang from a pause or a dead sender. A stalled or degraded status therefore makes the run `unknown`, not `unhealthy`. A Flight Recorder `collective_hang` still makes it unhealthy.
  - `memory_pressure` and `allocator_retries` are observations too. Healthy runs that fill the GPU, and batch-size finders that catch OOMs on purpose, meet those rules. Group C's Colab cell should confirm this.
  - Incidents still never close. A design for anomalies that recover is in the session notes: `ended_step` on anomalies only, recovery when the rule's own statistic stays below half its trigger, and the run state counting only open anomalies.
  - Testing: Python and the server agree through the real server on a throttled GPU (`healthy`), a spike beside it (`unhealthy`) and a stall (`unknown`). All 12 deliberate changes to classes or the rule fail a test.
- [x] **Re-timed with twenty-five kinds (measured Nov 16, 2026).**
  - *Method.* The same examples on the synthetic Granite-shaped store, plus a copy with the new columns filled at realistic sizes. The server from before that day ran beside it at the same load.
  - *Single thread.* Detector time per 50-step flush of 64 ranks is 61 to 63 ms on the store as before, the same as the old binary. With the columns filled it is 66 ms, and 146 to 150 ms on the 1-in-20 flush that carries a probe, so about 70 ms averaged over a run (+14%).
  - *Why the probe flush costs more.* About 95% of its extra cost is serde_json reading past the 64×64 attention maps. They are 98% of the probe's bytes, and no rule reads them. Storing a probe flush's parts takes 350 ms against 155 ms, which the server already paid and no benchmark included.
  - *Live, at Granite's pace.* Without the new columns, every flush of the 64 live streams was acknowledged within 202 ms (3 runs). With them, 3 runs of 4 stayed within 250 ms and one reached 620 ms, unattributed. Server CPU rose to 12 to 13% of a core and peak memory to 650 to 700 MB. The 10,000-step restart rebuild is 7.1 to 7.9 s.
  - *By the plan's rule.* The budget holds as detector time. As acknowledgement time it no longer holds reliably once probes are on.
  - *Next, before probes are on by default.* Move the maps and histograms out of `probe_json`, into a column the detectors do not parse or out of the step record. Shrinking `MAP_SIZE` would cut storage as well.
  - *Not measured:* the 10 ms pace, more than 64 ranks, a real Granite probe, a GPU, S3 or GCS.
- [x] **Focused plan: eight decisions (decided Nov 16, 2026).** A shorter plan, `TensorQuill — Focused Plan.md`, was drafted the same day. It gives TensorQuill one job (wake a team's coding agent with layer-level evidence when a multi-GPU PyTorch run goes wrong), freezes other work, and lists five agent-loop pieces and five results to prove. Checked against `main`, it disagreed with the code in five places, which the author settled as below. This document stays the decision log. The focused plan is its front page: updated to match these decisions and committed the same day as [TensorQuill — Focused Plan.md](TensorQuill%20%E2%80%94%20Focused%20Plan.md).
  - *1. Run state: the code stands.* Invariants and anomalies both make a run `unhealthy` ("Incident classes"). The focused plan's rule, that an anomaly only makes a run `degraded`, is dropped: `degraded` already means impaired monitoring in the watchdog and in `run_state`, and a second meaning would cost a rewrite in two languages. False alarms are limited in the trigger instead. It fires on invariants by default, and anomalies are opt-in until their thresholds are tuned on the author's own job.
  - *2. What was checked.* Each kind gets one of three values: checked, not configured, or unavailable. Only unavailable makes the run `degraded`, so the default router-only capture can still read `healthy`. The summary gives the count, such as "9 of 32 kinds checked". *Built the same day ("What was checked").* Before it, `not_checked` covered four checks (attention, the learning rate across a resume, TrainCheck and the Flight Recorder), and the MoE fixture `unseen-1`, whose records carry no weight check, loss scale or replica spread, lists none. This replaces the focused plan's "a kind with no inputs makes the run `unknown`", and settles item (1) of "Plan and implementation reviewed".
  - *3. Agent benchmark.* It is the Gate 1 harness with its own scoring, without the Rune arm (B). "Beats scalar-only data" is claimed only on real captures that carry loss and gradient curves, because the scalar arm is empty on the four MoE fixtures: their records hold no loss, learning rate or total gradient norm (counted Nov 16, 2026). Both Gate 1 lines compare arm B, so the comparison arm and the pass line go under "Amendments" in `experiments/gate1/PREREGISTRATION.md` before the first trial.
  - *4. Trigger.* It fires once per new incident, not on a change of run state, because incidents never close.
  - *5. Drifted rank.* The outlier is worked out at gather time, where each rank's fingerprints exist, and recorded only when drift is found. The step record otherwise keeps only the spread per parameter. It is built after the two-GPU run, since two ranks cannot name one.
  - *6. One Rust copy.* It applies to the 28 kinds the server has. The four Python-only kinds (`collective_hang`, `collective_desync`, `traincheck_violation`, `memory_imbalance`) already exist once. For those 28 this reverses "Python as the reference and for offline work" (Nov 15, 2026). The parity tests stay until each Python duplicate is deleted. How Python calls the Rust detectors, a PyO3 extension or a `detect` subcommand on the server binary, is still open.
  - *7. Bookkeeping.* Labelling every kind is done ("Incident classes"). The freeze on cloud stores covers real-AWS and GCS runs; the S3 and GCS code is built and on by default. The probe-flush delay ("Re-timed with twenty-five kinds") is an open gap.
  - *8. Where the plan lives.* Decisions are logged here, as before.
  - *Frozen by the focused plan, each until its condition is met.*
    - New detector kinds stop at 32 until a real job shows a failure none of them catches. This ends "Built ahead of Gates 0 and 1". *Lifted by the author on Nov 17, 2026; see Decisions, "The freeze on new detector kinds is lifted".*
    - The GCS end-to-end test, the `s3://` and `gs://` reader and real-AWS runs wait for a user who asks (next action 8).
    - The hosted tier, SSO and pricing wait until three outside teams run the SDK.
    - RL, robotics, diffusion and multimodal packs wait for a user who brings a workload.
  - *Confirmed by the author the same day:* postponing the Rune app ("Rune viewer postponed"). It reverses the viewer decision of Nov 14, 2026 ("the Rune app is built either way"). Dropping the Rune arm from the benchmark (item 3) had not decided it.
  - *The three open-source debuggers in the focused plan's comparison, read from source the same day and not run.* None detects multi-GPU failures or publishes a measured `torch.compile` cost.
    - [NeuralDBG](https://github.com/LambdaSection/NeuralDBG) 1.5.0 (`61239f4d`, MIT) hooks every leaf module on every step for gradient and activation health. Its hooks are wrapped in `torch._dynamo.disable`. Its compile tests check that events arrive under `aot_eager` and that a hooked run is under 20× the baseline; the test named for graph breaks asserts nothing. It only warns when the model is wrapped in DDP or DataParallel. Its dead-expert check is an empty stub. The README calls the engine proprietary, but an `engine/` folder marked MIT is bundled. Its MCP server has three tools, and the diagnose tool executes model code passed to it; it does not read a live run.
    - [MoEWatch](https://github.com/Abineshabee/moewatch) 0.1.0 (`76b664f`, Apache 2.0) reads router outputs of Hugging Face MoE models, found by class name. It counts only each token's top-1 expert, copies the router output to the CPU on sampled steps (every 10th by default), and has WARN and ERROR alerts with JSON output but no trigger. Nothing in it handles `torch.compile` or more than one process.
    - [LLM-Training-Debugger](https://github.com/YanKaFei/LLM-Training-Debugger) (`3f904c9`, package 0.1.7a1, Apache 2.0) is the closest in design: events to Parquet, then incidents that carry their evidence and say what could not be concluded. It has `compare` (the first step where two runs diverge) and `scan` (the outlier in a batch), which TensorQuill lacks. Its own limits list says multi-rank, `torch.compile`, AMP and FSDP are untested, and that every number was measured on an Apple M2 Max with 4–6 layer models. Its v0.2 list names multi-node, Flight Recorder hangs, attention, MoE and checkpoint comparison.
  - *Checked after the update, two points open.*
    - Build piece 1's done test says a router-only capture "still reads `healthy`". An evidence file built without a server's `watchdog.json` reads `degraded` today (reason `no_status`), so either a missing status counts as not configured or the test runs against a server's store. *Settled the same day, open to change: not configured for a local file, unavailable in a server's store ("What was checked").*
    - Result 4 asks for at least 8 real captures with loss and gradient curves. Of the seven captures recorded Nov 15, 2026, only the two `trainer_check.py` runs record a loss. `moe_dogfood.py`, `dense_outlier_check.py`, `seeded_failures.py` and `replica_check.py` log none, so week 1's GPU runs would not count unless those scripts log the loss first.
      - Fixed the same day: all four log `train/loss` through `session.log`, the name the `Trainer` adapter uses, and `replica_check.py --out DIR` keeps each rank's records (it kept them only in memory).
