- [ ] **Trial jobs: nanochat and nanoMoE (noted Nov 16, 2026; not decided, nothing run).** The author proposed them for the "own job" runs. Both are cloned in `/Users/ak/Documents/tensrview`. Everything below was read from their code and from `capture.py`; no training, install or download was done.
  - *What they are.*
    - `nanochat` (karpathy/nanochat, `92d63d4`, MIT): a dense GPT trained from scratch. It pins `torch==2.9.1`, compiles the model (`torch.compile(model, dynamic=False)`), and on several GPUs uses no `DistributedDataParallel`: its optimizer (`MuonAdamW` in `nanochat/optim.py`) averages gradients inside `step()` with `all_reduce` or `reduce_scatter`, updates a slice on each rank and gathers the weights back. Rank 0 calls `wandb.init(project="nanochat", name=args.run)`. `runs/runcpu.sh` is a 6-layer run of 5,000 steps, tuned by its author for about 30 minutes on an M3 Max; `runs/speedrun.sh` uses 8 GPUs.
    - `nanoMoE` (wolfecameron/nanoMoE, `661fbcf`, MIT; the folder is `nanoMoE`, there is none named `nanochatmoe`): a nanoGPT fork with MoE layers. Its main config is 6 layers, 8 experts, top-2, a router in every second block, standard DDP on two GPUs, `torch.compile`, and W&B project `nano-moe`. The MoE settings (`n_exp`, `top_k`, `use_aux_loss`, `stride`, `device`, `compile`) are globals of `train.py`, so the small Shakespeare config (5,000 steps, dense by default) becomes an MoE run from the command line.
  - *What they would test that nothing has yet.* Training from scratch on code the SDK has never seen. Every threshold was set on Granite and SmolLM2 fine-tunes, so false alarms during warm-up are what these runs measure. Also: a second MoE implementation, a distributed scheme that is not DDP, and two real W&B projects and run names for "Projects, run names and comparing runs". nanoMoE with its balance loss on and off is a paired run for the comparison.
  - *Against the focused plan's results.*
    - Result 2: either can meet the pass line (1,000 steps, a real anomaly or zero false incidents). The row says "one of the author's real jobs" and the plan says this result decides whether the product is wanted; these are other people's training code. Whether they count is the author's to decide.
    - Results 1 and 3: unchanged. The two-GPU `replica_check.py` and `seeded_failures.py` runs and the Granite head-to-head are still owed.
    - Result 4: a capture counts only if it holds a failure for the agent to name. nanoMoE without its balance loss may give a natural one; not known.
  - *Three things the code shows.*
    1. nanoMoE's `Router` returns `(used_capacity, cb_weight, sec_mask)`, and `used_capacity` is an integer tensor of per-expert counts. `_first_tensor` in `capture.py` takes the first integer tensor of a router's output as expert indices, so watching that module would count wrongly and raise nothing. The inner linear layer (`w_g`) gives the logits, and so the assignments the router asked for; the ones kept after the capacity limit are not seen. The first step of any run is `named_modules()` and one forward to confirm what is hooked.
    2. On several ranks nanochat's `.grad` is the rank's own gradient until `step()` reduces it, and TensorQuill reads gradient norms before the step, so norms are local to a rank. Weights are gathered to every rank, so `replica_drift` is expected to read clean; an expectation only.
    3. On Apple's MPS the exact weight check is off (no float64), so a Mac run uses the CPU or goes without `stalled_weights`.
  - *Proposed order, if the author says go.*
    1. nanoMoE on Shakespeare on the Mac's CPU, with the Python detectors. No install; one download by its own `data/shakespeare_char/prepare.py`: `input.txt`, about 1 MB, from the `karpathy/char-rnn` repository. The integration lines are kept as a patch in this repository and the clone is left as it is.
    2. nanochat's `runcpu.sh` run. It needs its own environment (PyTorch 2.9.1, `rustbpe`, `kernels`, `tiktoken`), data shards and a tokenizer run; their sizes were not read.
    3. Both on two GPUs, with the runs of result 1.
  - *Open.*
    - A guard in `capture.py` for a router output whose integer tensor cannot be indices (item 1). *Built Nov 17, 2026:* by default the hook takes an integer tensor as indices only when its size is a whole multiple of the tokens the router was called on, read from the shapes alone, so nothing depends on the data and it holds under `torch.compile`. A router that fails this is not counted, and the session warns once at the next step, naming the router and the two ways out (`Router(..., indices=...)`, or the inner module whose output is the logits). `sdk/tests/test_router_guard.py` (4 tests, a stand-in for nanoMoE's router; two of them fail without the guard); the whole suite passes with it (1,224 and 81). Open: such a router still writes zero counts each step instead of no entry; a router given an `indices=` function is not checked; nanoMoE itself was not run.
    - PyTorch 2.9 is inside `torch>=2.6` but no CI job runs it.
    - nanochat's own data loader is not a `DataLoader`, so the data kinds would read `not_configured` without an adapter.
    - No timing is to be taken from either while other builds run on the same machine.
- [x] **Optimizer ownership as evidence for `stalled_weights` (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** Equal float64 norms do not prove unchanged weights, so the incident stays a warning (worded "float64 norm did not change"). Each weight check records `optimizer_ownership`: whether an optimizer that has stepped holds each trainable parameter. It is `unknown`, never "not held", when ownership cannot be established: the optimizer hooks are off (`auto_step=False`), or no optimizer that stepped holds any of the model's parameters (DeepSpeed ZeRO, Megatron and FSDP1 step copies or flattened parameters). It travels in `extra_json` through the server. It is attached to the stalled incident as `optimizer` (`known` with the parameters no optimizer holds, or `unknown` with the reason) and is never an alarm of its own, because a parameter may be left out on purpose.
- [x] **Learning rate across a resume (decided and built Nov 15, 2026).** `detect_resume_lr_changes` reads every attempt and, at the first step of each resumed attempt, compares its learning rate with the earlier attempt's: at the same step when the earlier attempt recorded it, otherwise continuing the earlier attempt's recent slope. A difference is reported as `resume_lr_change`, an observation and not a verdict: some restarts reset warmup on purpose, and a record cannot show the intent, so the incident says whether the resumed value equals the run's first learning rate and the summary says it is expected when the schedule was changed deliberately. A restart of the numbering is left to `repeated_steps`. A resume that cannot be compared because no learning rate was recorded goes into a new optional `not_checked` list in `evidence.json` (still `schema_version: 1`; readers that ignore the key lose nothing).
- [x] **TLS for the ingest stream (decided Nov 14, 2026):** the server uses tonic's `tls-aws-lc` feature, not `tls-ring`. `object_store` already brings rustls with the aws-lc provider, and with both providers compiled in rustls cannot pick one: a `tls-ring` build panics at startup when given `--tls-cert`/`--tls-key` (reproduced on the build before the switch). The sender trusts the system's certificate authorities for `grpcs://`; `TENSORQUILL_TLS_CA` (or `tls_root_certs=` on `FlightSender` and `send_file`) names a PEM file for a private or self-signed one, read at `init` so a wrong path fails before training starts. Tested on loopback only, with a self-signed certificate.
- [x] **The sender across `fork` (decided Nov 14, 2026):** a forked child (`DataLoader` workers on Linux, `multiprocessing`) gets a copy of the sender without its thread. `FlightSender.close()` compares the process ID with the one that created it and does nothing in a child, instead of an `os.register_at_fork` handler, which would add process-wide state for the same effect. A review claimed forked workers deadlock on their first batch; two tests (forked `DataLoader` workers during streaming, and a forked child that closes its copy) show no deadlock on macOS, and CI runs both on Linux.
- [ ] **Price for the post-Gate 4 hosted tier**, after Step 26 measures storage and server cost per run.
- [x] **Viewer (decided Nov 14, 2026):** Rerun is temporary. TensorQuill builds its own Rust viewer app on Rune that reads `evidence.json` and the JSONL/Parquet data directly; the `.rrd` export stays as the interim viewer until the app reaches parity. This replaces the earlier rule that a custom view was built only after a ≥2× win in Gate 1. *Postponed Nov 16, 2026 ("Rune viewer postponed"): Rerun stays the viewer and the app is not built until outside users ask for views Rerun cannot give.*
- [x] **Framework adapters (decided Nov 14, 2026):** PyTorch stays the only capture engine; adapters are thin and exist only where a framework hides the optimizer, clipping or scaler loop. Order:
  1. A Hugging Face `TrainerCallback` (`sdk/src/tensorquill/huggingface.py`, built Nov 14, 2026 and tested on CPU; named `huggingface` so the module does not shadow the `transformers` package), accepted on a dense single-GPU CUDA run with `sdk/examples/trainer_check.py`, which passes in toy mode on CPU and is still to run on a GPU. It does not wait for the multi-GPU gate. It covers TRL and PEFT; the first TRL test is `SFTTrainer` with LoRA, then DPO and GRPO separately (GRPO can generate rollouts in a separate vLLM server, which trainer-side capture does not see).
  2. An Accelerate recipe for custom loops. Built Nov 14, 2026 as `tensorquill.accelerator.watch_accelerator(session)` (`sdk/src/tensorquill/accelerator.py`), which the `Trainer` callback now uses too; tested on CPU. The loss is logged on every `backward` as the running sum for the step, because wrapping `AcceleratedOptimizer.step` does not work: a learning-rate scheduler replaces `step` on the optimizer instance.
  3. In parallel, validation of what is already supported: DDP over NCCL, then FSDP2 and tensor parallelism, Megatron-LM's `auto_step=False` recipe, and Lightning with `torch.compile`.
  4. One PPO example and one LeRobot imitation-learning example, to find what RL and robotics runs need beyond model internals (rollout and update boundaries, a link to policy evaluation).
  5. DeepSpeed: no adapter until someone brings a workload. Checked Nov 14, 2026 on a DeepSpeed 0.20.1 engine on CPU: at ZeRO stage 0 the default works; at stages 1 and 2 the optimizer steps flattened copies of the weights and automatic counting recorded nothing, silently. TensorQuill now warns after 10 optimizer steps with none on the model's parameters, and `RUN_ON_YOUR_JOB.md` has an `auto_step=False` recipe that records steps, the loss and DeepSpeed's global gradient norm. Stage 3, mixed precision and several ranks are not run. JAX, TensorFlow, tree models and FSDP1 stay out of scope.

  Checked against the Transformers (5.24.0.dev0) and Accelerate (1.16.0.dev0) sources, which shape the callback:
  - `Trainer` wraps, prepares and (through Accelerate's `TorchDynamoPlugin`) compiles the model before `on_train_begin`. Callbacks receive the unwrapped `trainer.model`. The callback must attach hooks before the first compiled forward, and a second `train()` on the same trainer is an open case.
  - Step boundaries are `on_pre_optimizer_step` and `on_optimizer_step`, which surround `optimizer.step()`; TensorQuill's own optimizer hooks already fire there. `on_log` fires every `logging_steps` with interval averages, after the step has ended; the callback does not record them in v1.
  - `accelerator.clip_grad_norm_` no longer calls `torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_` (it uses `get_total_norm` and `clip_grads_with_norm_`), so the clip wrapper records nothing under `Trainer` or Accelerate. This is new in 1.16.0.dev0; Accelerate 1.15.0 still calls it. The callback wraps `Accelerator.clip_grad_norm_` (and `Accelerator.backward`, for the per-step loss, which `Trainer` gives callbacks no access to) while a session is open, and skips recording when the inner PyTorch call already did.
  - `AcceleratedOptimizer.step` calls `scaler.step` and `scaler.update` itself, so `scaler=accelerator.scaler` should work unchanged.
  - Ray actors that share no process group all resolve to rank 0 and, given the same `run_id` and folder, append to one file. v1 answer: one learner writes, or each process passes its own `sink=` (documented in `RUN_ON_YOUR_JOB.md`). Per-role streams wait until actors are instrumented.
- [x] **CI (decided Nov 14, 2026):** GitHub Actions runs the CPU matrix on every push to `main` and every pull request, installing CPU-only PyTorch wheels. The GPU compiler gate is a manual `workflow_dispatch` job on a self-hosted runner: GitHub's free runners have no GPU, and a job that pull requests cannot start keeps outside code off that runner. On CPU the gate script runs on `aot_eager`, so Inductor and CUDA graphs are checked only by the `gpu` job.
- [x] **Default capture level (decided Nov 13, 2026):** `capture="routers"` is the always-on default for MoE models, and `capture="full"` is an opt-in debugging mode.
- [x] **Multi-GPU reporting (decided Nov 14, 2026):** every rank writes its own `tensorquill-out/<run_id>.rank<rank>.jsonl` and computes `replica_spread` on `replica_group` every `replica_every=50` steps where `(step + 1) % replica_every == 0`.
- [x] **Ingest architecture and v1 server hosting (decided Nov 14, 2026):** the SDK always writes the local `.jsonl` file first; when `endpoint=` / `TENSORQUILL_ENDPOINT` is set, an opt-in sender tails that file from `<run_id>.rank<rank>.offset` and streams over Arrow Flight `DoPut` to the open-source, self-hosted Rust `tensorquill-server` binary (`server/`), which deduplicates by `(run_id, rank, attempt, step)` and writes Zstd Parquet parts via `object_store`.
- [x] **Ingest details the specification left open (decided Nov 14, 2026, with the build of `flight.py` and `server/`):**
  - *One long-lived `DoPut` stream per rank, acknowledged only when stored.* The sender keeps one stream open and the server answers with `PutResult.app_metadata = {"end_byte_offset": N}` (JSON, like the request metadata, so fields can be added) only after the Parquet part holding those rows is written. A stream per batch would either write a part every 50 steps instead of every 500, or acknowledge rows that exist only in server memory, and then a killed server would lose steps the sender had already moved past. The cost is that `.offset` trails the file by up to 10 seconds or 500 steps; the end-to-end test kills the server while it holds 100 unstored steps and loses none.
  - *Batches are at most 50 steps and 4 MiB even when catching up after an outage*, so the sender's memory stays bounded; a single line over 4 MiB is sent alone. gRPC keepalive (30 s) detects a dead connection, because a long-lived stream cannot carry a deadline.
  - *Three columns beyond the listed schema:* `step_skipped: Boolean` and `grad_scale: Float64` (written by `scaler=`, added after the schema was drafted), and `extra_json: Utf8`, which carries any key the schema does not know and any value that does not fit its column's type, so nothing is dropped and `row_to_record` returns the original dict. The `moe` field is the `moe_counts` column. Non-finite numbers are real NaN and infinity in the float columns and return to the strings `"nan"`, `"inf"`, `"-inf"` when read back.
  - *The server checks only the key columns* (`schema_version`, `run_id`, `rank`, `step`, and `attempt` when present) and the batch metadata, and writes whatever other columns arrive. A column added by a newer SDK then needs no server release; a schema change in mid-buffer starts a new part, because a part has one schema.
  - *The watermark is read when an attempt's first batch arrives*, from the footer of its newest part (by the last step in the file name; a part with a foreign name is always opened), not for every run at startup, which would list the whole store.
  - *Only the footer is fetched (decided Nov 14, 2026).* One suffix range request of 64 KiB, a bounded range on stores that refuse suffix requests, and a second read only for a larger footer; page indexes are skipped, since the row-group statistics hold the step range. The server's own parts measured 7.2–7.7 KiB of footer on router-only and full captures, so 8 KiB, the first choice, left about 500 bytes; DataFusion's default for the same reader call is 512 KiB, which also covers page indexes. Before this, each part opened was downloaded in full.
  - *Quota.* Buffered bytes are the rows' own Arrow bytes (`get_slice_memory_size`): `get_array_memory_size` counts a decoded message's shared buffer once per column. A batch that does not fit first writes that rank's own buffer; if it still does not fit, the reply is `RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED`. A batch larger than the whole quota is accepted when nothing else is buffered, so it cannot wait forever. Messages up to 64 MiB are decoded.
  - *Setup errors raise at `init`; failures while running never do.* A bad endpoint scheme, a missing `pyarrow`, or `endpoint=` with a sink that has no file raises before training starts. After that the sender only records `last_error`, backs off and retries, and `close()` prints the local path if bytes are unconfirmed. `tensorquill.flight.send_file` sends what a finished run still owes.
  - *Complete lines that are not step records of this run are skipped and counted* (`skipped_lines`), which is what a killed writer leaves when the next process appends after its half line.
  - *`run_id` on the server is limited to letters, digits, `-`, `_` and `.`*, because it becomes a directory name; other IDs are refused with `INVALID_ARGUMENT`.
  - *On `SIGTERM` the server does not wait for open streams*, which never end on their own: it refuses new batches, writes every buffer and exits.
  - *Consequence of watermark deduplication, handled Nov 14, 2026 by attempts (see above):* a run restarted from an earlier step under the same `run_id` used to have its repeated step numbers dropped by the server.
- [x] **Where detectors run (decided Nov 14, 2026):** detectors run in Python (`sdk/src/tensorquill/evidence.py`) on local `.jsonl` or live server Parquet (`python -m tensorquill --watch`), and will only be ported into the Rust server if the 64-rank benchmark exceeds 250 ms per 50-step flush or 1 CPU core. *Benchmarked Nov 15, 2026: at Granite's shape the Python detectors exceed both limits, so the condition is met, and the port began the same day (see the entry on live detection in Rust).*
- [x] **Who explains failures (decided Nov 14, 2026):** v1 hands `evidence.json` and Rerun screenshots to the team's own coding agent; a built-in VLM explainer is postponed.
- [x] **Tier 3 activation replay and MLflow scalar importer (decided Nov 14, 2026):** both postponed from v1 (revisit the MLflow importer only if ≥3 engineers join).
- [x] **Data-wait tests hold what is true by construction (decided Nov 17, 2026).** `test_with_loader_workers` failed once in CI (the second batch's wait was 8 ms against a 10 ms floor) and 8 of 8 times on a Mac (Python 3.13, torch 2.15, spawn). The timer was right. A `DataLoader` worker fetches the next batch while the loop runs the step before, so when the first step is slow the batch is already waiting and the loop really does not wait for it. The test now holds three things: each wait is above zero and no longer than the test's own stopwatch around that `next()`; and since one worker hands over batch `k` no sooner than `k` fetches after the loader starts, the waits add up to at least the fetch time minus the time the loop itself was busy. The tests for counting twice and for other threads and the eval phase now also hold each wait to the test's own stopwatch, not a fixed number of milliseconds. A timer reading zero, counting twice, or ignoring the thread, phase or `_fetching` checks each fails a test. Open: the upper bound in `_assert_fetch_timed` (fetch time below `FETCH + MOVE/2` per batch, used by the watch, `time_data`, Lightning, Accelerate and Hugging Face tests) still depends on how long a sleep overshoots on a loaded runner. A replacement, `step_time − data_wait ≥ MOVE` on each step after the first, held on the watch and Lightning runs, but it was not tried on Accelerate or Hugging Face, which do not run locally.
- [x] **The data-wait move check no longer depends on runner speed (decided Nov 17, 2026).** This closes the open item of the entry above. `_assert_fetch_timed` in `sdk/tests/test_datawait.py` (used by the watch, `time_data`, Accelerate, Lightning and Hugging Face Trainer tests) no longer caps the waits at `FETCH + MOVE/2` per batch, which a loaded runner's late sleeps could overshoot. It now requires, on every step after the first, `step_time − data_wait ≥ moves × MOVE`: each step sleeps that long after the loader hands the batch over, so that much is outside `next()` however slow the machine, and a load only adds to it. The fetch floor and `step_time ≥ data_wait` stay. The Hugging Face test's steps hold two microbatches (`moves=2`), and the plain-iterable half of the `time_data` test now sleeps `MOVE` per batch too. Checked from outside the product with a pytest plugin: a timer that also counts the move, or the whole step, fails all five tests inside the helper (the `time_data` one first at its own stopwatch check, and inside the helper when only its second half is faulty). The file passed 50 of 50 runs with no added load and 50 of 50 beside 8 CPU-spinning processes on an 8-core Mac (Python 3.12, torch 2.15, accelerate 1.16, transformers 5.23, lightning 2.6.6), none skipped; other sessions' tests shared the machine during the unloaded runs. Limits: the first record has no `step_time` and is not checked this way; on a loaded machine a faulty timer can slip past on one step that has a `MOVE` or more of other time (a Lightning step that runs validation), but not on all of them. The other timing checks in the file are floors, the test's own stopwatch, or a 10 s hang guard, and only get looser under load. Not run on Linux.
- [x] **The thresholds line is printed for a local file (built Nov 17, 2026 in a side session).** Closes the open item of Nov 17, 2026 "the thresholds line is not printed for a local file" (item 6 of "A stall at critical fires the trigger").
  - *What was missing.* Before this, no output of `python -m tensorquill` on a local `.jsonl` file printed the line when a threshold flag was off the default: not one read, `--rank 0`, `--compact`, or `--watch`, and not with the Python detectors or with `--detectors rust`. A read of a store's parts without `--watch` did not print it either. All of these go through `build_evidence`, whose evidence has no `detection` key and kept three of the five options as keys of their own, without `drift_tolerance` or the `top_k` that was given. `--json` does not exist, and `--rank all` refuses a file.
  - *What was built.* When one of the five options is not the default, `build_evidence` lists all five it ran with as `thresholds`. That is the name the server's report gives them, and the key has the shape of `detection.thresholds`, with the values that were asked for. The Rust path already refuses a binary that applied other values. `thresholds_line` reads `detection.thresholds` when the evidence has a `detection` key, and otherwise this key, as `the detectors ran here with ...`. No command-line code changed: every output already prints `incident_summary`.
  - *At the defaults nothing changed.* Evidence at the defaults, including evidence with an option typed at its default value, has no key and prints no line. The Gate 1 and bake-off outputs (`harness.py build` and `generate.py`, 73 `.json` and `.txt` files) were made before and after this change and are byte-identical. The golden files freeze other keys and pass.
  - *Tests.* `sdk/tests/test_thresholds_line.py` now has 20 tests (it had 12). The new tests cover the evidence key for each option and for all five, and `top_k` per router. They also cover one read, `--rank 0` and `--compact`, each with flags off the defaults (the line once, before the run state) and at them (none typed, and each typed at its default value: the same stdout and the same evidence bytes). They cover `--watch` and `--watch --compact`, the Rust detectors in one read, `--rank 0`, `--compact` and `--watch` (the same line and key as the Python ones; not `--watch --compact`), and a read of a store's parts without `--watch`. Without pyarrow, 15 pass and 5 skip; the 5 need pyarrow and the server binary, and the file is already in CI's server job. One assertion was changed on purpose: the old one held that a local file with options off the defaults gives no line.
  - *Run, macOS, this worktree's release binary.* Python, the whole suite in `sdk/`: 1,360 passed. The 81 experiment tests passed.
  - *Open.*
    - Evidence at the defaults does not list its thresholds. Its file says the defaults applied only by having no key, which is also what a file from before the key looks like.
    - A read of a store without `--watch`, and `--watch --recompute`, run the detectors here with the defaults or with the flags typed. They do not take the thresholds the server applied, as `--watch` does. Their evidence has no `detection` key, so the line comes from the new key: it is right about what ran here, but the read can disagree with the server's report.
    - Evidence judged here with a server's non-default thresholds now also has the top-level key. This covers a watcher whose report cannot be used (`missing`, `stale`, `other_thresholds`), which also has `detection.thresholds`; the line reads `detection` first and prints once. It also covers `compare` on a rank whose report cannot be used (`compare.py` passes the applied thresholds to `build_evidence`). The `compare` tests pass, but `compare` was not run against such a store.
  - *Not run:* Linux and CI; `cargo test` (no Rust file changed).
- [x] **The trigger and the watchdog: one firing for one stall, a lost connection, compact evidence, a fresh window after a restart (decided by the author Nov 17, 2026; built the same day in a side session).** Branch `claude/trigger-watchdog-706634`, on `main` at `19b82ec`. In `sdk/src/tensorquill/trigger.py`, `detect_watchdog_incidents` and the stall text in `evidence.py`, and `server/src/watchdog.rs`. Reference repos read first: `wandb`'s `run.alert` holds an alert back by its title within a run, not by the step (taken: key a stall by its run); `torch.distributed.elastic` fails a worker group as a unit when one worker dies and reports a dead process by its exit signal, apart from a hang, and drops a rendezvous node whose keep-alive has expired (taken: the lost rank labels a job that stops together); `mlflow`'s webhooks carry a delivery id (already used); `lightning` only requeues on a SLURM signal, nothing on a restart window.
    1. **One firing for one stall.** The trigger keys `job_stalled` and `rank_silent` by the run, not by rank, kind and step; other kinds keep their key. A silent rank that becomes a stalled job, and a second rank that falls silent at an earlier step, fire nothing more. A `*.fired.json` of the earlier version loads unchanged (`STATE_VERSION` 1): any stall it lists is the run's stall, so an upgrade fires nothing again. A stall named with `--fire-kind` that fired at `warning` does not fire at `critical`, nor does the `job_stalled` a named `rank_silent` becomes. A try still to make for a stall sends the stall as it reads now, under the firing's own id. *Open:* a run that stalls, fires, steps again and stalls a second time fires nothing for the second stall under this rule; not decided. Also open: a state file of the earlier version that holds two stall firings with tries still to make sends both, each as the current stall. A mixed `job_stalled` read as `lost_connection` is no longer upgraded to `confirmed_hang` by a Flight Recorder dump (the stuck collective is then the survivors waiting). An `unresponsive` rank's verdict held over a restart is forgotten if the server restarts again inside the window. *(Changed Nov 17, 2026 by the author: one firing per stall episode, not per run; see Decisions: "One firing per stall episode".)*
    2. **A killed job fires, labelled as a lost connection.** No new kind (still 32; golden files and Gate 1 fixtures unchanged). The label is the stall's existing `assessment`, with a new `lost_ranks` field and summary text: `lost_connection` reads "The connection of rank N dropped and did not come back: read as a lost connection, not a hang", names an out-of-memory kill as the likely cause, and drops the sentence that a hang and a killed job look the same. *The mix, decided here:* a stall reads as a lost connection when a lost rank explains it: every stopped rank is lost, or the whole job stopped (`job_stalled`) and at least one rank is lost, since connected ranks of a job that stops together wait for the one that is gone (an OOM-killed rank and the others blocked in a collective was a `suspected_hang` before). A lost rank beside a silent one while other ranks step stays `suspected_hang`, with the lost rank in `lost_ranks`. Python and Rust build it alike (a Rust test and a Python test on the same cases; a real-server test compares `status["incidents"]` with `detect_watchdog_incidents(status)`). The command gets `TENSORQUILL_INCIDENT_ASSESSMENT`, the JSON document a top-level `assessment` (null for other incidents; payload version still 1), and the trigger's log line says `(lost connection)`.
    3. **The trigger turns on `--compact`.** On the command line, `--on-incident` or `--webhook` with no `--compact` keeps writing the full evidence as before, and the trigger writes `<name>.compact.json` beside it on every look and hands that file over: `TENSORQUILL_EVIDENCE` and the document's `evidence` name the compact file, because the agent that is woken should read the small one and the full file must stay for everything else. Built in `trigger.py` (`Trigger(compact_window=...)`, set by `from_args`), so the watcher's own writes are untouched. With `--compact` given, the watcher writes the compact file alone, as before, and that is handed over. A compact file that cannot be written is said once and the full file is handed over. The state file's name is unchanged. Checked with `--rank all --watch --webhook` against a real server and a local listener.
    4. **A fresh window after a server restart.** This changes the logged rule "a warning given before the first batch keeps its clock over a server restart" (the Nov 16 entry "A rank that never stores a batch ...", item 3) and the Nov 15 rule that a restored stopped rank keeps its quiet time: now every restored rank that was not finished is judged from the restart. A rank that has shown no training step gets `--start-seconds`; one that has stepped gets its own silence threshold (`--silent-seconds`, or its slower cadence), chosen over the start window because its process went on through the restart and has only to reconnect (the sender's backoff is capped at 30 s) and send its next batch, which the threshold already allows for, while the start window is sized for compilation and data loading it does not do again. Built as a restart instant that no verdict clock starts before; the clocks the status shows (`seconds_since_progress`, `phase_seconds`, pauses) are kept. Inside the window a rank whose sender has not come back is `reconnecting`; after it `lost`, or `unresponsive` if its heartbeats had already stopped (a frozen process cannot reconnect, and is not a lost connection), and a rank that never stepped is critical `--critical-seconds` after its window. *The cost, with the defaults (pinned by a Rust test on a made clock with `WatchdogConfig::default()`):* a hang or a killed job that spans a restart is called later by the time it had already been quiet: a rank hung or killed 800 s before the restart (a warning) is critical 900 s after it instead of 100 s, 800 s later; a rank that never stepped is critical 2,700 s after the restart. A stall that was already critical (and fired) reads healthy for the window, and a server that restarts more often than the window never calls a hang.
    - *Verified* on macOS with Python 3.12 and this branch's release server: `cargo test` 88 unit and 31 integration tests pass (1 ignored, as before), clippy with `-D warnings` and `cargo fmt --check` clean; the whole Python suite with the experiments' 81 tests: 1,438 passed and 1 failed (`test_object_store_e2e.py`'s restart test, which pinned the old restart rule; rewritten, that file passed alone, 3 tests); on the final tree (`54d7173`) the whole run again: 1,439 passed, 0 failed; with `pyarrow` hidden and no server binary, the four changed test files pass or skip (46 passed, 13 skipped). Tests changed because they pinned the rules replaced here: in `test_trigger.py` the stall that fired again on a change of kind or step, in `test_watchdog.py` a mixed stall read as a suspected hang, and every restart test (`watchdog.rs`, `server/tests/ingest_test.rs`, `test_object_store_e2e.py`). New: in `test_trigger.py` (28 tests, 5 new) the run key, an old state file, the compact hand-over, and three real-server tests: a silent rank that becomes a stalled job posts once to a `--rank all --watch --webhook` listener (compact file named, full file kept); a rank killed with SIGKILL beside a live one reads as a lost connection and starts the command once, Rust's and Python's incidents equal; a hung rank at `warning` across a SIGKILL restart of the server fires nothing for 9 s and once at critical, 12 s after the restart. The last two fail against the server binary built before this change. In `watchdog.rs`, four new tests (the mix rule, the fresh window before a first batch, an unresponsive rank after its window, the cost at the default timings). Golden files and the Gate 1 fixtures are unchanged.
    - *Not verified:* the default timings (300, 900, 1,800 s) have never been run for real; the real-server tests use 2 s and 6 s, 3 s and 12 s. Linux and CI (macOS only). A real out-of-memory kill (SIGKILL from the test stands in for it), a real stuck collective, a real agent, an outside webhook. *For the merge:* no new test file; the three new real-server tests are in `tests/test_trigger.py`, already in CI's server job.
- [x] **A checkpointed block under `torch.compile(backend="eager")` no longer fails `backward()` (decided and built Nov 17, 2026 in a side session).** Closes the finding "activation checkpointing of a block holding a hooked module, inside `torch.compile(backend=\"eager\")`, raises into training", logged on branch `claude/strange-keller-5aae46` (`b8bc492`).
  - *Why it raised.* Dynamo turns `torch.utils.checkpoint.checkpoint`, reentrant or not, into one `tag_activation_checkpoint` region, and the hook's buffer, read from its closure, becomes one of the region's tensor inputs. Under the eager backend PyTorch runs that region with non-reentrant checkpointing (`torch/_higher_order_ops/wrap.py` forces `use_reentrant=False`), which saves every tensor input with its version (`_CheckpointFrame.save_inputs`). The hook's in-place write (`counts.add_`, `buf[row] = ...`) bumped the version, so unpacking the inputs for recomputation raised. Under `aot_eager` and Inductor, AOTAutograd moves the write out of the region: the forward graph computes the new counts functionally and returns them for the runtime to copy back, and the backward graph never recomputes them (read in the AOT graphs), so nothing is saved.
  - *A second fault behind the first.* The eager backend replays the region in backward from the graph, where `_recomputing()` had been decided once at trace time. Hiding the version alone made the counts come out doubled (`[8, 6, 26, 24]` instead of `[4, 3, 13, 12]`), and with two forwards before one backward `capture="full"` kept the first forward's statistics.
  - *The fix (`capture._add_compiled`, `capture._set_row_compiled`).* Under compilation each hook's write goes through a function marked `torch.compiler.allow_in_graph`: one opaque node for Dynamo, so no graph break. When the graph runs as is, the function gets the real buffer: it writes and then sets the buffer's version back (`torch._C._autograd._unsafe_set_version_counter`, what `torch.autograd._unsafe_preserve_version_counter` does; nothing differentiable reads the buffer), and it skips the write while the autograd engine runs, as `_recomputing()` does in eager mode. `_commit` sets the versions of the activation and counts buffers back the same way after zeroing them, for an optimizer step taken between a forward and its backward (that raised too, before and after the first version of this fix). The version calls are best effort: if the private API is missing, the version stays bumped, as before. No device work is added, so no sync. When AOTAutograd traces it, the buffer is a fake or functional tensor and the function does exactly the write the hook did before. The uncompiled hook path is unchanged. These are the only two in-place writes a compiled forward hook makes: the probe's hooks do nothing under compilation, and no other module registers forward hooks.
  - *Unchanged where it worked.* With `attempt`, `ts`, `run_id`, `system` and timing keys removed, the records, the parameters after three steps, and the graph, graph-break and frame counts are identical to `3e63842` in all 50 working combinations of five setups (default router, `indices=` router, `capture="full"`, gradient accumulation of two with full capture and a router, two hooked routers in one block; the `indices=` function detaches the router's output), three checkpoint settings (none, reentrant, non-reentrant) and four backends (none, eager, aot_eager, Inductor on CPU). The AOT forward and backward graphs are textually identical, and Inductor's generated code is identical apart from log timestamps. The ten eager-backend combinations that raised now give the uncompiled run's records and parameters exactly; so does `aot_eager`. No new warning.
  - *Inductor was not affected* (run on CPU with the system C++ compiler): no raise and the uncompiled counts before the fix.
  - *Tests.* In `test_review_fixes.py`, 16 cases. `test_checkpointed_hooks_under_compile_record_what_eager_records` (12): eager and aot_eager, reentrant and non-reentrant, default router, `indices=` router and `capture="full"`, each step two forwards before one backward, compared with the uncompiled run and with no graph break. `test_a_step_between_a_checkpointed_forward_and_its_backward_under_compile` (4): an optimizer step between the forward and its backward, routers and full capture together. On `3e63842` the eight eager cases raise; with only the hook's version hidden the first test fails on doubled counts and on the first forward's statistics, and the second still raises. The whole Python suite passes on macOS with Python 3.12 and PyTorch 2.15 against a release server built from this branch: 1,397 passed, none skipped.
  - *Not verified:* PyTorch 2.6, the floor (nothing ran there, and whether its private version call takes tuples was not checked; the code falls back to one tensor at a time, then to no change), CUDA, and `mode="reduce-overhead"` (the identical AOT graphs are the only evidence that CUDA graphs are unaffected). Selective checkpointing with a user `context_fn` (saving `mm` and `addmm`) was checked by hand, not in a test: it raised the same way under the eager backend on `3e63842`, and now gives the uncompiled counts and statistics under eager and aot_eager. *PyTorch 2.6 was run on Nov 17, 2026 and failed under `aot_eager`: see "A checkpointed hooked block under compile on PyTorch 2.6".*
  - *Found on the way, not changed:* an `indices=` function that does not detach the router's output (`out.topk(2, dim=-1).indices`) makes non-reentrant checkpointing raise `CheckpointError` ("A different number of tensors was saved") in plain eager mode, on `3e63842` as after: the function saves tensors for backward in the forward, and the recomputation, where hooks are skipped, saves fewer. The default router path detaches for this reason; the `indices=` path does not.
- [x] **One firing per stall episode (decided by the author Nov 17, 2026, replacing "key a stall by run" of the same day; built the same day on `claude/trigger-watchdog-706634`).** *Why:* one firing per run, ever, is too coarse: a job started again that hangs later is a new failure and should wake the agent. A job that stalls, recovers and stalls again fires each time; each firing needs a fresh `--critical-seconds` (900 s) of silence, so a few times an hour at most, and a job that keeps stalling is worth knowing about.
    - *Built* in `sdk/src/tensorquill/trigger.py`, with one new field on `rank_silent` (`other_ranks`, Python and Rust) and one Rust test that pins what the status shows for a job started again. A stall that fires opens an episode, kept in `<evidence>.fired.json` under a new `episode` key (`STATE_VERSION` still 1): the kind that opened it, the firing's id, the run's highest stored step, and each stopped rank's last stored step and `attempt`. While it is open no stall fires. It is judged only when a stall is next listed, so a stall that is not listed for a while (the fresh window after a server restart) has not ended for that.
    - *"A step beyond the one it stalled at", as built.* An episode opened by `job_stalled` ends when the run's step (the highest `last_step` of the stall's ranks, or its `newest_step`) is beyond the run's highest step when it fired: a lagging rank whose late rows raise its own step below that does not end it. An episode opened by `rank_silent` ends when a rank of the episode has stored a step beyond the one it stopped at, whether it is still stopped or has recovered: so the trigger can see a recovered rank, `rank_silent` now lists the ranks that are not stopped under `other_ranks` (rank, state, last step, attempt), built alike in Python's `_progress_incidents` and Rust's `progress_incidents`. The ranks stepping beside a silent rank are not in the episode and never end it, however many looks; a rank that is only `reconnecting` (after a server restart) has not stepped. A rank that stops during an open episode joins it at the step it stopped at; once any rank of the episode steps on, the episode is over, so a first rank that recovers while a joined one is still stuck ends it, and the joined rank's stall then fires. A stall that fired before anything was stored (`last_step` null) ends with the first stored step. A silent rank whose job then stops altogether is one episode.
    - *A new attempt* is read from the server's status: the `attempt` of a rank of the episode, as the stall's `ranks` carry it, differs from the one recorded. The server sets it from the new attempt's first stored batch (rows below the old highest step count, and `last_step` stays the old highest), which also makes the rank active, so it cannot fire at once. A status without `attempt` (hand-made, or an older server) says nothing. *(Changed the same day: the server now learns the attempt from the new process's heartbeat, before it stores a step, and gives it a start window; and a new attempt ends an episode only when no rank of it is still stopped as it was. See the sub-entry "The gap against rule 3 closed".)* A watcher of a local file given a status with `watchdog=` reads the same fields from that status; the records' own `attempt` is not used. A server restart is not a new attempt and re-arms nothing (tested: a second restart after the firing, past the window and the critical time, fires nothing more).
    - *State files.* One keyed by rank, kind and step and one written by the run-keyed version both load: their last stall firing is the open episode, which takes the first stall it sees as its own baseline without firing; progress or a new attempt then re-arms it. Only the open episode's firing sends a try still to make, which closes the earlier open item "an old state file holding two pending stall firings would send both". A stall named with `--fire-kind` that fired at `warning` does not fire again at `critical` in its episode. A later episode's firing at the same step gets an id of its own (`.../job_stalled/99/2`), so a receiver that drops repeats does not drop it. The lost-connection label, the compact evidence and everything else are as merged; no new kind; golden files and Gate 1 fixtures unchanged.
    - *Open.* A job started again that hangs before it stores a step is not seen as a new attempt: heartbeats carry no attempt, and seeing it needs the sender (`flight.py`), the server's heartbeat (`ingest.rs`) and perhaps the capture. Until its first stored batch such a rank reads silent and critical at once from the old attempt's last batch (pinned by the Rust test `a_job_started_again_reads_stalled_until_its_new_attempt_stores_a_batch`); the trigger does not fire for it, since its status still shows the episode that fired. Giving a new attempt a start window there is a follow-up. *(Closed Nov 17, 2026: see the next sub-entry.)* Any rank of the episode that stays stuck (the first or one that joined) keeps the episode open while no rank of it steps on, so a later hang of another rank fires nothing in that time. `other_ranks` was checked on made statuses in Python and Rust; no real-server test compares Rust's and Python's `rank_silent` or runs the recovered-rank path on the server's own output, and a server built before this change writes no `other_ranks`, so there a recovered rank's later stall beside it is still not seen. The silent-rank-beside-stepping-ranks case over many looks is tested on made statuses; the real-server test stops the other rank soon after the post. After an upgrade from an earlier state file the first stall seen is taken as the open episode and never fires, even if the run had moved on since the listed firing. An episode whose firing the user removed from the state file (as the "given up" line says to do) or that cannot be read is dropped on load, so the stall may fire again.
    - *Verified* on macOS with Python 3.12 and this branch's release server, on `main` at `3e63842` merged in: the whole Python suite with the experiments' 81 tests, 1,481 passed, 0 failed; with `pyarrow` hidden and no server binary, `test_trigger.py` and `test_watchdog.py` 52 passed, 13 skipped; `cargo test` 89 unit and 31 integration (1 ignored), clippy `-D warnings` and fmt clean. `test_trigger.py` has 37 tests (9 new, among them a later stall of another rank after the first recovered, and a stall whose firing the user removed from the state file, which fires again): with made statuses, a job stall that fires once over many looks and a restart of the watcher, a lagging rank's late rows that do not end it, progress that re-arms it, a new attempt that re-arms it and a missing attempt that does not, a silent rank beside stepping ranks over 21 looks, a joined rank, a stall named at `warning`, and state files of both earlier formats with two pending firings; with a real server, a rank that stalls, steps 100 steps past it and stalls again fires twice, and a job killed and started again that resumes below the old step and hangs fires again (only after its first stored batch), and the restart test now restarts the server a second time after the firing and sees nothing more. The real-server new-attempt test also fires `repeated_steps` (an invariant), since the new attempt numbers its steps from 0 again; the test counts only the stalls. Mutation checks: with the three lines that end an episode neutralised, the seven re-arm tests fail at their second firing; with the hold disabled nine once-tests fail. *Not verified:* the default timings for real; Linux and CI; a real out-of-memory kill or GPU hang; a job started again that hangs before storing a step (not seen, by design above).
    - **The gap against rule 3 closed (Nov 17, 2026, at the reviewer's request; branch commits after `7c0e3b1`).** A job started again that hangs before it stores a step now fires. *Built:* the heartbeat names the session's attempt (`flight.Heartbeat`: read once, when the session hands over its work counter just after taking its attempt, and that beat is sent at once; a number more in each beat, nothing on the training thread). The server (`ingest.rs` parses it, `Watchdog::attempt_seen` in `watchdog.rs`) takes a higher attempt than the rank's as the job started again: the status's `attempt` changes at once and the rank is marked `attempt_without_steps` until the attempt stores a batch; until then it is judged as a rank before its first step from that beat (`starting` for `--start-seconds`, then `silent` at `warning`, critical `--critical-seconds` later; `reconnecting` for that window while its stream is not open), where before it read silent and critical at once from the earlier attempt's last batch. The trigger's episode re-arms on the changed attempt, so the hang fires once it is critical. The summary says the rank was started again and the step shown is the earlier attempt's. *Numbering:* the attempt is the session's start time in Unix milliseconds (`capture._next_attempt`), new for every `tensorquill.init`, whether the job restarts with no checkpoint (it numbers its steps from 0 again, which `repeated_steps` reports) or resumes from one; `last_step` stays the highest stored of any attempt. *Between the two processes:* the killed process's stream drops (`reconnecting`, then `lost` after `--silent-seconds`, with its old step, attempt and severity, the episode that fired). The new process's heartbeat thread holds its first beat until the session hands over its counter and names the attempt (at most `FIRST_BEAT_HOLD_SECONDS`, 2 s), and its stream's first connection waits for that beat (at most 3 s), so the server has the new attempt before the stream opens: `reconnecting` within its start window while the stream is not open yet, then `starting`. Without that hold a real restart showed about 0.3 s in which the rank read as an open stream of the old attempt, judged silent and critical from its last batch (the real-server test catches it). If the hold runs out (a session that takes longer than 2 s to start after its sink is made) that gap comes back for that long; an open episode holds it, and with none open a status written in it could fire. A new attempt seen while the stream is already open gets no reconnecting window: a kill before its first batch is `lost` after the floor from the drop (Rust test). *Kept apart:* a beat of the same attempt (a reconnect, the sender's backoff) is no new attempt; while the new attempt has stored nothing, a beat of a lower attempt (a process of the earlier attempt still alive) is ignored and an earlier attempt's rows sent late raise `last_step` and are not its progress (the trigger's `job_stalled` rule does read that raised `last_step` as the run's progress); the attempt's first gap is measured from its first beat; a server restart keeps the mark (`attempt_without_steps` in `restart`; a status without it restores as an attempt with steps). *The trigger:* a new attempt now ends an episode only when no rank of it is still stopped exactly as it was, so a multi-rank job whose ranks come back a look apart does not fire the old stall of a rank not yet started again (that was possible at `7c0e3b1` too, when one rank's first batch was stored a look before another's; fixed in `3805b69`). *Cost with the defaults:* such a hang is critical, and fires, 2,700 s after the new process's first heartbeat (1,800 s start window, then 900 s), plus up to 10 s for the server's look and 2 s for the watcher's; one that took training steps it had not stored yet follows its work counter (critical 900 s after the counter stopped, or three of its thresholds). *Compatibility:* a server older than this ignores the field (serde does not refuse unknown keys), so a new SDK streams and is watched as before; a sender older than this names no attempt and is watched as before, its new attempt known from its first stored batch. *Costs and limits.* A job that crash-loops faster than its window without storing a batch is never called critical now, since each new attempt starts the window again; before, each new process read critical at once from the old progress and the first fired. A restart in which one rank of the episode never comes back does not re-arm by attempt (that rank is still stopped as it was). Attempts are wall-clock milliseconds, so a restart on a host whose clock is behind the earlier one's has a lower number: it is not seen as a new attempt before it stores a step (watched as a sender without attempts), and its beats and batches are taken as before, so it reads active as it steps (Rust test). Beats and rows of a lower attempt are set aside only while a new attempt has stored nothing. *Open:* with heartbeats off (`heartbeat_seconds=None`) or an older sender the gap remains. The attempt reaches the heartbeat through `capture._last_attempt` read as the session sets the counter, because `capture.py` was being edited elsewhere: one line there handing the attempt to the heartbeat is its proper home. *Verified* on macOS with Python 3.12 and this branch's release server: the whole Python suite with the experiments' 81 tests, 1,488 passed, 0 failed; with `pyarrow` hidden and no server binary, `test_trigger.py`, `test_watchdog.py` and `test_flight.py` 55 passed, 18 skipped; `cargo test` 94 unit and 31 integration (1 ignored), clippy `-D warnings` and fmt clean. New: three Rust tests (a new attempt's window from its first beat, with a reconnect, an earlier attempt's beat and late rows; the mark kept over a server restart and a status without it; critical 2,700 s after the first beat with the defaults), the no-attempt case kept as a pin; in `test_trigger.py` a staggered restart (made statuses), the beat's attempt equal to the session's rows, and two real-server tests: a job killed and started again that never steps fires once, after 4 + 6 s, not inside its window, with the new attempt in the payload; and an older sender (heartbeats patched to name no attempt, as the manifest lane simulated an older sender) streams and fires once, with no new attempt seen. The hang-before-first-step test runs with `--rank all` and with one rank's watcher, and asserts no status after the drop shows an open stream of the old attempt (with the hold removed it fails, a 0.3 s gap); a Rust test pins a new attempt seen while its stream is open and killed before its first batch. The final SDK against a server built from `7c0e3b1` (archived and built in a scratch folder, not committed): the stored-step, step-past-the-stall and killed-rank tests pass, and the hang-before-first-step test (both views) fails there, as it must (that server ignores the attempt). "Old-format status" was read as a status written before `attempt_without_steps` existed: it restores as an attempt with steps (Rust test). The 2 s hold was exercised with a one-layer model; how long `init` takes between making its sink and handing over its counter on a large model is not measured. *Not verified:* a truly older SDK checkout as the sender (simulated by the patch, which sends the same payload); the default timings for real; Linux and CI; a real multi-rank restart of processes started apart (made statuses only).

The freeze's own condition was not met: no real job has shown an uncaught failure, and results 1 and 2 are still not run. This is a change of plan, not a met condition.

Per-token routing entropy needs its cost under `torch.compile` measured first. Output-logit growth in the head waits for numbers from real runs. Slow ranks would be an observation. Hidden expert collapse has one case report and needs expert outputs, which nothing captures.

The line that began "The first candidates when the freeze lifts are hidden expert collapse ..." was replaced at the author's request the same day (revision 63) by the build order below, which puts hidden expert collapse last, as the review does.

- [x] **Second batch of papers: what it changes in the build list (Nov 18, 2026).** The papers session read twelve more PDFs added to `researchpapers/` and wrote section 7 of `Research papers and SageMaker Debugger review.md`. The author asked for its points to be applied to the build list. Checked here against the PDF text before use: the figures quoted below from CCL-D, OpGuard, AEGIS, SDCs in the Wild, Alibaba's Aegis and OLMoE. The other six papers were not checked here.
  - *K2, the cross-rank compare, needs its design chosen before it starts.* The row said to compare a per-layer activation statistic across data-parallel ranks and name the rank far from the median. Ranks see different batches, so that compare needs a tolerance, and a tolerance loses precision: OpGuard placed the fault exactly in 7 of 20 cases at a tolerance of `1e-3` without determinism controls (OSDI '26, p. 13). SDCHunter is not an online detector: it replays the alerting iteration after an alert (Zheng et al., OSDI '26). Two designs within reach of a hook library: every rank runs the same probe batch and their block statistics are compared (*corrected Nov 18, 2026:* not only the review's reading; Ma et al. propose a shadow replica that takes one rank's inputs and compares gradients before the reduction, proposed and not built as a detector), or a forward output is compared with its recomputation in the backward pass, which AEGIS does with retained and recomputed activations (Lei et al., OSDI '26). AEGIS's third mechanism, a checksum from the float32 accumulator inside the matmul kernel, is out of reach.
  - *K6, slow ranks, is reachable for compute-slow only* (*corrected Nov 18, 2026:* "half" was wrong; the share depends on the cluster, see the next entry). Compute-slow: the group whose communication time is under 0.8 of the typical is the slow computer (Alibaba's Aegis, NSDI '25, α = 0.8), which Flight Recorder's per-collective durations can show. Communication-slow and hardware hangs need send and receive counters inside the communication library, which CCL-D and Aegis both customise.
  - *A hang has more than one root-cause rule.* In CCL-D the rank that did not enter the collective is the root cause for 11.8% of hangs; for 58.9% the ranks that are not hanging are; for 29.3% it is hardware (arXiv 2605.04478). TensorQuill's `collective_hang` reports what PyTorch's analyzer finds and claims no rule of its own, so nothing changes in the product; the agent instructions should not add such a rule.
  - *K1 is not a corruption detector.* Added to the K1 prototype note, with Zheng et al.'s figures.
  - *The 300-second silence default has a precedent, on one cluster* (*corrected Nov 18, 2026*). CCL-D bounds a hang at five minutes because "97% of cases exceeding this limit cannot recover", and says the bound "reflects checkpoint and normal barrier durations" there. It is not a general source: a checkpoint elsewhere can take far longer. The default is safe because the check is paused during a marked phase (`sdk/README.md`, "Evaluation and checkpoints are not hangs").
  - *The compile claim stays narrow.* OpGuard's wrappers "preserve the original execution mode (eager, torch.compile, CUDA Graphs)". Added to the gap paragraph.
  - *The nanoMoE proof run has a precedent,* OLMoE's collapse without the balance loss. Added to row M2.
  - *Not applied.* The untracked `Training failure research survey.md` is the author's and is not committed; the review lists seven places where it goes further than its sources (*corrected Nov 18, 2026 from nine:* on Ma et al.'s false positives at bf16 and on OLMoE's second expert the survey was right), so anything built from the survey should carry the review's corrections. The review's other points (a second spike definition from OLMo 2, MaxVio as an MoE balance measure, two more bug sets for M1, the bit-for-bit point for the `1e-6` drift tolerance) are not in a build row yet. The focused plan is not changed: its competitor table has no OpGuard, AEGIS or CCL-D row, which is the author's to add.
- [x] **Review corrections after the full read (Nov 18, 2026).** The papers session read all 34 papers page by page (one, on INT8 inference, only in part) and added sections 8 and 9 to `Research papers and SageMaker Debugger review.md` (commit `2fe4a91`). It found that section 7 had been written from first pages and keyword search, and that four of its statements copied into the entry above were wrong or too loose. Checked here before use: nine quotes and figures against the PDF text (Ma et al., CCL-D, Sha, Chen et al., Wang et al., TrainSDC, Greyhound, OLMoE), and the router-count script, run on `main`.
  - *Corrected in the build list.* K7 has no paper behind it (*superseded in part the same day:* Motif 3 and Omi et al. are sources for its design and statistic; what stands is that Sha does not support it and that no run is reported to fail this way; Decisions: "Review sections 9 to 12"). K5 judges the gap between BF16 and FP32 logits, not growth: the logit norm "grows under every AdamW baseline, including FP32 ... but it destabilizes training only in reduced precision" (Chen et al., arXiv 2610.38277, p. 2). K2's same-input design is Ma et al.'s proposal, and a mismatch between a forward output and its recomputation also comes from software, so it is reported as "bug or hardware". K6 covers compute-slow only, and how much that is depends on the cluster.
  - *Corrected in the entry above, in place and marked:* the K2 and K6 sentences, the 300-second default, and the count of survey overstatements (seven, not nine).
  - *Router counts can be the wrong routing (the review's F1; reproduced here on `main`).* For a router that returns scores, the hook counts the top-k of the hooked tensor. Where the model adds something to the scores before choosing, such as the per-expert bias of loss-free balancing (Wang et al., arXiv 2408.15664), the counts are not the routing. On the review's toy the real routing was 1,703 / 1,406 / 1,003 / 1,008 tokens and TensorQuill recorded 5,120 / 0 / 0 / 0 and raised `moe_imbalance` with three dead experts: a wrong incident on a healthy model. The review reports that an `indices=` function applying the bias records the right counts; that half was not run here. No real model was tested. New row S7.
  - *For K1, on lane C's branch and to be checked at its merge.* The evidence should carry the update's RMS over the learning rate beside the expected steady value, `sqrt((1-β1)/(1+β1))`, which is 0.229 at β1 = 0.9 (Rotational Equilibrium, Table 1; the review's toy saw it hold only while the loss was high). The cost must not be quoted as 1%: TrainSDC re-implemented the detector with replay and measured 11.2% to 12.1% (arXiv 2608.30769, Table 1). The review also names a test that needs no injected fault (a slingshot spike on modular division, Liu et al.) and the original authors' public injector, which needs a GPU.
  - *The drift check (the review's F2).* On one 1,000 by 1,000 float32 matrix, a flip of a low mantissa bit moved the fingerprint by 7e-13 to 3e-9 of the norm, far under the `1e-6` tolerance, and an integer checksum of the raw bits differed every time. So "the replicas are not bit-for-bit equal" should be reported as its own fact. It goes with D1. The review's measurement was not repeated here.
  - *Wording for the agent instructions and README, not written yet.* New row S8.
  - *Not applied.* The focused plan's build-order line still names "output-logit growth" and "hidden expert collapse" as kinds; it is the author's to change. The review's count of TrainCheck bugs TensorQuill might catch (up to seven of 20, untested) is not in a row. Nothing here was run on a GPU or a real model.

- [x] **Server wheels (built Nov 17, 2026; nothing uploaded, and the workflow has not run).** Part 2 of "Packaging and the release plan", the wheel half: `tensorquill-server` is packaged with maturin, a workflow builds and checks the wheels, and the `crate` CI job is gone. The protocol number, the SDK's lookup of the binary and its dependency on this package are other work and are not in this entry.
  - **What was added.**
    - `server/pyproject.toml`: maturin with `bindings = "bin"`, so the wheel's content is the compiled program, as a script. The distribution is `tensorquill-server`, `Apache-2.0 OR MIT`, with both license files, no authors and no URLs. `server/README.md` is new and is the PyPI page text.
    - `server/packaging/fallback_wheel.py` makes the fallback wheel; `check_wheel.py` checks a wheel file, or which wheel pip installed; `testpypi_install.sh` is the container test. All three use the standard library or plain shell.
    - `.github/workflows/server-wheels.yml`: jobs `build` (three platforms), `fallback`, `install` and `publish`.
  - **Departures from the task as given.**
    - `sdk/tests/test_packaging.py` gains one test that pins the server's wheel metadata to the crate's, and the no-contact-metadata test now reads `server/pyproject.toml` too. The task named only the comment in that file.
    - `server/Cargo.toml`: its comment said there is no server README and gave `cargo package` as the reason for the license list. A README now exists, so the comment is reworded. Nothing else in the file changed.
    - `server/README.md` was not asked for. Without it the PyPI page is empty, and it is where a user reads what the fallback is.
    - `twine` was installed into the same throwaway venv as maturin to run `twine check`. The author's yes named maturin only.
  - **Decisions.**
    - *Version.* The number is written once, in `server/Cargo.toml`. `dynamic = ["version"]` makes maturin read it from there, and `check_wheel.py` fails a wheel whose version is not the crate's. The fallback wheel copies its `METADATA` from a built wheel, so it cannot carry another number. How maturin turns a Cargo pre-release (`0.1.0-alpha.1`) into a Python version was not checked: use plain `x.y.z` numbers until it is.
    - *What the fallback wheel holds.* `METADATA`, the two license files, `WHEEL` and `RECORD`: five files, about 6 KB, tagged `py3-none-any`. It has no program and no stand-in script. A stand-in named `tensorquill-server` was considered and rejected: the SDK would find a file with the program's name that is not the program, and every caller would have to tell the two apart.
    - *What a user on such a platform sees* (Windows, Intel macOS, Alpine and other musl Linux, glibc older than 2.17). `pip install tensorquill-server`, or the SDK that depends on it, succeeds. There is no `tensorquill-server` command. The SDK can tell this case from "not installed": `importlib.metadata.distribution("tensorquill-server").read_text("WHEEL")` has the line `Tag: py3-none-any`. The README says to build from source with `cargo build --release`.
    - *The fallback is made from a built wheel, not built on its own.* Its `METADATA` is then byte for byte the platform wheels'. The `fallback` job makes it from each of the three wheels and fails unless the three results are the same file.
    - *No sdist is published.* With a `py3-none-any` wheel in the release pip never reaches a source archive, and one would need its own contents check.
    - *`requires-python = ">=3.8"`.* The wheel holds no Python. The floor is looser than the SDK's 3.10 so that it can never block an SDK install, and the new test fails if it becomes tighter.
    - *`manylinux_2_17` by building inside the `manylinux2014` image* (glibc 2.17), which is what `PyO3/maturin-action` does with `manylinux: 2014`, and not with `--zig`. `rerun` builds in a manylinux container too (`reusable_build_and_upload_wheels.yml`, at `manylinux_2_28`). arm64 uses the `ubuntu-24.04-arm` runner; the action's image table maps an arm64 host with an `aarch64` target to `quay.io/pypa/manylinux2014_aarch64`, so nothing is emulated.
    - *The `crate` job is removed, not replaced inside `ci.yml`.* Its replacement is the new workflow. `cargo package` and `cargo publish --dry-run` are no longer run anywhere. `Cargo.toml` keeps its `include` list; only its comment changed.
    - *The comment in `test_packaging.py`.* The assertion message said `license` with `license-file` confuses the crates.io check. That is still true of crates.io but is no longer the reason, so the message now says the license is the SPDX expression alone. The assertion is unchanged.
    - *Publishing.* The `publish` job runs only when the workflow is started by hand with `publish` set to `testpypi` or `pypi`, and `pypi` only from `main`. It uses `pypa/gh-action-pypi-publish` with `id-token: write` on that job alone and no secret. It was not started.
  - **pip takes the platform wheel over the fallback.** Read in the project venv (pip 26.3 and the copy of `packaging` 26.3 it carries in `pip/_vendor`):
    - `pip/_vendor/packaging/tags.py`, `compatible_tags` (line 605; the two `yield` lines are 635 and 637) yields `py3x-none-<platform>` for every platform before any `-none-any` tag, and `sys_tags` puts those after the interpreter's own tags.
    - `pip/_internal/index/package_finder.py`, `CandidateEvaluator._sort_key` (line 516; the tag position is taken at line 556 and returned at 577) ranks a wheel by the position of its best tag in that list, lower first, when the versions are equal.
    - Measured on this Mac with pip 26.3's copy: `py3-none-macosx_11_0_arm64` is at position 690 and `py3-none-any` at 1271 of 1284; for a Linux x86_64 list, `py3-none-manylinux_2_17_x86_64` is at 3 and `py3-none-any` at 44.
    - Run with both wheels in one directory and `--no-index --find-links`: pip 24.0 and pip 26.3 take the macOS wheel here; `pip download --platform` takes the fallback for `win_amd64`, `macosx_10_12_x86_64` and `musllinux_1_2_x86_64`, and the macOS wheel for `macosx_14_0_arm64`.

  - *Protocol number (the author's rule: "The binary reports one protocol number, and the SDK checks it").*
    - `tensorquill-server protocol` prints one whole number on a line and exits; it is `PROTOCOL` in `server/src/main.rs`, 1 today. It is a subcommand and not a key in the report, so it is known before any record is sent and `server/src/detect.rs` is untouched.
    - `PROTOCOL` in `sdk/src/tensorquill/server_detect.py` is the same number, and a test reads both files and fails when they differ.
    - *Decided here:* only the same number is taken. A larger one is refused as a smaller one is, because the SDK was not written for that binary's reports.
    - *Decided here:* the number sits beside the checks `build_evidence` already makes on a report (every server kind listed, the thresholds applied, `kind_checks`, the sample count), and none of those is removed. The number is checked first and costs one start of the binary per process (the answer is kept by path, modification time and size); the others still catch a binary that reports the number and does not do what it promises.
    - *Decided here, on a mismatch or a binary that reports no number:* `--detectors rust` and `detectors="rust"` stop with the two numbers and `pip install --upgrade tensorquill tensorquill-server`; `auto` uses the Python detectors with its one warning, which carries that message; `--watch` stops before its first look. A binary that does not answer within 10 s is refused.
    - *When the number goes up:* whenever what `detect` reads, takes or prints changes in a way the other side would get wrong: a kind added or removed, a threshold or option added, a report key renamed or dropped. The update-size kind being built on another branch is such a change, so the merge raises both constants to 2.

"Beside the interpreter" is the scripts folder of the running Python (`sysconfig`), the folder `sys.executable` is in (not resolved, since a virtual environment's `python` is a link), and the user scripts folder. That is where the server wheel's note says an install puts the program. A name given or set that is not a file is said, not passed over. Not found: a `pip install --target`.
    - The fallback wheel installs no program. When it is the one installed, the message says to build from source, not to install the package again.
  - *Which detectors run (the author's rule: "`--watch` requires the Rust binary and stops with the install hint; `--detectors python` overrides that. One-shot reads use `auto` and fall back to Python with one warning").*

    - `--watch` needs the binary, its protocol number and `pyarrow`; without them it exits with status 2 before the "watching" line. *Decided here:* `--watch --detectors auto` is the same as `--watch`, since by the rule only `python` overrides.
    - *Decided here:* `auto` is chosen once, on what is installed. After it has chosen Rust, a threshold the Rust rules refuse or a `detect` that fails is an error, as with `--detectors rust`, and not a quiet change to Python.
    - *Decided here, the library default:* `build_evidence` and `watch` keep `detectors="python"`. A library call then starts no process and gives the same evidence on every machine; the MCP server calls `build_evidence` and is held by a test to start no process; and a Rust default in the library is R5, which is not to be started. `detectors="auto"` is accepted by both and warns with one `RuntimeWarning`; a watcher decides once, before its first look.

A release that raises the protocol number must raise both packages and move the range, or a new server inside the range would send every read to the Python detectors.
