- [x] **`update_spike`, the first new kind, and the `replica_drift` note (K1 and S3; decided and built Nov 17 to 18, 2026 in a side session, branch `claude/lane-c-update-size-18d2d4`).** A jump in the size of the optimizer's update, read each step from Adam's own state with no copy of the weights. The kind count is now 33: 26 per-rank kinds in Python and Rust, 3 from the server's status, 4 Python-only; 12 invariants, 12 anomalies, 9 observations.
  - *Names.* The kind is `update_spike` (the working name is kept: it sits beside `grad_spike` and `activation_spike`). The capture option is `tensorquill.init(..., updates=True)`, the record field `update_rms`, the threshold option `update_window`, the `not_checked` entry `update_size`, and what the capture says once about an optimizer it could not read `update_not_read`.
  - *1. The rule.* The paper's step-to-step rule (Altenbernd et al., arXiv 2604.00726, eqs. 8 to 13, read here on pp. 6 to 8), with one departure. `R` is the largest RMS update over the parameters on a step. A step is flagged when `|R_t - R_(t-1)| > baseline / (0.05 * sqrt(rise))`, with `rise = max(0, G_t - G_(t-1))` on the total gradient norm before clipping, so a norm that did not rise flags nothing. The incident lists up to ten parameters by how far their own RMS update moved, largest first.
    - *The departure: the baseline is the mean of the last `update_window` changes that were not flagged (default 100), not a mean frozen after the warm-up.* The paper averages once over its learning-rate warm-up (1,000 steps), which a reader of the records does not know. I first built the frozen mean over the first N changes, as the prototype measured it, and then ran the prototype's toy with a 400-step warm-up as well as its 100-step one: 16 clean runs of 800 steps (two warm-ups, clipping at 1.0 and none, four seeds). With a frozen mean, one clean run (400-step warm-up, no clipping, seed 3) was flagged at step 404 for every baseline from 20 to 400 changes, at 1.80 to 1.08 times the limit. A second (100-step warm-up, no clipping, seed 0) reached 0.993 of the limit at step 201 with the prototype's own 100-step baseline, so the prototype's "no flag in 8 clean runs" had under 1% of headroom. With the mean of the last 100 unflagged changes no clean run was flagged, and the worst clean step was at 0.75 of the limit. Dividing the update by the learning rate before comparing was worse (clean steps at up to 3.1 times the limit).
    - *What it costs.* The weakest fault caught is nearer the limit: a whole-gradient fault of ×100 with clipping at 1.62 times the limit under the frozen mean and 1.13 under the rolling one (scratch simulation). And a window of 300 is not safer than 100 (worst clean step 0.95): a longer window lags the learning-rate ramp.
    - *Judged by the code as built, both paths, on the 34 cached runs of that toy:* 16 clean runs silent; 16 of 18 faults flagged at their step, both misses the ×10 fault with clipping (one whole-gradient, one one-parameter); 8 of 9 one-parameter faults name the seeded parameter first (the ninth is the uncaught ×10); Python and the server gave identical incidents on all 34.
    - *Defined the same in both languages.* The first `update_window` comparable changes fill the baseline and are not judged. After that every change that is not flagged joins it, whether or not the gradient norm rose, and a flagged one never does. The mean is an exact sum (`math.fsum`, and the server's port of it). A record is compared with the last one before it that could be judged, in the same attempt; the first record of a new attempt is compared with nothing. Left out, and never the record before: a step the loss scaler skipped, a record without `update_rms` or without a finite total, and sizes that are not finite. `records_needed` is `update_window + 2`.
    - *A non-finite total is not flagged here.* The paper also flags a non-finite pre-clipping norm; `non_finite` reports that, and this rule leaves the record out.
    - *A property to know.* The change is taken either way (the paper says "absolute change"), so the fall back after a jump is also a large change. With a rising norm it is flagged; with a falling one it joins the baseline and dulls the rule for the next window. Both are tested.
  - *2. The gradient norm.* The rule reads `clip_grad_norm` for the whole run once any record has it (the norm before clipping, which the capture records when it sees `torch.nn.utils.clip_grad_norm_` or `record_grad_norm`), and `grad_norm` otherwise: the same choice, and the same restart in the server when clipping first appears, as `grad_spike`. Router-only capture records no gradient norm unless the run clips. So `updates=True` turns the per-parameter gradient norms on for every step on which clipping was not seen, as a weight check does for its steps. A record that carries `update_rms` and no total is not judged, and a run with no such total at all reads `not_configured` (the input is "update_rms with grad_norm or clip_grad_norm"). The paper's eq. 11, without the gradient norm, is not used as a fallback: nothing here tested it.
    - *A blind spot.* A run that clips through a function TensorQuill does not see has a `grad_norm` read after clipping, which stays flat, so nothing is flagged and `kind_checks` cannot tell.
  - *3. Opt-in, and the cost on CPU.* Off by default. Measured on an M1 Pro laptop while three other sessions were building (load average 43 to 50), by timing the read alone, median of 400 and of 100 calls, three repeats: 0.46 to 0.48 ms for a model of 28 parameters holding 21,760 values, against a training step of 2.8 to 3.2 ms (15% to 17%); 3.3 to 5.2 ms (fastest 2.5 ms) for 28 parameters holding 3.2 million values, against a step of 41 to 47 ms (8% to 11%). The gradient norms it turns on cost 0.07 and 0.48 ms. A paired end-to-end timing on the same machine was too noisy to quote (the uncaptured step itself moved between 43 and 51 ms). This is a CPU figure only: nothing is measured on a GPU, and the paper's "roughly 1%" is the paper's, on one GPU, for its own implementation.
    - *How it reads.* One parameter at a time, so the only temporary is one tensor of that parameter's size; float32 for bf16 and fp16 moments. When the step count is on the CPU the bias corrections are plain numbers; on the device (fused, capturable) they are tensor arithmetic, so no value is synced. The whole read is wrapped: a failure is said once (`update_not_read`), that optimizer is not read again, and nothing is raised into the training step.
  - *4. Optimizers.* Adam and AdamW only, and a subclass of either that defines neither `step` nor `_init_group`. Read in `/Users/ak/Documents/tensrview/pytorch` (commit `7c84e46a525`, `torch/optim/adam.py`): the single-tensor, foreach and fused paths all apply `lr / (1 - beta1^t) * exp_avg / (sqrt(exp_avg_sq) / sqrt(1 - beta2^t) + eps)`, amsgrad reads `max_exp_avg_sq` (lines 499 to 547, 730 to 800, and `aten/src/ATen/native/cuda/fused_adam_utils.cuh` 137 to 145), and under a loss scaler the fused step takes its count back when it skips (line 897). The capture includes the bias correction, which the paper's eq. 7 leaves out "for clarity", so the number is the step PyTorch applied with weight decay left out. Checked by test against the weights before and after the step, in the venv's torch 2.15.0, for Adam and AdamW on single-tensor, foreach, fused, amsgrad, a tensor learning rate and two param groups, with and without clipping: within the 2e-4 relative the test allows (the float32 weights limit what a difference of them can show; the worst seen in a scratch run was 6e-7). Also run by hand on MPS (3e-7) and with bf16 weights (2%, the applied step rounds).
    - *Anything else is said, never silent.* SGD, Adafactor, RMSprop, NAdam and a subclass that overrides `step` each give a `not_checked` entry `update_size` naming the optimizer and the reason, in Python and in the server, and `update_spike` reads `unavailable` (the run `degraded`). So does a loop with `auto_step=False` that never calls the new `session.record_updates(optimizer)`, and a loss-scaler run whose optimizer holds none of the model's parameters. With the hooks on and only a foreign optimizer stepping, no step is recorded at all, as before, and the existing warning says so.
    - *The five patterns, each a test.* Two optimizers on one model (each is read; the one that is not Adam is named; an optimizer that steps after the lead has ended the step lands in the next record); `step(closure)`; a step that raised (nothing read, the next steps are); a step the loss scaler skipped (no update on the unfused path; on the fused path the unchanged state is read, the record is marked `step_skipped` and the rule leaves it out); and an optimizer that holds none of the parameters. A parameter Adam left alone on a step (no gradient) is left out of that record.
  - *5. Class and trigger.* An anomaly. The trigger fires by class, so it does not fire for it by default; `--fire-anomalies` or `--fire-kind update_spike` adds it. Tested.
  - *What carries over from the paper, and what does not.*
    - Carries over: the quantity (the largest RMS of Adam's update over the parameter tensors), the step-to-step change, the square root of the gradient-norm rise in the limit, and α = 0.05.
    - Does not: "no false positive on its clean runs at α = 0.05" (its baseline is a mean over a known 1,000-step warm-up, on LLaMA models of 60M to 1.3B parameters; this baseline is rolling, and a frozen one flagged a clean toy run here); its detection rate (about a third on average at α = 0.05 in its Fig. 4, read off the plot here on p. 8: near 0.65 for bit positions 11 and 12, under 0.1 for 9 and 10, with faults in GEMM kernels on a GPU); its cost of roughly 1%; and its recomputation of a flagged step, which TensorQuill never does (nothing blocks, pauses or ends training). Its limit is in absolute units of the gradient norm, so a model whose norm is 100 times larger is judged ten times more sharply for the same relative rise: that is the paper's rule, kept, and not examined here.
  - *S3.* A checked `replica_drift` entry of `kind_checks` carries `note`: "equal replicas do not rule out silent data corruption: a corrupted gradient is reduced with the others and the same wrong result goes back to every rank, so the replicas stay equal (SCOUT, arXiv 2608.11034, pp. 1 and 3)". One table per language (`KIND_NOTES`), held equal by a test; only a checked entry has it, and the all-ranks view carries it. The words on p. 1 were re-read here in the PDF.
  - *Where it is.* `capture.py` (the option, `_read_updates`, `record_updates`, the ring, the record field); `flight.py` (the `update_rms` column, a map of name to float; a sender without it reads as `not_configured` on the server, and `update_not_read` travels in `extra_json`); `detect.rs` (the rule, the tables, the sixth threshold, the note); `evidence.py`; the MCP series (`update_rms` beside `grad_norm` and `weight_norm` for a parameter); `divergence.py` (the field is compared between runs as a per-parameter series); `examples/seeded_failures.py --case update_spike`. Not changed: compact evidence (it carries `kind_checks` whole) and the Rerun export (it lists no kinds and logs totals only).
  - *The sixth threshold.* `update_window` is an option like the other five: `build_evidence`, `read_server_report`, the all-ranks look, the server's `--thresholds`, and a `--update-window` flag. α stays a constant, listed in a report's `thresholds` as `update_alpha`.
  - *Verified.*

- [x] **512 and 1,024 simulated ranks into the server (measured Nov 17 to 18, 2026; synthetic, on CPU).** The fourth proof run of "Packaging and the release plan", part 3. The server takes 512 ranks at the default settings and stores everything, but only up to about 192 ranks inside the 250 ms budget (128 for a rank's first flush). At 1,024 ranks the default 128 MiB quota refuses about one rank in seven.
  - *Method.* `server/examples/live_streams.rs` with `--ranks N`: rank r replays source rank r modulo 64 under its own number, so the record shape is that of "Measured under 64 live streams" (Granite-shaped, 24 blocks, 32 experts, top-8, 50-step parts, the columns before Nov 16). One `DoPut` stream per rank, 65 ms a step, 2,000 steps, every rank's flush coinciding, release server with `--flush-steps 50`, a fresh local-disk store per run. One command per run: `examples/simulated_ranks.sh <label> <source store> <ranks> --step-ms 65`, which also samples the server's memory and CPU every second and its open files every 10 s. A Rust client and no training process; a rank whose stream fails does not connect again, where the Python sender would. Unlike Nov 15, the client prepares a batch in the second before it is due and not straight after the acknowledgement before. The acknowledgement figures below are of every flush after a rank's first, as on Nov 15; the first, which finds the rank's folder and watermark, is slower and is given beside them.
  - *Machine.* MacBookPro18,3, Apple M1 Pro, 8 cores (6 performance, 2 efficiency), 16 GB, macOS 27.2.1. Client and server share it and compete for the cores: the client took 6% to 8% of a core at 64 ranks, 39% to 45% at 512 and 72% to 82% at 1,024. Lanes A to C were building and testing on the same machine. Each run waited for the 1-minute load to fall under 5 (under 8 for the 500-step runs); the load after a run was 3 to 16, and 29 and 40 after the two runs marked below. The restarts did not wait: they followed their run at once, at a load of 14 to 19 for 1,024 ranks and 7 for 64. Swap held 6.5 to 11.9 GB throughout. So every figure here is taken beside other work, and none on a quiet machine. The full runs at 128, 192 and 256 ranks were the quietest, at a load of 3.5 to 6 before and after.
  - *64 ranks, the check.* Three full runs: sent to acknowledged 44 to 49 ms median, 71 to 81 ms p95, 82 to 92 ms p99, 105 to 107 ms worst, none over 250 ms; a rank's first flush 86 to 144 ms median and 119 to 185 ms worst; 11% to 12% of a core; 510 MiB stored. This matches Nov 15 (54, 100 and 132 ms; 12%). Server memory 287 to 366 MiB at the median of the second half, 318 to 377 MiB peak.
  - *512 ranks, default quota, two full runs.* Every rank finished, all 20,480 parts were stored (4,072 MiB) and every `incidents.json` reached step 1,999 with no incidents. Sent to acknowledged: 460 and 461 ms median, 1,012 and 1,127 ms p95, 1,218 and 3,018 ms p99, 1,399 and 7,076 ms worst; 83% and 79% of flushes over 250 ms. A rank's first flush: 985 and 918 ms median, 1,546 and 1,302 ms worst. The second run's tail was taken with the load reaching 29; one flush took longer than the 3.25 s between flushes, so that rank's next batch left 5.1 s late. Server CPU 99% to 103% of a core. Memory 991 and 1,140 MiB at the median of the second half (1.9 to 2.2 MiB a rank), 1,493 and 1,613 MiB peak (2.9 to 3.2 MiB a rank). Open files 525: one per rank and 13.
  - *1,024 ranks, default quota, two full runs.* 157 and 154 ranks stopped early; 139 and 154 of them were refused with `ResourceExhausted: ingest buffer full`. For the ranks that went on: 744 and 827 ms median, 1,519 and 1,634 ms p95, 1,822 and 1,966 ms p99, 3,122 and 3,212 ms worst; 93% and 96% over 250 ms. A rank's first flush: 1.9 and 2.4 s median, 4.7 and 5.9 s worst. Server CPU 186% to 192% of a core, memory 1,675 to 1,847 MiB at the median and 2,745 to 2,780 MiB peak.
  - *1,024 ranks with `--quota-mib 2048`, two full runs.* Not the default. One run finished clean: all 40,960 parts (8,144 MiB) stored, 925 ms median, 1,569 ms p95, 1,908 ms p99, 3,321 ms worst, 95% over 250 ms, 203% of a core, 1,902 MiB at the median (1.9 MiB a rank), 2,843 MiB peak (2.8 MiB a rank), 1,050 open files; a rank's first flush 1.4 s median and 2.1 s worst. The load reached 40 during it, and it is the only complete 1,024-rank run. In the other run 150 ranks lost their stream (see the unexplained failure below).
  - *Where the budget is first missed.* One full run each at 128, 192 and 256 ranks, default quota, nothing refused or dropped:
    - 128 ranks: 79 ms median, 136 ms p95, 154 ms p99, 325 ms worst, 8 of 4,992 flushes over 250 ms; first flush 141 ms median, 202 ms worst, none over; 23% of a core; 563 MiB at the median, 611 MiB peak.
    - 192 ranks: 127 ms median, 199 ms p95, 213 ms p99, 236 ms worst, none of 7,488 over; first flush 225 ms median, 284 ms worst, 42 of 192 over; 34% of a core; 712 MiB at the median, 744 MiB peak.
    - 256 ranks: 155 ms median, 273 ms p95, 297 ms p99, 335 ms worst, 1,197 of 9,984 over (12%); first flush 232 ms median, 378 ms worst, 105 of 256 over; 44% of a core; 913 MiB at the median, 1,021 MiB peak.
    - So the budget holds to about 192 ranks for the flushes after the first, and to about 128 for the first. In 500-step runs 41% were over at 384 ranks and 68% at 512.
  - *Where the quota first refuses.* Nothing was refused in the full runs to 512 ranks with detectors, or in a 1,000-step run at 640. Refused: 22 of 768 and 59 of 896 in 1,000-step runs, and 16 of 512 in the one full run with `--no-detect`. So the refusals start at about 512 to 768 ranks, not at the 90 the Nov 15 entry estimated; why the estimate was low was not looked into.
  - *Falling behind.* To 512 ranks the server kept pace: every part was stored and reported, and no rank waited on it except the one flush of the second 512 run. At 1,024 ranks some flushes took longer than the 3.25 s between them (the worst first flush was 4.7 to 5.9 s), so those ranks sent their next batch 1.5 to 3 s late and the runs ended about 2 s past 130 s. The delay did not grow over the run.
  - *File descriptors were not a limit.* One per rank and 13 while streaming; 1,270 to 1,500 during a 1,024-rank restart. The shell's limit was 1,048,576 and was not raised.
  - *The quota bounds buffered Arrow bytes, not the process.* With the quota at 128 MiB the server's resident memory was 1.5 to 1.6 GiB at 512 ranks and 2.7 GiB at 1,024. macOS's peak memory footprint, which counts compressed pages, was within 8% of the peak resident size in every full run (2,948 to 3,163 MiB at 1,024 ranks). Memory per rank falls with the count: 4.5 to 5.7 MiB at 64, 4.4 at 128, 3.7 at 192, 3.6 at 256, about 2 at 512 and 1,024, at the median of the second half.
  - *Detector time.* The server reports none. From one `--no-detect` run each: 81 s of CPU against 131 to 136 s at 512 ranks, and 159 s against 244 to 253 s at 1,024, so about 2.5 ms of CPU per rank per flush for the detectors and incidents files, against about 1.6 ms at 64 ranks on Nov 15. A difference of single runs with different refusals, so a rough figure.
  - *Restart.* A new server on the store of a 1,024-rank run, then 3 batches a rank from step 2,000, sent flat out, four times: all 3,072 batches acknowledged in 29 to 36 s; a rank's first batch 2.3 to 2.5 s median and 7.6 to 9.2 s worst; the batches behind the rebuild 3.8 to 6.0 s median and 26 to 33 s worst; server CPU 411% to 479% of a core. The same on 64 ranks with 2,000 steps: 1.4 to 1.8 s in all, 1.3 to 1.7 s worst. Nov 15 measured 6.1 to 7.0 s for 64 ranks with 10,000 steps. Both 1,024-rank stores were short of 2,000 steps on about 150 ranks, and these restarts ran at a load of 14 to 19.
  - *Unexplained failure.* Streams ended with `Unknown: h2 protocol error: error reading a body from connection` and no line in the server's log: 2 of 64 ranks before their first acknowledgement in one run, 18 of 1,024 in one default-quota run, and 150 of 1,024 partway through one run with the quota raised. It was absent from the other 19 runs. It may be the same fault as the unexplained broken pipe of Nov 15. Whether the client or the server ends the stream is not known.
  - *Fixes to consider, none made (the server's source was not changed).*
    - Say in `--help` and the server README that `--quota-mib` must grow with the rank count, or size the default from the streams open: about 1.3 MiB per coinciding 50-step part of this shape.
    - Make a refused batch wait for room for a bounded time before `ResourceExhausted`, since every rank flushes at the same step.
    - Log a line when a stream ends in an error, so the h2 failure can be attributed.
    - Have the server report detector time per flush.
    - Bound how many ranks rebuild at once after a restart.
  - *For the benchmarks file, when the README's measurements move there:* the table of acknowledgement time, CPU and memory at 64, 512 and 1,024 ranks; the budget met to about 192 ranks (128 for the first flush); the quota setting needed above about 512 ranks; the restart times.

The default flush of 500 steps or 10 seconds. The time from acknowledgement to `incidents.json` above 64 ranks (2.8 to 2.9 ms median, 34 to 54 ms worst at 64). Linux.
  - *Clean-up.* Every server store and the source store were deleted from the scratchpad. The raw output and commands are in the hand-over note `lane-e-simulated-ranks.md`.

- [x] **Rows S7 and S8: the wording written (Nov 18, 2026).** Docs only: `sdk/README.md` and `sdk/AGENT_INSTRUCTIONS.md`, no code.
  - *S7, written.* The README has a bullet beside the `moe` record, "When router counts are the routing": a router whose output has no integer tensor is counted as the plain top-k of the hooked scores, which is the routing only when the model picks the plain top-k of those scores. A routing-only balancing bias (DeepSeek-V3, Motif 3, Mamoda2.5), noise added after the hooked module, or a capacity limit after it makes the counts wrong, and a healthy model can get `moe_imbalance` with dead experts. For a bias, pass an `indices=` function that applies it; for noise or a capacity limit, hook the module whose output holds the chosen experts, because an `indices=` function sees only the hooked module's output. The agent instructions have a new section, "Before you call it a fault", whose first part tells the agent to ask how the model picks experts before reading the MoE imbalance or starved-experts kind as a fault, and that the evidence does not say how a router was read (no record field says it; checked in `capture.py`).
  - *S7, run.* The review's F1 appendix script, unchanged, at `7dd0379` on CPU: the model routed 1,703 / 1,406 / 1,003 / 1,008 tokens, TensorQuill counted 5,120 / 0 / 0 / 0 and raised `moe_imbalance` at step 0 with experts 2 to 4 dead. The second half, not run before in this repository: the same script with the router declared as `Router(4, top_k=1, indices=lambda out: (out + model.blocks[0].balance_bias).argmax(-1))` counted 1,703 / 1,406 / 1,003 / 1,008, equal to the real routing. One `moe_imbalance` still came, at step 5, with no dead expert: one 64-token step put a single expert over the overload line, which is the toy's small step. The function reads the block's own bias buffer, so it follows the bias as it changes. A toy, one layer, a fixed bias; no real model was tested, and noise and a capacity limit were not tried.

The README carries the ones about what TensorQuill sees: a fault that goes away under full capture is not gone (in "Things to know"); the stall stacks do not include worker processes (after "Where a silent rank is stuck"); the reporting rank is often a victim and what a stuck collective's record points to, as one company's experience, with TensorQuill's `collective_hang` label set beside the post's "desync" (after the Flight Recorder paragraph); full sharding alone gives the drift check nothing to compare, and a fault every rank shares, such as a reduction in low precision, is not seen by it (beside "Tensor parallelism"); a shared expert is not seen by the router hook (beside the `moe` record). The agent instructions carry all eighteen items in the new section, grouped as router load, gradients and activations, hardware or software, and stalls and hangs. Kept: "one team", "one model", the fine-to-NaN line as Elsen et al.'s opinion, the Flight Recorder post as Meta's fleet, the BF16 attention fault as one non-standard 450M model that a plain 102M transformer did not reproduce and whose backward error every fused BF16 backend tried shared, and the within-sequence point on the dead-expert rule as a reading of the rule, not a run. No rule for who caused a hang was added, and no paper's threshold is given as TensorQuill's.

The new section therefore names the MoE kinds and the collective hang in words, as the file already names the replica-drift and update-size kinds; `grad_spike` is named once more, on a line that says it is an anomaly.
  - *Tests.* The SDK suite, with this tree on `PYTHONPATH`: 1,012 passed, 619 skipped, none failed; `test_mcp_server.py` and `test_packaging.py`, which read the two files, pass (64 passed, 1 skipped).
  - *Not done.* No real MoE model was run with a balancing bias, noise or a capacity limit. `sdk/RUN_ON_YOUR_JOB.md` says a router that returns only logits needs only `top_k`, with no word on a bias; it was outside this row's files and is left as it is.
- [x] **The router hook reads `indices=` with grad off and refuses an output with nothing to read (S1 and S2; built Nov 18, 2026 in a side session, branch `claude/lane-2-router-hook-s1-s2-d599ac`; merged the same day).** Closes two open items of "Three lanes merged".
  - *S1, what was wrong.* An `indices=` function that did not detach the router's output (`lambda out: out.topk(2, dim=-1).indices`) built autograd nodes inside the forward hook, which saved tensors. Under non-reentrant activation checkpointing the hook does not run in the recomputation, so the two saved different numbers of tensors and `backward()` raised `CheckpointError` in eager mode: reproduced in a test first (7 to 9 tensors saved in the forward, 6 in the recomputation). Whether it also raised under `torch.compile` was not separated: the compiled test failed before the fix, but its eager reference run raises first.
  - *S1, the fix.* The hook calls the function under `torch.no_grad()`, on a detached view of the output (`capture._detached`: the tensor, or a plain tuple, list or dict of tensors, detached; a named tuple or a model's own output class is passed as it is). Grad off is what carries the guarantee: a function that also reads a parameter (`(out @ proj.weight).topk(...)`) would still save tensors with the detach alone, and it is in the tests. The function no longer has to detach; the `Router` docstring says so. No README or agent-instruction line said it had to, so neither is touched.
  - *S1, what is checked.* Three functions that do not detach (plain `topk`, `softmax` then `topk`, one that reads a parameter), each with no checkpointing, non-reentrant and reentrant: training runs, every token is counted once, and the loss and every gradient of three steps, and the final parameters, are `torch.equal` to an unhooked run of the same model, whose router logits feed the loss inside the checkpointed block. Under `torch.compile` (`eager` and `aot_eager`, both checkpointing modes) the counts equal eager's. The existing compiled checkpointing test (`test_review_fixes.py`) still finds no graph break.
  - *S2.* A default router whose output holds no integer or floating-point tensor at its top level used to count nothing and write eight zeros every record, which read as a router that routed no token. It is now refused by the router guard's own path (`refuse` in `_router_hook`), as every router the counting cannot read is: said once as a warning, no entry in `moe`, `moe_top_k` or `moe_stats`, the reason under `moe_refused`, a `not_checked` entry in the evidence, and the MoE kinds `unavailable`. The reason reads "its output, a NoneType, holds no integer or floating-point tensor at its top level" (or the class's name, or "its output is a bool tensor, not expert indices or logits"), followed by the usual fix: `indices=`, or name the inner module. Tested for `None`, an object, a tensor nested one level down and a bool tensor, eager and compiled. The case "no tensor: nothing counted, nothing refused" in `test_what_was_counted_before_is_counted_as_before` pinned the old behaviour and is removed. No change in `detect.rs`, the golden files or the protocol number: the server prints `moe_refused` as written.
  - *Run, macOS, PyTorch 2.15 only.* SDK suite without the server binary: 1,031 passed, 619 skipped, none failed. `experiments/`: 92 passed. `git status` shows no change under the golden files or `experiments` after the runs. Nothing version-specific is used (`torch.no_grad`, `Tensor.detach`), so PyTorch 2.6 is expected to behave the same; not run on 2.6.
  - *Open.* A router output nested more than one level deep is refused, not searched: `_first_tensor` reads the top level only, as before.
- [x] **K4: four head numbers in the probe (Nov 18, 2026, lane 4, branch `claude/lane-4-head-probe-k4-af52c6`, merged the same day).** Each probe now records `head`: the output logits' norm, signed mean, log Z, and the gap between BF16 and FP32 logits on the same hidden states, plus `gap_relative`. The evidence gains `head_trends`. These are numbers only: no incident kind, no threshold and no `not_checked` entry. K5 judges the gap once real runs give it a threshold.
  - *Source of the gap.* The review gives no formula for the gap, only "the gap between BF16 and FP32 logits on the same hidden states" (D2). The formula comes from the paper itself. Chen et al. (arXiv 2610.38277, p. 7, read from the PDF in `researchpapers/` this time) define logit quantization error as RMS(z_bf16 − z_fp32). z_fp32 is the head recomputed in FP32 from the same hidden states as the native forward, with the head's GEMM in FP32. `gap` is exactly that: absolute, in logit units, over every position and vocabulary entry. Their appendix (p. 24, Fig. 18) also plots RMS(z_bf16 − z_fp32)/std(z_fp32). That ratio is recorded as `gap_relative`. The paper does not say over which axes its std is taken; here it is the RMS over positions of each position's standard deviation across the vocabulary. The paper's third measure, KL(p_fp32 ∥ p_bf16), is not recorded.
  - *The other three.* All three are computed on the FP32 logits z (positions t, vocabulary v). `norm` is the mean over t of ‖z_t‖₂. `mean` is the signed mean over every t and v. `log_z` is the mean over t of logsumexp_v z_t. The paper pages read do not define the axes of its "output logit norm" or "output logit mean", so these choices are mine, not Chen's. Per-position quantities match its notation z = Eh ∈ R^V. The mean is signed because the drift has gone both ways (J6). Every position the head sees counts, padding included, since the probe has no mask for the head. Positions whose FP32 logits hold a NaN or Inf are left out and counted (`nonfinite_positions`). The record also carries `positions`, `vocab`, `calls`, `input_dtype` and `weight_dtype`. Non-finite positions and a JSONL round trip (with `gap_relative` None for a head with no spread) are tested.
  - *How the BF16 and FP32 logits are made.* A permanent pre-hook on the head reads its input only while a probe runs, so compiled code never recompiles; the no-recompile test now includes the head. The hook then applies the head twice with `F.linear` on cast copies, with autocast turned off around both passes. A first version left autocast on, and a probe run under `torch.autocast("cpu", dtype=torch.bfloat16)` read a gap of exactly 0.0, because autocast cast the FP32 pass to BF16 too. A test now covers it. `torch.amp.is_autocast_available` is in `torch/amp/__init__.py` at `v2.6.0`. One pass uses input, weight and bias in float32. The other casts all three to bfloat16, which is what autocast does to a linear layer. It never calls the module again and never uses autocast, so the numbers do not depend on how the user's probe ran. They are the head module's output, before any soft-cap or temperature the model applies after it. On CPU, `bf16_dot_with_fp32_arith` accumulates in FP32 and rounds the output to BF16. Read in `aten/src/ATen/native/cpu/BlasKernel.cpp` at `v2.6.0` and at HEAD; `KERNEL_CPU(linear, lower_precision_fp)` is in `autocast_mode.cpp` at both. The GPU kernels were not read. A head already in BF16 upcasts exactly, so for such a run the BF16 pass is the run's own computation. The vocabulary is taken in chunks of at most 2^26 logits. Each chunk is reduced in float32 and the chunks are added in float64. The spread is summed about each position's first-chunk mean, so a common shift of 30,000 does not cancel it (tested). The hook runs at the head's call, not after the forward, because under FSDP the weight is gathered then. A weight that is still a `DTensor` there is not read; that case is untested. A failure in the hook loses the head's numbers, not the probe.
  - *How the head is found.* Only two sources are used, and nothing is matched by name or position. The first is a module the user names (`probe_head="lm_head"`; a missing name, or a module that is not a plain `nn.Linear`, is an error at `init`, as an unknown router is). The second is the module a Hugging Face model reports through `get_output_embeddings()`. A plain `nn.Linear` here means the class's forward is `nn.Linear.forward`; a subclass with its own forward is refused, as for Adam in K1. Otherwise the record says `{"not_read": reason}`. The reasons: the model names no output head, `get_output_embeddings()` returned None, it raised, it returned a module that is not a plain Linear, or the head was not called in the probe's forward. A module called `head` in the test toy is not read unless named. `probe_head=False` leaves the key out. No Parquet, protocol or `server/src/` change was needed: `probe` travels whole in `probe_json`, and the server's probe reader skips keys it does not know (`detect.rs`, `ProbeVisitor`).
  - *Measured on a CPU toy (test `test_the_gap_grows_with_a_scaled_head_and_stays_small_on_a_healthy_toy`).* The toy has vocabulary 32 and width 16, and the batch is 4 × 16. Healthy: norm 2.298, mean +0.0327, log Z 3.581, gap 0.0012, gap_relative 0.0029. With the head's weight and bias × 1000: gap 1.19 (about 1,000 times), gap_relative 0.0028 (unchanged, as pure scaling should leave it). With a common shift of +1000 on the bias, as a drifting mean: mean +1000, gap 0.424, gap_relative 1.02. Near 1,000, BF16 values are 4 apart, so the rounding is as large as the logits' spread, which is the collapse Chen describes on p. 24. These are toy values, and nothing is claimed about real models.
  - *Nothing else changes.* A test trains two seeded toys with AdamW and dropout, one plain and one probed every step with the head read. The training outputs, the gradients at the probe, the CPU generator's state after each step and the final weights are bitwise equal.
  - *Cost, timed on CPU* (Apple M1 Pro, PyTorch 2.15.0 in the project venv, 6 threads, load average about 6 from other lanes; median of `session._probe()` with `probe_head=False` against the head read, settings interleaved; final code). On the test toy (vocabulary 32): 1.27 ms against 1.58 ms, +0.31 ms. On an LM-shaped toy (vocabulary 32,000, width 256, 4 blocks, batch 4 × 128): 36.8 ms against 122.9 ms, +86 ms (+234%), or 0.086 ms a step at `probe_every=1000`. A run before the autocast fix gave 39.5 ms against 134.4 ms. Most of it is the BF16 GEMM: 55 ms alone on this CPU, against 9 ms for the same GEMM in FP32. Read, not measured: PyTorch's CPU BF16 GEMM is its own loop (`bf16_dot_with_fp32_arith`), not the BLAS that the FP32 one calls. A first version summed each element in float64 and cost +151 ms; it now reduces each chunk in float32 first. A GPU's BF16 GEMM is native, so these CPU figures say nothing about GPU cost.
  - *Suite.* The whole SDK suite passes: 1,640 passed and none skipped, on the committed code, with `TENSORQUILL_SERVER_BIN` pointing at the server built from this branch. An earlier run without the binary, before the last fixes, gave 1,018 passed and 619 skipped, which are the tests that need the binary.
  - *Not run.* No GPU, no real model, no Hugging Face model (`get_output_embeddings` is tested on a toy that defines it), no FSDP or tensor-parallel head, no MPS. No PyTorch 2.6 run: the venv has 2.15.0, and the APIs used were checked at `v2.6.0` in the reference repo. No measurement of peak memory. By arithmetic, each chunk holds up to 2^26 logits in FP32 twice, about 0.5 GB, plus temporaries, so about 1 GB is held briefly. `head_trends`, like `weight_trends`, is absent from evidence built from a server report. Follow-up, not fixed here: `_attention_stats` multiplies the query and key inside the user's forward, so under autocast its scores are probably BF16 too. No threshold, kind or golden file was touched. The README gained one bullet. Docstrings were updated in `probe.py`, in `init`'s `probe_head`, and on `head_logit_trends`.
- [x] **S4: end marker and stacks grouped (decided and built Nov 18, 2026, lane 3, on its branch; merged the same day).** Row S4, from review proposal 6 with D3, G2 and E4. No change to `server/src`, the Parquet schema, the protocol number or the kind tables.
  - *How a run was closed before this, and what the marker changes for each reader.* `Session.close` flushes, stops the writer thread and closes the sink; with `endpoint=` the sink (`StreamingSink`) stops the heartbeat, closes the file, then lets the sender send what is left and end its `DoPut` stream (`done_writing`). The server's watchdog calls a rank `finished` when its stream ended cleanly (`Stream::Finished` in `watchdog.rs`) and the run `finished` when every rank did; a local `.jsonl` file had nothing, so a finished, a hung and a killed job looked the same in it (README). Now, once the file is closed and complete, `close` writes `<name>.end.json` beside `<name>.jsonl` (temporary file and rename; never raises; none when the writer thread or the flush failed). For each reader: `build_evidence` of a local file (and the CLI, `watch` and MCP's `list_incidents` on one) carries `run_end` and the summary a "Run end:" line; `watch` on a local file rewrites when the marker appears (the file itself does not change then); `list_runs` has an `end` cell (`closed`, `closed, raised`, `not closed`; a run of several ranks is closed only when every rank is), also in MCP's `list_runs`; the server's watchdog, a store's run and the all-ranks view of a store are unchanged, since the server already knows how each stream ended. The flight sender never sees the marker, which is not a line of the records.
  - *What the marker is, read from MLflow and W&B first.* MLflow's `end_run` writes `FINISHED` and an end time, its context manager writes `FAILED` when the block raised, and a killed process leaves the run `RUNNING`; W&B's `finish` sends an exit record with an exit code, 1 when the `with` block raised. So the marker holds `outcome` (`closed`, or `raised` with the exception's type when `with session:` ended by an exception, or the Lightning callback closed the session from `on_exception`; the Hugging Face callback closes only at `on_train_end`, so a Trainer that raised leaves no marker), `closed_at`, `attempt`, the run ID and rank, and the file's size at close (`records_bytes`). Unlike both, no `atexit` hook writes it: a script that never calls `close()` is not known to have closed. A marker beside a file is a sidecar, not a line in the file, because the stream sender and every reader read each line as a step. Absent (`not_closed`): still running, killed, never closed, or written before markers existed, which the file cannot tell apart; the wording never says crashed. A session that opens the file removes the marker first (a run started again under its run ID appends to the same file), and a reader calls a marker whose size the file has outgrown `written_after_close`, so a resumed and then killed run is never read as closed.
  - *What "the same stack" means.* The main thread only (other threads are cut to four frames and differ by timing). Every frame the heartbeat kept, at most 40, innermost first: a depth cut would merge two ranks in the same collective reached from different places in the job, which is the mismatch worth seeing, and the outer frames all ranks share never split a group; a stack deeper than 40 is compared on what was kept. Each frame on its function, its file as the path after the last `site-packages/` or `dist-packages/` or else the base name (other hosts have other prefixes and checkouts, and the heartbeat keeps a path's last 200 characters), and its line only for the job's and TensorQuill's own code: two lines of the job's code are two calls, while a library's line numbers differ between releases and the hosts of one job may not all have the same patch release. The cost, listed as a limit: two ranks blocked at two lines of one library function, reached the same way, are one group (more likely under Lightning or the Hugging Face Trainer, whose loops are library code). Measured on `thread_stacks` itself, 12 samples each: a main thread in a `queue.get(timeout=...)` loop, in `Event.wait()`, in an `Event.wait(timeout)` loop and in a `time.sleep` loop kept one line in every sample, so waits do not move between lines; that is not the reason lines are dropped.
  - *When a group is named.* Only stopped ranks (silent, lost, unresponsive) that sent a stack are grouped. The odd group is named only when there are exactly two groups and the smaller is one rank or at most a quarter of the ranks with a stack, so 2 against 1 is named and 1 against 1, 2 against 2, 1 against 1 against 1, two lone ranks at two other places, and 3 of 8 are not. One rank, no stacks, all alike, or no clear odd group are each said plainly, with no rank named. The ranks that sent no stack are always listed (an unresponsive rank never sends one, and may be the rank that matters). The block, `stopped_stacks` in the all-ranks view (store and local rules both) and in MCP's `get_all_ranks_status`, carries its three caveats: an observation, never the cause (Mycroft takes the odd rank as the stuck one; ByteRobust treats ranks outside the largest group as suspects; CCL-D finds a slow rank's stacks like everyone's); the main process only, so a rank stuck in a data-loader worker shows the main process waiting (G2); and each rank's last heartbeat stack, not a simultaneous snapshot, and one snapshot cannot name a slow rank (D3). It is not added to the `job_stalled` or `rank_silent` incident, because the server writes those incidents too and the end-to-end test holds the two equal. Each stopped rank's place (not its timings) is now part of the watcher's status stamp, so a stack that arrives or moves after the rank went silent is written, by the all-ranks watcher and by the single-rank watcher, which shares the stamp; a rank spinning in the job's own code at a new line every beat is therefore rewritten every beat.
  - *Tests.* `sdk/tests/test_stall_evidence.py`, 21, and one case in `test_lightning.py` (a fit that raised leaves `raised`): a closed run read by every reader, a session closed by an exception, a run killed by SIGKILL in a child process, a closed run started again under its run ID and killed, a marker outgrown or not one, two ranks with one closed, the watcher rewriting on the marker alone; three ranks with one odd stack, all ranks alike, ranks with no stack, the edges of "clearly odd" (seven cases), what the same stack means, the all-ranks view and its MCP tool, the stamp, and the all-ranks watcher writing when a stack arrives and not when only its timing moves (taking the stack out of the stamp fails it). Existing tests changed only where they listed exact files or keys, or held a local file's evidence equal to a store's (`test_run_manifest.py`, `test_compact.py`, `test_watch_rust.py`, `test_evidence.py`, `test_flight_e2e.py`, and the stall summary in `test_watch_all_ranks.py`, which gains its "Stacks:" line). SDK suite on macOS with Python 3.12 and a server binary built from this tree: 1,652 passed, 0 skipped, 0 failed (16 min). No Rust was touched.
  - *Not done.* No GPU, no real stuck collective, no data-loader worker's stack (reading child processes' stacks is new code); no repeated snapshots as ByteRobust takes for slow jobs; the per-rank evidence and the single-rank summary still give only `stuck_at`. A local run's all-ranks view through `combine_rank_evidence` has no status and so no stacks. The marker is not sent to the server.

Two PyTorch 2.15 jobs failed on one test that no lane touched, `test_ranks_that_issued_different_collectives_are_a_desync`. PyTorch's Flight Recorder loader lists the dumps in the file system's order (`os.walk`), and its analyzer takes the first rank it reads as the reference, so where two ranks issued different collectives the rank it calls the culprit, and the collective it names, followed the runner's disk. Reproduced here by listing the dumps in reverse. Fixed at the author's word: `analyze_flight_recorder` puts the dumps in rank order before the analyzer reads them, and a new test runs the same dumps listed both ways (it fails without the fix). This makes the answer the same on every machine, not more right: with two ranks that disagree nothing says which is at fault, and the summary still quotes PyTorch's "Culprit rank N". Whether to soften that wording for two ranks is the author's.
